Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template  
• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annex es must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the  usual 
service standard time.  
• The rec ommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• Under  the GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill o ut information on disclosure in section G.4 .    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity  Short Name]- [Country/Region] -[YYYY/MM/DD ]”

 A.21. Executive summary  
1. Background 
The Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP Phase 1)4 is an existing climate finance facility for 
small to medium -scale grid- connected and distributed renewable energy projects in sub- Saharan Africa. 
Operational since 2016, REPP Phase 1 is grant -funded through the United Kingdom (UK) Foreign, 
Commonwealth & Development Office (FCDO). An initial capitalisation of £48m was followed by a further 
commitment of £40m from the FCDO due to the success of the programme. Camco Management Limited 
(Camco) is investment manager for REPP Phase 1.  
2. Introducing REPP 2  
By launching a new Programme entitled REPP 2 , Camco is seeking to build on the successful track record 
of REPP Phase 1 (described further in Annex 28) and scale up support for small-scale renewable energy 
power generation in key African markets, focusing on Least Developed Countries (LDCs).  
REPP 2  is a blended finance facility  with a goal to create a paradigm shift for the small -scale renewable 
energy sector in S ub-Saharan Africa that will scale up and replicate funding for low -emission and climate -
resilient power generation, with increased access for local and vulnerable populations to modern renewable energy solutions.  
REPP 2  targets  funding and support to smaller -scale renewable energy projects and businesses in the LDCs  
of sub- Saharan Africa. To be eligible for receipt of GCF Proceeds, investments must be located in a 
jurisdiction which has provided a Letter of No Objection in a form acceptable to the GCF (Host Countries). 
Nine priority countries, seven of which are LDCs, have been selected for GCF funding, including:  Cameroon, 
Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) , Lesotho,  Madagascar , Malawi , Niger , Nigeria, Sierra Leone and 
Zambia .5  
3. Climate change problem  
Many countries in sub-Saharan Africa are highly vulnerable to climate change.6 Drivers of this vulnerability 
include:  
• Existing climate hazards (droughts, floods, extreme temperatures, etc.);  
• High dependence on rain -fed subsistence agriculture for a largely rural population across the region:  
• High levels of poverty and food insecurity; and  
• Widespread disregard for human rights (including limited access to water, sanitation, education), 
which often leads to high levels of national and regional conflict.  
As a result, the majority of the region’s population (particularly those living in rural areas) have very limited 
capacity to adapt to climate change . One outcome of this is that fewer than half of the population of the 
region (estimated at 570 million people) has access to electricity. In rural areas, this proportion falls to 25%.7  
4. Additional barriers  
Smaller -scale and off -grid renewable energy companies and investors face numerous  barriers, including:

 • A lack of early -stage or development funding  to ensure projects or companies become technically 
and commercially viable;  
• Limited access to finance for growth and construction of smaller -scale renewables . In addition to a 
general lack of funding for smaller -scale renewables, commercial8 funding remains scarce at both 
the funding platform and individual asset levels ; 
• A limited appetite for investment in new markets , with generally higher levels of real and 
perceived risk ; 
• Challenging economics for smaller projects and companies , due to limited generation and 
revenue potential, which mean that costs are typically large relative to potential returns for investors . 
This situation has been exacerbated in the aftermath of the Covid pandemic due to delays, supply 
chain disruptions and cost escalations ; 
• The burden and cost of funder requirements , which add to delays and project costs;  
• Limited  or untested  domestic policy and regulatory frameworks , which further limit the 
bankability of smaller renewable energy projects and the appetite from private sector investors ; 
• The potential for negative climate change impact s on national  grids including the potential for 
reduced power generation from exi sting assets such as hydropower plants and the potential for 
increased grid instability as more variable forms of renewable energy generation are introduced ; 
• Grid utility political and bankability risk  which further limit public and private investor appetite; 
• Limited experience and funding for local developers which can result in delays, higher costs and 
greater potential for project failure; 
• Under -representation of women  in the sector;  and 
• Environmental and social barriers , with the level of environmental and social safeguards and 
management systems in many markets in sub- Saharan Africa regularly failing to meet international 
best-practice standards, leading to environmental hazards which often disproportionately affect 
women and other vulnerable groups . 
5. Climate benefits  
REPP 2  will deliver climate impact by:  
• Financing renewable energy power generation, which contributes to reduced GHG emissions from 
renewable energy installations (Outcome 1)  aligned with Host Country  priorities;  
• Delivering improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure (Outcome 2)  through 
diversification and decentralisation of renewable energy generation;  
• Delivering improved climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities (Outcome 3)  
through access to clean energy, which improves livelihoods and builds gender equity;  
• Achieving increased private sector investment (Outcome 4)  for renewable energy generation, 
particular at the level of the underlying assets funded by REPP 2, which benefits local developers , 
builds gender equity  and establishes a more commercially  viable market for smaller -scale 
renewables ; and  
• Contributing to stronger  technology deployment, innovation and market transformation 
(Outcome 5)  for smaller-scale and off -grid renewables in all Host Countries . 
All of the targeted climate impacts of REPP 2  are closely -aligned to the national priorities of Host Countries 
for mitigation and adaptation, including those defined in their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) 
and National Adaptation Plans (NAPs).  
 
 
8 In this Funding Proposal, a distinction is drawn between ‘private’ and ‘commercial’ sources of capital. Private sector capital 
is sourced from private investors on a commercial basis. Commercial funding may be sourced from public entities including 
Development Finance Institutions, where such entities source or raise  capital on their own balance sheets on a commercial 
basis including directly from private capital markets and therefore provide this funding on a commercial basis. The 
objective of leveraging both private and commercial capital, whether provided at either the fund level or the level of the 
underlying assets, is aligned to the GCF’s objectives  on mobilization of capital.

 6. REPP 2  financing and support  
REPP 2  will further catalyse the growth of sub-Saharan Africa’s small- scale renewable energy sector by 
scaling up from the success of REPP Phase 1, further leveraging private sector capital flows into small -scale 
and distributed renewable energy markets across the region, with a focus on LDCs. The programme is 
expected to deliver 330MW of new installed capacity, more than 12.7 million tCO 2e (mtCO 2e) of GHG 
emission reductions and  provide new or improved access to clean energy to more than 7. 7 million people  
and improve the resilience of nearl y one million beneficiaries  over its 20 -year total lifespan.  
REPP 2  financing and support mechanisms include:  
• Early -stage and development funding,  which includes early -stage and growth funding for off -grid 
companies  (including solar home system and mini -grid companies) to demonstrate that selected 
technologies are fit -for-purpose,  to acquire first customers and to secure inventory and to 
demonstrate  that innovative new business models  are viable and sustainable. Additional 
development -stage  funding will be provided for grid- connected projects;  
• Gap funding for construction and implementation  of grid -connected projects  to ensure 
generation and installed capacity targets in Host Countries  are achieved. REPP 2  will also facilitate 
access to and financial risk mitigation to ensure bankability of projects ; and  
• Technology deployment, innovation and market transformation, designed to build skills and 
capacity amongst local businesses and developers, improve the ability of businesses and developers 
to deliver gender equity, and improve their ability to manage environmental and social governance 
(“ESG”) risk and impact.

9 Trisos, C.H., I.O. Adelekan, E. Totin, A. Ayanlade, J. Efitre, A. Gemeda, K. Kalaba, C. Lennard, C. Masao, Y. Mgaya, G.

B.1. Climate con text 
1. Climate change problem statement  
Climate change hazards 
People and communities across sub-Saharan Africa are exposed to a wide and expanding range of climate 
change hazards.9 A summary of key climate hazards for each REPP 2 Host Country  is provided below and 
discussed in greater detail in Annex 2: 
• Cameroon : Hazards include drought, heavy rainfall events, flooding, heavy winds, high 
temperatures, and heatwaves. Energy infrastructure is vulnerable to precipitation, extreme rainfall 
and flooding, as well as drought, and sea- level rise; 
• DRC: Hazards include intense rainfall, erosion, floods, heatwaves, and seasonal droughts. Reliance 
on the use of traditional biomass significantly increases the climate vulnerability of the population;  
• Lesotho : Hazards include more frequent, severe droughts and increased flooding. The energy 
infrastructure, particularly hydropower, is highly vulnerable to increasing temperatures. Reliance on 
subsistence agriculture significantly increases the climate vulnerability of the rural population;  
• Madagascar : Hazards include intensification of cyclones and associated flooding, sea- level rise, 
intensification of droughts in the south and west, and a more intense wet season elsewhere. The 
energy sector is vulnerable to a reduction in precipitation and cyclones destroying infrastructure. 
Over -reliance on the use of firewood further increases the level of vulnerability;  
• Malawi : Hazards include floods, droughts, strong winds, intense rainfall, riverine and flash floods and 
tropical cyclones. Vulnerabilities include poverty, rapid population growth, dependence on the 
agricultural sector reliance on rain- fed agriculture and biomass as well as lack of financing and weak 
institutional capacity to manage climate change;  
• Niger : Hazards include higher temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme events, 
increased frequency of erratic rainfall and extreme droughts. Vulnerabilities include lack of financing, 
rapid population growth, and low institutional capacity. The energy sector in Niger is expected to be 
impacted by the effects of heavy rainfall resulting in damage to energy infrastructure;  
• Nigeria : Hazards include extreme heatwaves and rainfall events leading to droughts, floods, erosion, 
as well as rise in sea -level leading to intrusion and inundation of land. The energy system is 
vulnerable to increased demand for cooling and heating and already experiencing multiple supply 
shortages;  
• Sierra Leone: Hazards include floods, droughts, coastal erosion, decreasing river flow, storm surges, flash floods, sea-
level rise, and high winds. Vulnerabilities include water resources and a high 
dependence on commodity price; and  
• Zambia:  Main hazards includes floods and wildfires, with droughts also increasingly common. The 
frequency and intensity of extreme climate events are expected to rise, posing significant risks to Zambia’s economy, its people’s livelihoods (nearly 60 % of Zambia's  
people earn less than the 
international poverty line, and three quarters of the poor live in rural areas), and the sustainability of 
its natural resources base.

17 https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/SP.RUR.TOTL.ZS?locations=ZG

Development Bank, http://www.iadb.org/intal/intalcdi/PE/2012/12152.pdf  Climate change vulnerabilities  
The sub- Saharan Africa region as a whole is generally highly vulnerable to climate change .6 There are 
several mutually reinforcing drivers of this vulnerability, including:  
• Exposure to pre -existing climate hazards,  as outlined above;  
• High levels of poverty and food insecurity : More than two thirds (417 million people in 2017) of 
the global population living in extreme poverty live in sub-Saharan Africa.10 Levels of inequality 
across the region are also pronounced and have widened this century.11 In terms of food insecurity 
across Africa, 2 82 million people ( 20% of the population) faced hunger  in 2019.12 The recent COVID -
19 pandemic and war in Ukraine have exacerbated food insecurity across the region;13,14  
• High dependence on rainfed subsistence agriculture for a largely rural population : More than 
95% of agricultural production in sub-Saharan Africa is rainfed.15 More than 70% of the region’s total 
population is dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods, the majority of whom are smallholder 
farmers who rely on subsistence production.16 The World Bank estimates that 58% of the region’s 
population lived in rural areas as of 2021;17 
• Energy poverty  is endemic across all of the H ost Countries, with fewer than half of the population 
of sub- Saharan Africa having access to electricity. Of the 760 million people globally,  who do not 
have access to electricity, 5 68 million (7 7%) are located in the region and only 25% of people living 
in rural areas have access;7  
• Vulnerability of energy infrastructure to climate hazards and climate change impacts:  
Countries which depend on hydropower generation and are exposed to high rainfall seasonality and 
the occurrence of droughts  are vulnerable to climate change.  Projected decreases in annual rainfall 
and increases in the frequency and intensity of severe droughts exacerbate t he vulnerability  of 
energy infrastructure . Increased temperature decreased water availability and extreme  weather  
events are anticipated to lead to reduction in plant efficiencies . Given that many national grids are 
small and poorly -maintain, power generation as well as transmission and distribution infrastructure 
are often vulnerable to climate extremes ;18 
• Inability to cater to basic human rights  (including limited access to water, sanitation, education);6 
and 
• High levels of gender -based violence, as well as national and regional conflict , which may be 
exacerbated through the impacts of climate change and competition for resources.6  
As a result, the majority of the region’s population, particularly those living in rural areas, have very limited 
capacity to adapt to climate change .

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 8  
 
  
19 GCF, 2022: Steps to enhance the climate rationale of GCF -supported activities  2. Climate rationale  for mitigation 
Despite being amongst  the lowest contributors to global GHG emissions, sub-Saharan Africa has already 
experienced widespread loss and damage due to climate change, with impacts including biodiversity loss, 
water shortages, reduced food production, reduced economic growth and even loss of lives.6 Power 
generation across most parts of sub- Saharan Africa is still dominated by fossil fuels. In rural areas not 
connected to the grid, power generation is largely dependent on biomass and diesel generation.  
The potential of the REPP 2  Programme  to mitigate GHG emissions lies in:  
• Replacement of fossil fuel -intensive grid electricity with renewable energy power generation 
from grid -connected utility -scale projects and large mini -grid projects (isolated grids);  
• Replacement of fossil fuel -intensive off-grid electricity with renewable energy power 
generation ,  through  large mini -grid projects ( isolated grids );  and 
• Provision of new or improved access to clean energy for people and communities  that have 
historically had little or no electricity access, through off ‑grid solar home systems and solar 
photovoltaic mini -grid systems (which typically replace candles, kerosene and diesel power 
generation).  
The REPP 2 Programme will avoid and reduce greenhouse gas emissions (MRA 1) and promote a paradigm 
shift for climate mitigation in each of the Host Countries. The Programme will be aligned with the principles 
for demonstrating the climate rationale of mitigation activities, as recently clarified by the GCF.19 Specifically:  
• Emission reduction activities supported by REPP 2 will be strongly aligned with priorities defined in 
the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) of all H ost Countries;  
• At the time of investment, REPP 2 will determine and assess project impact boundaries, GHG 
emission baselines using standard emission factors that are aligned to national GHG reporting, 
projected emissions avoided or reduced, and additionality using internationally -accepted Clean 
Development Mechanism (CDM) methodologies  (as presented in A nnex 22.B ); 
• The same CDM methodologies will be used to establish monitoring protocols;  and 
• A robust and comprehensive measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) system for GHG 
emission reductions has been prepared in accordance with international best practise and GCF 
requirements.  
3. Climate rationale for adaptation  
The combination of hazards, exposure, vulnerability to current climate variability and existing and limited 
adaptive capacity due to existing socioeconomic circumstances described above leave people and 
communities across sub-Saharan Africa highly vulnerable to climate change. The mutually reinforcing 
vulnerabilities (induced by climate, socio- economic, cultural and political drivers) result in very limited 
adaptive capacity and make building climate resilience a priority. Climate adaptation initiatives and the need for significantly scaled- up financing for adaptation are thus a priority in all H ost Country NDCs and National 
Adaptation Plans.  
Without increasing adaptation capacity, climate change impacts are anticipated to deteriorate in all Host 
Countries , including decreased food security and further loss of livelihoods. Climate change hazards are 
also likely to disproportionately affect rural women, given their culturally -defined roles, and other vulnerable 
groups. The potential of REPP 2 to increase climate change resilience and adaptive capacity lies in:  
• The use of multiple energy sources and technologies to make energy systems more climate 
resilient against supply disruption. For example, hybridising  hydropower generation (which is often


---
*Page 13*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 9  
 
 seasonally variable and may be negatively impacted by reduced rainfall due to climate change) with 
solar or wind generation can provide a buffer against climate variability ; 
• Decentralising and diversifying energy supply with renewable energy systems and off -grid 
solutions,  which can provide increased energy security  through the ability to localise and buffer grid-
based supply disruptions ; 
• Increasing access to renewable energy in off -grid communities  to provide hous eholds and 
businesses with solutions that help to build resilience and respond to the impact s of climate change. 
Such s olutions include electricity provision for clinics to respond to increased illness, malnutri tion, 
and injury ; electricity provision to refugee camps  that are  accommodating di splaced people, ; 
electricity provision to educational  facilities to improve awareness and knowledge about climate 
change; improved access to climate information through cellular networks , radios, and internet  
enabling people and communities to benefit from access to climate early -warning systems for 
disaster risk reduction and seasonal climate information for planning and decision- making for 
management of crops and livestock ; providing power for increased cooling need; irrigation  to 
increase agricultural productive output ; e-mobility  to provide inc reased access to markets  as well as 
providing improved  cooking solutions  to households ;   
• Supporting local private developers  and helping them to become financial sustainable, thereby 
making the local renewable energy sector (as well as local economies more broadly) more resilient 
to macroeconomic shocks ; and  
• Supporting technological innovations that build the capacity of households and communities to 
anticipate and respond to climate impacts, including innovations for the delivery of climate services 
(e.g., access to mobile phones and internet improves access to meteorological and 
agrometeorological reports and early warning systems), market information, and other information 
on climate change, its impacts and the solutions; as well as technological innovations  for productive 
use applications.  
Please see Tables 1 and  2 below which present REPP 2 eligible adaptation responses. Detailed country  
level data is provided in Annex  2. 
The REPP 2 Programme responds directly to the urgent and immediate needs of vulnerable countries, as 
outlined in their NDCs. The Programme focuses on nine Host Countries in sub-Saharan Africa, of which 
seven are LDCs. The potential of REPP 2 to assist these countries to adapt to the impacts of climate change 
in the context of promoting sustainable development and a paradigm shift will be confirmed through the 
following:  
• Identification: People, communities and energy infrastructure in all H ost Countries and across the 
sub-Saharan African region are highly vulnerable to climate change  (see below eligibility criteria) ; 
• Response: The Programme will target adaptation impacts through increasing the resilience of the 
most vulnerable people and communities (ARA1), in addition to energy infrastructure and the built 
environment (ARA3) ;  
• Alignment : REPP 2 will place a strong focus on aligning its investments with national -level climate, 
energy and sustainable development priorities. The proposed climate adaptation impacts of the 
Programme (improved resilience of energy infrastructure and local populations) are closely aligned 
with and strongly support national plans and climate strategies of the H ost Countries. This is ensured 
through  document analysis and extensive engagement with key governmental  and non -
governmental  stakeholders, and confirmed through securing  Non-Objection Letters  from all H ost 
Countries ; and 
• Monitoring and evaluation: A comprehensive and detailed monitoring and evaluation framework 
has been developed for the Programme as part of the Environmental and Social Management 
Framework (ESMF) to demonstrate its adaptation impact over time, as is expected to be approved 
by the  general partner of Camco REPP SCSp.

Eligibility criteria for programmatic adaptation projects  
The REPP 2 Programme is cross -cutting, targeting a 20% overall contribution to climate change adaptation  
and 80% contribution to mitigation. Interventions may be classified as  contributing to climate change 
adaptation where they can be demonstrated to be  a response to specific impacts and are  expected to deliver 
improved  adaptive capacity or climate resilience. The contribution towards the 20% target for adaptation will 
be based only on those funded interventions which are classified as an adaptation response and may be 
less than the total funding provided to that investment. In all cases , classification of adaptation responses  
will be undertaken through application of Camco’s Adaptation Decision and Project Tool  (ADAPT) , and 
guidance on the eligibility of the investment as an adaptation response will be considered by the REPP 2 
Investment Committee. The ADAPT tool will be implemented according to the following high- level decision 
process:  
• STEP 1: Identify climate hazards and climate change impacts  
• STEP 2: Define adaptation response  
• STEP 3: Identify adaptation metrics and monitoring, reporting and evaluation framework  
The ADAPT tool is  integrated within the existing analytical framework which drives eligibility, selection and 
approval of candidate investments  for categorisation as a climate change adaptation response . The ADAPT 
tool will provide analytical guidelines, including clear exclusion criteria and minimum criteria for acceptability. 
Assessments will be qualitative, rather than via a score, requiring the analyst to provide a rationale to support an investment’ s eligibility as an adaptation response as well as the  nature and extent of that contribution. 
Metrics, targets and monitoring and evaluation arrangements must be considered as part of the evaluation.  
Approval for the classification of an investment as cross -cutting, along with the climate change adaptation 
targets, will be provided by the REPP 2 Investment Committee.  
Further details of the process for classifying and investment opportunity as an adaptation response are 
provided in Annex 24.  Examples of the application of the ADAPT tool, based on an ex -post assess ment of 
three case studies from the REPP Phase 1 portfolio are shown in Annex 27.  
Most African countries (as illustrated in Annex 2) have low electrification rates, inefficient grid infrastructure  
and generally  rely heavily on hydropower generation ( with additional  dependence on diesel generation for 
sites not connected to the grid) . As a baseline for the REPP 2  Programme , eligible adaptation responses for 
improving the climate resilience of existing generation infrastructure are presented in Table 1 (after Johnston 
et al., 201220 and World Meteorological Organisation, 201721). Similarly, eligible adaptation responses with 
improv ed climate resilience for people and communities are shown in Table 2. Further detail is provided in 
the Market Analysis (Annex 2) for each Host  Country.

4. Business as usual scenario  
Given the paucity of funding (particularly in LDCs) for smaller -scale and off -grid renewables, and the catalytic 
role that GCF funding will play in mobilising funding at both the portfolio and project level, there is high 
confidence that the projected level of GHG emission reductions will not be achieved without GCF funding.  
5. Related projects and interventions  
Despite the successes of earlier programmes such as REPP Phase 1, key and persistent gaps in the funding 
landscape for smaller -scale renewables remain. This finding is reinforced after a detailed analysis of the 
potential for overlap and complementarity be tween REPP 2 and other financing programmes, including 
programmes funded by the GCF. Key gaps that REPP 2 will seek to address in nascent markets across sub-Saharan Africa, including LDCs, include:  
• An almost total lack of equity funding available across the spectrum for companies that need 
development capital for projects;  
• A very limited supply of growth equity for off -grid companies; and  
• Very limited investment in smaller -scale renewable energy projects in least developed markets, 
where real and perceived risks remain high.  
From a country perspective, investment is lacking in the places that need it most as exemplified by the 
distinct lack of funding in LDCs, even for more mature technologies, such as utility -scale solar PV. Similarly, 
there is also a significant and persiste nt funding gap for companies and projects that use innovative


---
*Page 17*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 13 
 
 technologies, focus on decentralised projects, which can improve climate resilience of energy infrastructure, 
or where some level of business model risk exists, even in middle- and upper middle- income countries.  
Of the 32 projects approved by the GCF that focus on energy generation and access in Africa, there is 
relatively little direct overlap with REPP 2.13 Where overlap does exist, there are extensive opportunities for 
complementarity, particularly because of the focus of REPP 2 on early -stage funding to fill gaps not 
addressed by other programmes.  The potential for overlap and complementarity of the REPP 2 Programme 
is analysed in further detail in Annex 23.  
An update on the status of c o-financing for REPP 2  is provided in Annex 25. A summary of the REPP 2 
pipeline is provided in Annex 26 . The rationale for selection of Host C ountr ies is provided in Section B.3.  
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram  
1. Goal statement  
REPP 2 will aim to create a paradigm shift by leveraging  additional private sector funding to promote 
investments in small- scale renewable energy projects in LDC s and other sub -Saharan African countries . As 
result of this , local populations of some of the world's most vulnerable communities will have improved 
access to clean energy, improved access to climate resilient solutions and information, and  the Host 
Countries will transition towards low -emission resilient development pathways, meeting their NDC targets . 
Accordingly , with increased investment , the GHG emissions from electricity generation will be reduced, 
climate resilience of the energy infrastructure is enhanced, and climate resilience of vulnerable people and 
communities will be improved.  
2. Paradigm shift potential  
If the proposed programme receives GCF funding and leverages further private sector and commercial 
finance, then local populations of some of the world's most vulnerable communities will have improved 
access to clean energy, improved access to climate resilient solutions and information, and Host Countries  
will transition towards low- emission development pathways, meeting their NDC targets. Because with the 
increased investment the GHG emissions from electricity generation will be reduced, climate resilience of energy infrastructure will be enhanced, and climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities will be 
improved.
 
The REPP 2 activities are anticipated to lead to more skilled and experienced local businesses and project 
developers, which in turn will develop with the help of REPP 2 funding more financially  sustainable, 
innovative, environmentally and socially sound climate resilient renewable energy projects . In this way, by  
building local developer capacities, as well as  de-risking and innovating financial solutions which enable 
renewable energy development, REPP  2 will create a higher volume of projects that will catalyse further 
private sector finance, contributing to stronger business environment and leading to more technology 
transfer .  
The resulting increases in renewable energy capacity will lead to cleaner production of energy, reduced  
GHG emissions, and greater  energy security  due to hybridisation of energy systems that are vulnerable to 
climate hazards, and by decentralising energy systems, transmission and distribution which is likely to be 
impacted by the increased risk of physical damage and distribution. REPP 2 investment is anticipated to  
lead to improved resilience to disasters through an improved ability to provide localised power solutions and to buffer supply disruptions through decentralisation.  
Furthermore, project developers are anticipated to provide electricity  not only for off-grid household basic 
electricity  use, but also  to critical services (e.g. clinics, water pumping stations), cooling services (e.g. 
refrigeration), information services (e.g. cell phone towers, radio) and productive  use of energy opportunities


---
*Page 18*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 14 
 
 which increase climate resilience (e.g. irrigation). These opportunities are expected to lead to additional 
income generation opportunities  and especially in off -grid project communities  works as a driver for long-
term economic growth . Off-grid renewable energy projects also often deliver first -time access to electricity 
for marginalised and vulnerable communities (including wom en-headed households) and businesses,  
placing them on a low -emission, climate -resilient, gender -sensitive and sustainable growth trajectory  
3. Activities and out puts delivered by REPP 2 
The activities and outputs  delivered by REPP 2 are designed to strengthen the market for small -scale 
renewables in sub- Saharan Africa and crowd in private and commercial capital  leading to GHG mitigation 
and improved resilience of  people and energy infrast ructure. This is achieved through an integrated suite of 
interventions, including:  
Early -stage development funding  
Activity 1: Provide early- stage growth funding for off -grid companies. REPP 2 will fund the early -stage 
growth and development of off -grid companies, notably solar home system businesses and mini -grid 
developers with a gender lens. This means funding of feasibility studies, ESIAs,  design and implementation 
of ESMS,  design of  gender action plans , as well as funding to acquire  first customers and secure inventory.  
Activity 2: Provide development -stage funding for grid- connected projects . REPP 2 will provide loans 
to developers of grid -connected projects to co- finance development -stage costs, such as feasibility studies , 
grid studies, ESIAs , design and implementation of ESMS , legal due diligence,  and gender  action plans.  
All funding activities will be considered from  a gender lens  perspective, with the view to target  that a minimum  
30 % of REPP 2  funding is invested in women owned and led companies, as well as companies that provide 
solutions to women as energy end users in line with the 2X GLI criteria.  
The two early -stage development funding activities delivered by REPP 2 will ensure increased technical and 
commercial viability of small- scale renewable energy projects ( Output 1), leading to an increase in 
renewable energy capacity and generation ( Output 2 ) as more projects reach financial close, are 
constructed and become operational due to REPP 2 financ ing. Furthermore,  the funding of environmental 
and social studies  (incl. gender)  and management plans will help the renewable energy project developers 
to understand and better identify risks and impacts and to establish comprehensive management plans to 
mitigate and minimise  such risks and to deliver environmentally and socially sound projects and ensure 
gender equity through increased awareness  and action (Output 3) . 
Activity 3: Finance expansion of SHS businesses. REPP 2 will provide equity or loans to fund scale up 
of solar home system businesses with a gender lens.  This growth -stage capital , in the form of loans or equity 
will help to ensure that SHS business achieve scale and long- term commercial viability ( Output 1) and will 
increas e the renewable energy capacity and generation ( Output 2).  All funding activities will be considered 
from a gender lens, with the view to target  that 30% of REPP 2 funding is provided to  women owned and led 
companies, as well as companies that provide solutions to women as energy end users in line with the 2X 
GLI criteria. This will help to promote women’s  empowerment and female ownership in REPP investments 
leading to gender equity ( Output 3).   
Activity 4: Finance construction of mini -grid portfolios. REPP 2 will provide financing for construction 
of mini -grids. The mini -grids funded can be in both peri -urban and rural areas as well as for isolated grid 
projects, which are typically large installations (servicing entire towns and tens or hundreds of t housands of 
beneficiaries) which are isolated from the national grid. This construction finance from REPP 2, which can 
be provided in the form of loans  or equity, will demonstrate that more small -scale renewable energy projects 
can achieve t echnical and co mmercial viability  (Output 1) and ensure increased renewable energy capacity 
when more mini -grids and isolated grids are constructed and become operational ( Output 2 ). REPP 2 will


---
*Page 19*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 15 
 
 be aligned with 2X GLI criteria, which means that it will target  30 % of fun ding is provided to women owned 
and led companies , as well as companies that provide solutions to women as energy end users to deliver 
greater gender equity ( Output 3 ).  
Gap funding for construction and implementation of grid -connected projects  
Activity 5: Prov ide gap funding for construction of grid- connected projects . REPP 2 will provide gap 
financing for the construction of on -grid projects  with a gender lens. Construction loans from REPP  2 will 
demonstrate that more grid-connected small -scale renewable energy projects can achieve technical and 
commercial viability ( Output 1) and are constructed and become operational leading to increased renewable 
energy capacity ( Output 2). The modalities of providing such funding will include innovative and less 
traditional financing approaches to reduce cost and complexity for developers and sponsors in bringing their 
projects to financial close. This approach is necessary given the limited  capacity of many first -time and/or 
local p roject developers when combined with the small scale of REPP 2 projects. In practice this requires 
Camco to balance the “belts and braces” approach to risk in project finance structures with the barriers to financial close that already prevent the deployment of small -scale renewables. For example, REPP 2 will 
consider taking a more limited set of direct agreements, proceeding with a limited or even no government 
guarantee, accepting a scaled- down security package and/or considering a mor e balanced  covenant 
package.  However, it should be noted that Camco will nevertheless ensure that material risks are addressed 
in documentation in a manner that protects the interests of REPP 2 and its investors. REPP 2 will also 
provide other type of financing which ar e needed in the market, such as mezzanine finance, 
bridge/construction finance, and convertible notes . REPP 2’s attractiveness in the market  stems from its 
flexible approach and the ability to provide the right financing from the large tool box of ins truments.  As 
mentioned above , all funding activities will be considered from a  gender lens, which means that a portion of 
REPP 2 funding (a target of  30%) will be provided to women -
owned and led companies, as well as 
companies that provide solutions to women as energy end users in line with the 2X GLI criteria. This will help to promote women’s empowerment and female ownership in REPP investments leading to gender 
equity ( Output 3).   
Activity 6: Support the provision of  financial risk mitigation  to ensure appropriate and cost -effective risk 
mitigation . REPP 2 will innovate in terms of its approach to funding and mitigating risk in SHS businesses 
(Activity 3 ) mini- grid portfolios ( Activity 4) . This may include additional sources of local currency funding 
and designing new risk mitigation strategies , including minimum revenue guarantees  (in partnership with 
key risk mitigation providers in the region) , to de- risk these investments and attract more commercial and 
private sector funding into these sectors. In addition,  REPP 2 will be promote the provision of and access 
to connections  subsidies in the markets in which it invests, and will encourage its investees to secure such 
subsidies . For individual transactions REPP 2 will employ a comprehensive risk management framework  to 
ensure that cost -effective  financial, technical and political  risk mitigation solutions can be offered for projects.  
By de -risking investments in smaller -scale renewables, REPP 2 can ensure that more projects become 
commercially viable ( Output 1) , leading to increased renewable energy capacity  (Output 2).  
Technology deployment, innovation and market transformation 
Activity 7: Provide targeted support to developers. REPP 2 will provide targeted support and capacity 
building to developers and entrepreneurs  as part of the investment process . Tailored support and capacity 
building will be  provided for improving financial models, business plans and risk mitigation as well as projects ’ 
environmental and social management. This support  will improve  skills and expertise of project developers 
and off-grid companies  in the small -scale renewables sector across sub-all target markets , thus supporting 
market transformation  (Output 3 ).  
Activity 8: Improve environmental and social risk mitigation . REPP 2 will provide tailored support and 
capacity building to its investees on environmental and social issues and  require all investees to develop


---
*Page 20*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 16 
 
 and implement an appropriate environmental and social management system (ESMS). Implementation and  
maintenance of suitable environmental and social safeguards in accordance with industry best practice will 
improve  skills and capacity for local developers and off -grid companies and improve their ESG practices , 
making them ready to take on further investment and scale their operations in the future. (Output 3 ).  
Activity 9: Non-financial support to promote gender equity . In addition to the investments  which will be 
aligned with the 2X GLI criteria,  REPP 2 investee companies will be required to establish gender action plans 
which will lead to co -benefits in terms of more gender balanced job creation, more equitable workplaces , 
as well as more  gender balanced stakeholder and community engagement and land acquisition. Further 
detail on the gender -disaggregated impacts and indicators are provided in Section D.1 and Annex  8. 
Further details on all activities are provided in Section B.3 (item 2 , REPP 2 activities).  
4. Key barriers and risks for climate outcomes  
The following key barriers and risks which may prevent the realisation of REPP 2 outcomes have been 
identified:  
• Uncertainties and delays in implementing policy and regulatory frameworks  and/or limited 
experience amongst public sector stakeholders  with implementation of new or untested regulatory 
frameworks and project finance documentation can result in delays in project implementation, additional 
costs,  devolve  private sector involvement and slow down or prohibit  sector grow th.  
• Political uncertainty and change  which may have unexpected influence on projects ’ financial viability 
due to change in  IPP approval process, grid extension planning, feed in tariffs  and mini -grid tariff setting, 
land- use, currency risk and legal and documentation risk . This may hinder projects from progressing, 
reaching financial close or receiving anticipated returns.   
• Limited bankability for on- grid utilities  in Host Countries  in which the national grid utility is not 
regarded as creditworthy . This will increase real and perceived risk, which may  limit co -founder’s appetite 
in the smaller renewables sector  and hinder a project reaching financial close.  
• Limited access to finance at both the fund level and the level of underlying projects.  At the fund level, 
there is limited appetite from investors (public and private) for smaller -scale renewables . Commercial 
investors (both public and private) typically seek risk mitigation through the capital structure of a fund, 
requiring junior investors or guarantee provider s to provide first loss protection.  Asset -level f inancing is 
usually available at financial close for “bankable” projects or off -grid companies of sufficient scale. 
Smaller ticket sizes may fall below the appetite of public funders such as development finance institutions 
(DFIs), infrastructure funds as well as private and commercial investors , which means that such 
investments face the risk of not achieving financial close.  As a consequence, REPP 2 may struggle to 
attract co -financing, particularly private and commercial co -financing, and may also struggle to leverage 
third-party financing into individual assets that it funds . 
• Limited supply of projects with economically viable business which provide tailored energy services 
and innovative financing models for resilient productive uses of electricity  and last mile customers. 
Smaller project sizes limit generation and revenue potential. Costs of development and growth to scale 
are typically large relative to this potential, resulting in more marginal return expectations. Higher levels 
of macroeconomic risk have emerged, due to high inflation- low growth conditions in local economies 
which have followed the Covid-  pandemic, as well as higher foreign exchange and balance of payment 
risks as interest rates are raised to combat inflation and protect growth.  
• Weak/underdeveloped legal  frameworks and capacity to enforce best ESG practice and gender 
equality.  The level of environmental and social safeguards , their operationalisation and monitoring in 
many markets in sub-Saharan Africa regularly fail to meet international practice standards  on pollution 
prevention, labour rights and occupational health and safety, as well as gender balanced consultation 
and implementation. This may create an environment where investee is not motivated to uphold the 
required standar ds.


---
*Page 21*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 17 
 
 • Limited ability and appetite from investees to improve their ESG practice and gender balance. 
Investees may consider this out of their core focus,  time-consuming and costly without understanding 
the importance for risk mitigation and benefit  of creating sustainable projects and businesses. 
Furthermore , amongst local communities, women are often constrained by gender -defined cultural roles 
and the rights of women – notably with respect to land ownership – are frequently not respected , which 
is likely to incr ease the complexity of implementing gender balanced engagement.  
• Limited capacity of grids and grid operators  to manage variable renewable energy supply which may 
arise due to lack of grid connectivity between high renewable resource regions to high power demand 
centers , grid flexibility or deficient assessment of available resources. This may lead to increased project 
costs due as grid infrastructure needs  to be upgraded to enable project to feed in.   
The f ollowing external assumptions , which  cannot be controlled by REPP 2,  have been made for a 
successful implementation of REPP 2 mandate :   
1. Sufficient s upportive policy and regulatory frameworks exist or can be developed and there is political 
will to do so ; 
2. Public and private investors are willing to invest in jurisdictions where REPP 2 operates ; 
3. Jurisdictions in which REPP 2 operates are and remain politically sufficiently stable for renewable 
energy  development;  
4. For grid- connected projects, utilities are bankable and offtake risks can be managed through 
available risk mitigation instruments ; and 
5. Sufficient demand for and opportunities exist to support women -owned and led businesses.  
The theory of change for REPP 2 is summarised in the diagram below.

B.3. Project/programme description 
1. The REPP 2 Programme  
REPP 2 will finance and promote the growth of small to medium -sized renewable energy projects across 
sub-Saharan Africa. Through this process, REPP  2 will catalyse the growth of sub-Saharan Africa’s smaller -
scale renewable energy sector, mobilising additional public , private and commercial funding to combat 
climate change and provide build climate resilience for people, communities and energy infrastructure. 
Building on the success of REPP Phase 1, REPP 2 will focus on support in nascent renewable energy 
markets, including eight  LDCs. The programme is expected to deliver 330MW of new installed capacity, 
reduce or avoid more than 12.7m tCO 2e of GHG emission , provide new or improved energy access to more 
than 7.7 million people, improve the quality of life of 3.7 million people and improve the resilience of nearly 
one million beneficiaries .  
In addition to reducing GHG emissions through renewables, REPP 2 will deliver climate adaptation impacts 
and build climate resilience by providing energy access to vulnerable people and communities, as well as 
diversifying and decentralising renewable energy infrastructure across sub-Saharan Africa. All of REPP 2’s 
targeted climate impacts are closely aligned with the national priorities of Host Countries  for mitigation and 
adaptation as defined in their NDCs, NAPs and other policy and strategic frameworks.  
REPP 2 will fund both on -grid and off -grid renewable energy projects and companies, including isolated grid 
developments in which renewable energy generation is used as the source of power for entire towns and 
small cities. REPP 2 investments will have significant transformational potential, with opportunities to provide clean, often first -time affordable power to tens of thousands of beneficiaries through one installation alone. 
REPP 2 will build on the success and learning from REPP Phase 1 to achieve greater scale and mobilise  
additional public and private funding into the smaller renewables sector. The Programme will focus on LDCs, where the funding and capacity gaps are greatest.  
The REPP 2 strategy is to take a bottom -up approach, working directly with private sector developers and 
companies to help develop a viable renewable energy sector  and a vibrant financing market for smaller 
renewable energy projects. Furthermore, REPP 2 seeks to be:  
• Additional , in that it stimulates sectoral development that would otherwise lag or not occur; and  
• Transformational , in that it helps to establish or build a market ecosystem that allows the ongoing 
growth of small -scale and distributed renewables in the region.  
REPP 2 will deliver on this mandate through providing funding and additional forms of support to developers   
designed specifically to overcome the barriers identified in Section B.1.

 2. REPP 2 activities  
All REPP 2 activities fall within a single component, designed to provide funding and support for smaller -
scale renewable energy businesses and projects across sub-Saharan Africa.   The activities delivered by 
REPP 2 are designed to strengthen the market for small -scale renewables in sub- Saharan Africa and crowd 
in private capital leading to GHG mitigation and improved resilience of people and energy infrastructure.  
The activities outlined below describe the provision of: 
• Early-stage growth funding for off -grid companies and development funding for grid- connected projects;  
• Gap financing for growth of SHS businesses as well as construction of mini -grids, isolated grids and grid-
connected projects; and  
• Support for technology deployment, innovation and market transformation  
 
Early -stage development and growth funding  
A range of funding activities for REPP 2 is described below. For all equity investments, REPP 2 will typically 
take a minority shareholding in accordance with its Investment Policy.  REPP 2 will act as an anchor investor 
and seek to crowd in other public and private sector investors to meet total project capital requirements . For 
debt investments, REPP  2 will typically require a minimum of 30% contribution from the investee.  
Activity 1:  Provide early -stage growth funding for off -grid companies  
REPP 2 will provide growth capital to off -grid electrification companies, many of whom are small, locally -
owned businesses. Growth capital for off -grid companies will be provided in the form of hybrid equity 
instruments (highly subordinated debt or senior ranked equity, typically convertible notes or redeemable 
shares), as well as subordinated and senior loans.  
Identified needs for SHS businesses include smaller equity investments to demonstrate that selected 
technologies are fit -for-purpose and the necessity to establish innovative new business models, acquire first 
customers and secure inventory.  
Mini- grid developers require smaller equity investments to demonstrate that selected technologies are fit -
for-purpose, as well as the need to establish innovative new business models, acquire first customers and 
to meet project development expenses.  
This funding will help these businesses  to achieve scale and sustainability  and overcome key barriers, such 
as a lack of early -stage or development funding, limited access to finance and challenging economics for 
smaller projects.  
Activity 2:  Provide development -stage funding for grid- connected projects  
REPP 2 will provide development -stage funding in the form of hybrid equity instruments  that are typically 
either repaid at financial close or include an option for REPP 2 to convert the loan into funding for construction 
at financial close. This funding approach directly addresses the key barriers experienced by developers of smaller -scale projects which are the lack of early -stage or development funding, and 
limited access to 
finance to develop bankable projects.   
The simplified development loan terms offered by REPP 2 will also help to reduce barriers related to 
challenging economics for smaller projects  because the costs of development are typically large relative to 
the potential of smaller projects to generate revenues, resulting in more marginal return expectations for 
developers, sponsors and commercial lenders.  
The simplified terms and conditions for REPP 2 development loans will also help to overcome t he burden  
and cost  of funder requirements . In accordance with the risk appetite of REPP  2, the development loans it 
offers will be structured to assume equity -like risk during the development phase and will be priced to ensure


---
*Page 26*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 22 
 
 commensurate returns if the development is successful. This structure is generally faster to execute, allowing 
more rapid deployment of capital for development than standard equity investments.  
Development funding for grid- connected projects will be used as a contribution towards the costs of project 
development (including design and permitting, technical feasibility studies, environmental and social impact 
assessments, gender actions plans, etc.) and due diligence (including legal fees and other third -party costs) 
as the projects approach financial close.  
Activity 3: Finance expansion of SHS businesses  
REPP 2 with provide funding for the expansion of SHS businesses in the form of equity (ordinary and 
preference shares)  and debt . Where REPP 2 provides debt , this will be in the form of mezzanine and senior 
loans . This funding must be used to scale these businesses to become fully sustainable once their business 
model is proven.  Funding for SHS businesses will be provided in the form of hybrid equity  instruments, as 
well as subordinated  and senior loans.  
This activity address es barriers to growth for SHS businesses, including a lack of early -stage or development 
funding,  limited access to finance for growth, and a limited appetite for investment in new markets . 
Activity 4: Finance construction of mini -grid portfolios  
REPP 2 may fund construction of mini- grid portfolios  and isolated grid developments  by way of   hybrid equity 
instruments, as well as subordinated  and senior loans.. This  funding will be used for construction and to 
achieve scale and sustainability once the mini -grid business model is proven.  
For mini -grid developers, this activity will help to overcome barriers which include a lack of early -stage or 
development funding,  limited access to finance for growth , and a limited appetite for investment in new 
markets . Mini- grid developers face additional barriers related to limited or untested domestic policy and 
regulatory frameworks  where regulatory frameworks are new and regulated tariffs are not cost -reflective. 
Activities  1-4 all support Out put 1 (Increased Commercial Viability) of off- grid businesses and grid- connected 
projects. Output 1 is described in detail in item 3 below.  
Funding for the expansion of SHS businesses (Activity 3) and construction of mini -grid portfolios (Activity 4) 
also help to deliver improved quality of life for people and communities provided with first -time electricity 
access for productive use of electricity and critical services (Output 2), described in further detail in item 3 , 
below . 
Gap financing for construction and implementation of grid -connected projects  
Activity 5: Provide gap funding for construction of grid- connected projects  
REPP 2 will seek to crowd in private and public  sources of funding and provide additional (gap) funding to 
fill a confirmed gap in the capital structure and achieve financial close. REPP 2 may provide funding via 
hybrid equity instruments, subordinated or  senior loans.  Loans will have a targeted weighted average tenor 
for the portfolio of 10 years and may also fund on concessional terms based on the needs of each project.  
This funding will help to overcome key barriers, including limited access to finance, by providing access to 
funding for construction for smaller projects, where investment ticket sizes often fall below the appetite of development finance institutions (DFIs), infrastructure funds as well as private and 
commercial lenders. 
REPP 2 funding for construction will also help overcome challenging economics for smaller projects and 
companies , where the smaller project size limits generation capacity and thus revenue and return 
expectations. Simplified and streamline funding conditions will also hel p to overcome the burden  and cost of 
funder requirements .  
Activity 1 -5: To enhance gender equality within renewable energy sector REPP 2 will target 30% of all 
funding towards companies that meet 2X GLI criteria. These are companies that: 
• Are founded by a woman or women or are majority -owned by women; and / or


---
*Page 27*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 23 
 
 • Have at least 30% women in senior management; and / or  
• Provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women; and / or  
• Have a workforce that is comprised of at least 30% women, together with a progressive human resource 
policy.  
Activity 6: Support the provision of  financial risk mitigation  
In nascent markets, developers of grid- connected projects face additional barriers related to limited or 
untested domestic policy and regulatory frameworks  and need to secure power purchase agreements with 
grid utilities and operators with limited creditworthiness and experience of working with independent power produced and variance energy generation from renewable energy sources such as solar PV (examples of 
grid utility political and bankability risk ). 
REPP 2 will seek to ensure appropriate and cost -effective risk mitigation  for off -grid business, as well as 
developers and sponsors  of grid -connected projects . In doing so, REPP 2 seeks to  reduce generally higher 
levels of real and perceived risk , particularly when developing smaller renewable energy projects in nascent 
and LDC markets . Elements of a more innovative approach to mitigating real and perceived risk and 
attracting more commercial and private sector funding include:  
• Attracting additional local currency funding  for SHS businesses (Activity 3) , mini- grid portfolios 
(Activity 4)  and grid -connected projects (Activity 5) . To reduce currency risk for businesses and 
funders alike, REPP 2 will work with additional sources of commercial as well as public local currency funders (in country as well as regional), to co-finance the roll -out of mini -grid p ortfolios and expansion 
of SHS business . 
• Securing innovative risk mitigation strategies  for mini -grid portfolios , including minimum revenue 
guarantees . This new form of risk mitigation is being considered in the market but is not yet widely 
available. REPP 2 will work with key risk mitigation providers for  the region, to de- risk these 
investments and attract more commercial and private sector funding into this  sector.  REPP 2 will 
also promote the provision of and access to connections  subsidies . 
• Employ ing innovative and less traditional project finance approaches to reduce cost and complexity 
for developers and sponsors to bring their projects to financial close. This approach may be 
implement for investments in both off -grid and grid- connected opportunities. Examples (often already 
utilised in REPP Phase 1) include  growth capital for off -grid companies (Activity 1) in the form of 
hybrid equity instruments , asset finance loans with loan principal growing in line with the asset base 
of the company , development -stage funding via hybrid equity instruments, typically either repaid at 
financial close or including  an option for REPP 2 to convert the loan into funding for construction at 
financial close (Activity 2) and  use of hybrid equity instruments to fund investments in the SHS sector 
(Activity 3).  
The capital structure of REPP 2 has been designed to address endemic risk factors such as currency risk, 
which impacts investors and investees . The junior equity/first loss tranche is designed to absorb losses from 
any cause across the portfolio, including through defaults caused by currency devaluation transfer and 
inconvertibility risk. This is likely to be more efficient than mitigating risk at the  level of each transaction. 
REPP 2 will also investigate sources  of first or second loss portfolio guarantees  to boost loss protection for 
senior equity investors and noteholders. Such guarantees cover the risk of losses due to any form of non -
payment, including where the cause of the failure to pay may be related to currency devaluation. 
At the transaction level, financial risk mitigation may take the form of political risk insurance, credit 
enhancement or mitigation of foreign exchange risk to address currency mismatches between revenues 
derived in local currencies and funding obligations in hard currencies. REPP 2 will work closely with i ts 
network of risk mitigation providers to reduce risks for governments, developers and funders alike. As was the case for REPP Phase 1, where a first- of-a-kind innovative risk mitigation solution for off taker liquidity risk 
was adopted, REPP 2 will continue to work with its partners to develop and implement innovati ve risk


---
*Page 28*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 24 
 
 mitigation solutions. Existing risk mitigation providers used by REPP 1 , and likely to be used by REPP 2,  
including  the African Trade Insurance Corporate (ATI), GuarantCo and World Bank MIGA. Continuous  
exchange of pipeline information and collaboration with partners are ensured through well -established 
communication channels. In all cases, a decision to secure risk mitigation (e.g. payment liquidity or payment 
default cover under a Power Purchase Agreement ( PPA) , or other forms of political risk cover) will be made 
on a case- by-case basis, based on the assessed level of risk and availability of risk mitigation instruments. 
These decisions will be approved by the REPP I nvestment Committee  in every case.  
REPP will attempt to secure (as appropriate and as available) the following existing forms of financial risk 
mitigation, in line with its Investment Policy:  
• Political risk insurance  (PRI):  PRI will be sourced at a transaction level.  Typically, PRI  cover will include 
transfer and convertibility  risk; breach of contract; expropriation; and war and civil disturbance.  REPP 2 
will source PRI cover from existing REPP partners such as the Africa Trade Insurance Corporation 
(“ATI”) as well as other reputable providers such as the World Bank (through the Multilater al Investment 
Guarantee Agency, “MIGA”) and the African Development Bank (“AfDB”).  
Offtaker credit risk:  Various forms of credit enhancement are possible / available to address offtaker 
credit risk  for national utilities. These include a) payment liquidity cover (e.g. standby letters of credit, or 
fully-funded  escrow accounts); and  b) payment default cover (e.g. via sovereign guarantees, corporate 
guarantees or breach of contract cover provided through PRI ). REPP 2 may source payment liquidity 
cover from the ATI’s Regional Liquidity Support Facility (“RLSF”) or seek funding of liquidity facilities 
directly from offtakers and sponsors (funding for debt service reserve accounts). REPP 2 may source 
non-payment (default) cover from the ATI as well as GuarantCo and other risk mitigation providers . A 
further source of protection for offtake r default risk will be via government guarantees (in support of 
national utilities which are acting as the offtaker for independent power producer projects).  
• Currency  hedging: In addition to efforts to secure local currency funding wherever possible and w here 
project economics and counterparty creditworthiness allows, c urrency  risk may be mitigated through  
denominating loans  and equity investments in hard currency , which transfers FX risk from REPP 2 to 
borrowers, investees and offtaker s.  
REPP 2 may engage in direct currency  hedging at the transaction level (via foreign exchange forwards 
and cross -currency swaps) which wo uld reduce  currency  devaluation risk to REPP 2 and also to 
borrowers / investees but would pass the costs of that hedging on to those borrowers / investees . Relative 
to the reduced scale of the revenues generated by smaller -scale renewable energy projects, these 
hedging costs are often prohibitive, even when sourcing hedging solutions from specialist market 
providers such as TCX. Other (potentially cost -effective) opportunities exist for  FX hedging at the portfolio 
level, which would also reduce FX risk to REPP 2 and help achieve economies of scale in terms of costs 
which can passed on to borrowers / investees . 
Activit ies 5 and 6 support achievement of Output 2 (Increase in renewable energy capacity and g eneration) 
through the construction of grid- connected projects .  
Support for t echnology deployment, innovation and market transformation  
A portion of REPP 2 operating costs will be allocated to build skills and capacity amongst local businesses 
and developers (Output 3), improve the ability of businesses and developers to deliver gender equity, and improve their ability to manage ESG risk and impact.  This support will be offered  alongside hybrid equity 
instruments or as part of subordinated or senior loan commitments at financial close.  Activities supported 
include the following:  
Activity 7 : Provide targeted support to developers  
REPP 2 will work to overcome the limited experience and funding for local developers  by engaging with 
them at their own pace and building local developer capacities by  providing technical support to upskill 
project developers and off -grid companies . This activity , conducted as part of REPP 2 investment approach,


---
*Page 29*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 25 
 
 will build project finance and specifically project finance risk management skills for local developers , enabling 
those developers to mitigate key risks and thereby secure both public and private sector funding. It will also 
support off -grid companies in strengthening their business plans and fundraising processes. Investees will 
also be supported in strengthening their internal gender and inclusion policies and designing their Gender 
Action Plans.  
Given the significance of local currency funding in providing long- term sustainable financing solutions for 
local developers, REPP 2 will also work to upskill and crowd in local (usually local -currency) funders, thereby 
making an important contribution to market development . 
Activity 8 : Improve environmental and social risk mitigation  
REPP 2 will require that all investees develop and implement an environmental and social management 
system (ESMS) that is commensurate with the level of risk for that investment and is fit for purpose in 
accordance with the REPP 2’s environment and social policy, which will itself be aligned with GCF and 
international best practise standards. This will support knowledge transfer to developers in REPP  2 Host 
Countries.  
REPP 2 will fund and proactively support investees in the development of their ESMSs and will establish 
new – and expand on existing – guidelines and toolkits to build their capacity. 
Activity 9 : Non-financial support to promote gender equity  
REPP 2 will integrate a gender equity  perspective into the design, implementation, monitoring and 
assessment of all renewable energy activities financed by REPP 2. This involves:  
• Analysing gender equity  in the investee company, country of operation, and targeted sector;  
• Establishing an investee- specific GAP by identifying gender performance indicators and gender -
disaggregated targets against an established baseline; and 
• Integrating the items listed in the two bullet points above into a monitoring plan. 
REPP 2 support for t echnology deployment, innovation and market transformation  will build  skills and 
capacity amongst local businesses and developers (Output 3), as described fu rther in item 3 . 
3. REPP 2 outputs  
The above activities will lead to the following REPP 2 outputs  
Output 1: Increased commercial viability  
All REPP 2 activities, including early -stage growth and development funding, gap funding for construction 
and support for market development combine to demonstrate the potential for technical and commercial 
viability of smaller- scale renewable energy businesses and projects  including female owned and led  in Host 
Countries  and across the region. Over its 20- year lifespan, REPP 2 targets support for thirty -seven  (37) new 
investments.  All new investments and the full funding commitment will be disbursed within seven (7) years 
of the first close for REPP 2.  
Output 2: Increased renewable energy capacity and generation  
Early -stage and construction funding from REPP 2 ensures that new renewable energy capacity and 
generation is realised. Renewable energy capacity and generation financed by REPP 2 contributes directly 
to improved access to clean electricity for women and men  and host -country priorities as outlined in their 
policy frameworks as well as their Nationally -Determined Contributions and National Adaptation Plans.

22 Note: in instances where REPP 2  will fund run- of-river hydropower assets, careful resource and design assessments will

REPP 2 support for project developers  establish a more active and commercially -viable market for smaller -
scale renewables, which is better -able to attract both public and private finance in future. These impacts 
accrue to Host Countries  and more broadly throughout sub-Saharan Africa, with a demonstrat ion impact for 
other emerging markets  and with developers being better placed to expand to those markets . REPP 2 will 
directly contribute to improved gender equity through a focus on gender mainstreaming  within investee 
projects and businesses . 
4. REPP 2 outcomes  
The activities  and outputs  delivered by REPP 2 are designed to strengthen the market for small -scale 
renewables and crowd in private capital leading in sub -Saharan Africa leading to  GHG mitigation and 
improved and strengthen resilience energy infrastructure  and vulnerable people and communities, as 
follows:  
Outcome 1: Reduced GHG emissions from renewable energy installations  
Early-stage and development funding from REPP 2 will contribute to the achievement of the programme’s 
objectives to reduce GHG emissions and increase renewable energy generation capacity by helping off -grid 
companies to establish their business models and begin their growth towards scale and sustainability, and 
by helping grid- connected projects to reach f inancial close.  
REPP 2  funding for construction ensures that renewable energy businesses and projects are constructed 
and become operational, thereby reducing or avoiding GHG emissions for Host Countries . Targeted 
mitigation outcomes include 12.7 m tCO 2e of avoided or reduced GHG emissions and 3 30MW of new 
renewable energy capacity over the 25 -year total life time n of the technologies .  
Outcome 2: Improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure  
Funding for c onstruction from REPP 2  contributes to the climate resilience of energy infrastructure in 
numerous ways  (see Annex  2 for country  specific details) : 
• Funding to achieve scale and sustainability of off -grid renewable energy installations which  offer lower-
cost, climate -resilient solutions  and stable sources of clean energy generation for people , communities 
and business not connected to the grid;  
• Decentralisation and diversification of energy generation can lead to greater grid stability and climate 
resilience , especially in countries w here transmission and distribution infrastructure  is already  
constrained , and where an  increased  risk of physical damage and decreased generation and 
transmission capacity  is expected due to climate change.  
• Countries which depend on hydropower generation but  are also drought -prone with high rainfall 
seasonality  are vulnerable to current climate variability. Projected decreases in annual rainfall and/or 
increases in the occurrence and severity of droughts  increase  climate change vulnerabilit y. Hybrid  power 
solutions  (e.g., where solar PV is augmented with existing hydropower infrastructure) can directly 
contribute to improved grid stability and greater climate resilience ;22 
Outcome 3: Improved climate resilience of  vulnerable people and communities  
Funding for off -grid electrification projects will not only provide first -time and affordable clean energy access 
and improve quality of life of low-income people who typically live in rural communities and are most 
vulnerable to climate change, but it will also build climate resilience outcomes including:

Outcome 4: Increased private sector investment

USD1.02 b illion. On this basis, the target for additional finance mobilised through co- investment directly into 
off-grid businesses and grid- connected projects is USD786 million. The target for private and commercial  
finance mobilised for investment is USD393 million.23 
In addition to the above, REPP 2  intends to target  up to 30% of its funding in support of 2X Challenge Gender 
Lens Investing (2X GLI) opportunities. This represents a target of USD70 million.  
Outcome 5: Contributing to stronger technology deployment, innovation and market transformation  
REPP 2  will: 
• contribute to more and more rapid technology deployment by providing targeted support and finance to 
renewable energy companies during early and construction stages of the assets  and by  financ ing the 
deployment of smaller -scale and off -grid renewable energy technologies  in nascent markets, often 
financing first -of-a-kind transactions and business models ; and 
• contribute to market development and transformation through its financing and capacity building support 
to local developers , based on the  application of sound commercial investment principles  aimed at 
delivering commercially  viable and bankable projects. This support – directly and through demonstration 
effect to broader sector stakeholders – will stimulat e sectoral development that would otherwise lag or
