5. REPP 2  co-benefits  
The construction and operation of renewable energy power plants will create new jobs  during construction 
and operations  for 76,801 people (co-benefit 1) .  
REPP 2  will also enhance gender equity  (co-benefit 2) through:  
• Anticipated 23,040 jobs for women  
• 70 mUSD funding committed to 2X Challenge’s Gender Lens Investing criteria- aligned companies    
• 11 female led or managed companies funded b y REPP 2 ; 
• All REPP 2  investees will be required to establish gender action plans to increase gender equity 
awareness and performance within investee company and though their project implementation. The 
REPP 2  target is 37 developers with improved gender equity awareness and practices;  
• 1,1 million  women with improved quality of life.  
• REPP 2  funding and support will lead to improved quality of life  through community and critical services 
connected to electricity  (co-benefit 3) . Co-benefits include improved access to healthcare, lighting , 
refrigeration,  improved access to information and more productive use of energy ( PUE) opportunities . 
These co -benefits will allow further socioeconomic  development of H ost Countries and their off -grid 
communities . The target for this co- benefit is improved quality of life through access to improved lighting for 
3.7 million people, improved access to  information for  1.7 million people.  
REPP 2  will deliver improved ESG practices  (co-benefit 4) by providing  its investees with train ing and 
funding to identify  environmental and social risk  and impacts, and to establish mitigation and management 
systems in accordance with industry best practices , leading to improved  environmental and social 
performance . Examples include improved waste management practices, mitigating the potential for negative 
impact s on biodiversity, soil and water resources, improved labour practices  and Occupational Health and 
Safety (OHS ) standards .  developers . 
6. Impact recognition 
Individual projects or off -grid renewable energy companies are eligible for multiple forms of support and 
funding from REPP 2 . For example, an off -grid company may receive both growth funding ( Activity 1) and 
gap financing for construction of mini -grids (Activity 4). Similarly, a developer of a grid- connect project may 
receive funding during the development phase (Activity 2 ) as well as gap financing for construction (Activity 
5). Recognition of t argeted impacts and Key Performance Indicator metrics will not be duplicated in these 
situations. Any decision to provide follow -on financing will typically not alter those metrics , with progress 
towards achievement of those metrics measured and reported through the project life cycle.  
Where a decision by REPP 2  to provide follow -on financing result s in a verifiable change to the amount of 
co-financing leveraged (from either public , whether concessional or commercial,  or private sources) or a 
change to the planned installed capacity for a project (or mini -grid or SHS portfolio) , such changes may  
result in changes to impact and KPI  targets . 
7. Rationale for selection of interventions  
The host country feasibility studies presented in Annex 2 include a country -level needs assessment that 
highlights the need for funding for on-  and off -grid renewables. The demand for the types of interventions 
outlined above is verified in Annex 2 based on reported impacts from REPP Phase 1 interventions as well 
as the stated pipeline of projects for REPP 2 .

24 As also further detailed in Annex 14 (Term Sheet)  The design of REPP 2  is informed by and builds on the successes of REPP Phase 1 to deliver scale, mobilise

8. Eligibility criteria  for REPP 2  
Installed capacity:  REPP 2  will invest in renewable energy power generation projects with an installed 
capacity of up to 25MW (and 50MW for wind). Eligible  projects can be further categorized as follows:24 
• Grid-connected projects (independent power producers or IPPs);  
• Large, isolated grids, where power is provided to entire towns (tens of thousands of connections); and 
• Off-grid RE power companies, providing mini -grid (MG) and solar home system (SHS) solutions  
Eligible technologies : As outlined in Annex 6, REPP 2  may invest in the following renewable energy power 
generation technologies: solar photovoltaic ( PV) with battery backup, onshore wind and run-of-river 
hydropower . 
Eligible countries: REPP 2  targets  small scale  renewable energy power generation projects in the Least 
Developed Countries of sub- Saharan Africa. To be eligible for receipt of GCF Proceeds , investments must 
be located in Host Countries, i.e.  a jurisdiction  which has  provided a Letter of Non -Objection in a form 
acceptable to the GCF. All such Host Countr y investments will be funded by Portfolio HoldCo 1  S.à r.l .  
REPP 2  may investment in other sub -Saharan African countries (including countries which are not classified 
as Least -Developed Countries ) where a Letter of No Objection has not been provided. Such investments 
will be funded from Portfolio HoldCo 2 S.à r.l   and will be ineligible to receive GCF funding.  
E&S risk categorisation:  REPP 2  will only invest in projects that are categorised as IFC risk category B or 
C. Category A projects are excluded . Please see Annex  6 for further detail.  
Investee requirements:  Where REPP 2  is making a project finance investment (typically for grid- connected 
IPPs), repayments and returns to REPP 2  depend on project SPV cashflows only (by definition, there is no 
recourse to the balance sheets of the sponsors). For this reason, risk assessment focuses on the commercial 
and technical viability of the project itself.  
Where REPP 2  is providing growth capital (either as debt or equity) to off -grid companies, the ability to 
service debt or generate returns depends on the long- term sustainability of the borrower. Key evaluation 
criteria include estimates of demand and revenue growth. In some cases, REPP 2  may insist on parent 
company guarantees prior to investment to boost creditworthiness of the borrower.  
Where REPP 2  is funding development for IPPs via development loans, such loans are exposure to the risk 
of failed development. Investment criteria include a requirement for a discernible pathway to commercial and 
technical viability for the project. In such cases, REPP 2  typical takes security in the form a pledge of the 
sponsor’s shares in the project SPV as well as legal charge over project assets . 
Off-grid companies:  Early -stage and g rowth capital for off -grid companies  will be provided in the form of 
equity  (ordinary and preference shares), convertible notes, junior and senior loans.  
SHS businesses : SHS businesses which sell SHS kits (typically panels , batteries , radio and/or TV , mobile 
phone charging  and lights ) and other appliances , including PUE devices (, cooling solutions, barber kits , 
irrigation  etc.) are eligible for REPP 2  funding. Business es which do not provide RE generation devices (e.g. 
sale of pico- solar devices only, or cookstoves), and business which operate as retailers or wholesales are 
not eligible for REPP 2  funding.


---
*Page 34*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 30 
 
 Mini-grid businesses:  Mini-grid business es must engage in the develop ment , construct ion and / or 
operat ion of  renewable energy  generation facilities (including generation, backup and reticulation)  at sites 
which are not initially connected to the main electricity grid. Typically , first-time connection includes lights. 
Such business may also provide PUE services  (e.g. irrigation, milling)  and other appliances  (such as 
pressure cookers , freezing) to their clients.   
On-grid projects:  IPPs must engage directly in the development, construction and operation of renewable 
energy  power generation facilities. REPP 2.0  may also fund the costs of grid connection.  
Skills and capacity development:  Skills and capacity building for developers (Activity 7), improved ESG 
practice (Activity 8 ) and promotion of gender equity (Activity 9 ) are targeted towards investee companies . 
To be eligible for these forms of support, beneficiaries must have an association with those companies 
(owners, management, staff, third-party advisors).  
9. Country prioritisation  
Only GCF Host Countries are eligible for investment from Portfolio HoldCo 1  S.à r.l . These Host Countries 
have been prioritised based on climate change vulnerabilities and impacts outlined in Section B.1 , Section 
D.4 as well as Annex 2 . Based on their NDCs and NAPs , further prioritisation is achieved through stakeholder 
consultation and by aligning their climate change mitigation and adaptation priorities with the objectives of 
REPP 2 , as outlined in Section D.4 as well as Annex 7 and Annex 2.   
For Portfolio HoldCo 2  S.à r.l , countries have been prioritized based on LDC status as well as demonstrated 
demand based on existing pipeline. In all cases, projects selected align with and promote country -level 
priorities in accordance with government strategic objectives, as reflected in NDCs, NAPs and other policy 
and strategic frameworks . 
Given that REPP 2  is driven by market demand for funding and requested support by project developers 
and off -grid renewable energy companies, country prioritisation is also a function of that demand, with the 
indicative pipeline shown in Annex 26  reflecting the current demand.  
10. REPP 2  risk management framework  
REPP 2  will incorporate a comprehensive enterprise risk management (“ERM”) framework , As outlined in 
the Operations Manual (Annex 21). Throughout the investment life cycle ( selecti on, analysis, approval, due 
lifecycle , implementation and exit / repayment),  risk will be assessed using a risk management toolkit which 
includes : 
• The ADAPT tool , used to assess a candidate investment’s eligibility  to be classified as a  climate change 
adaptation (cross- cutting) investment ; 
• A project risk assessment tool  (“RAT”) , which is used to assess  a wide range of risk factors  in all 
investments , including market, credit, financial, technical, environmental and social , country and 
regulatory as well as legal and documentation risks ; and 
• Additional specialised tools for detailed assess ment of  counterpar ty credit risk (for offtakers and 
borrowers) as well as environmental and social risks  (unique to each technology and asset class) . 
The REPP 2  risk management framework is designed to facilitate robust and dynamic risk identification, 
analysis, mitigation, reporting and ongoing review of the effectiveness of mitigation actions already taken. 
As Manager of REPP 2  and REPP Phase 1 before that , Camco Management Limited h as extensive 
experience and expertise in managing a wide range of risk factors, which affect individual technologies, 
asset classes and countries in unique ways. The ERM framework and proactive risk management approach 
adopted by an experienced team with on- the-ground presence throughout sub- Saharan Africa means that 
Camco is well- placed to manage existing and emerging risks effectively.

the unique risks associated with each technology (solar PV, wind and hydropow er), asset class (SHS, mini -
grids and grid- connected projects)  and Host Country . Key risks and risk mitigation actions for the successful 
implementation of REPP 2  and achievement of its goals are outlined in Section F.  
B.4. Implementation arrangements  
Camco REPP SCSp  
The main fund vehicle of REPP 2, and the primary Executing Entity (Camco REPP SCSp), is a société en 
commandite spéciale (“SCSp”) or special limited partnership, established in Luxembourg under the 
Luxembourg law of 10 August 1915 on commercial companies (the “1915 Law”).25  
The advantages of this structure for REPP 2 include:  
• The SCSp is a form with unlimited capacity and limited liability for its limited partners, provided they 
do not participate in the management of the SCSp vis -à-vis third parties (such management instead 
being carried on by its general partner);  
• It enables a flexible structure that can fully accommodate the blended capital structure of REPP 2;  
• The structure is market standard, frequently used for similar pooled investment structures and 
attractive to co- financing investors of REPP 2;  
• Governance can be adapted to reflect both the fiduciary standards of the GCF and any requirements of co -financing investors of REPP 2; and  
• Based in Luxembourg, Camco REPP SCSp will be an alternative investment fund or “AIF” for the purposes of the EU Alternative Investment Fund Managers Directive (AIFMD) and the UK equivalent of AIFMD. Compliance with AIFMD can be expected to provide additional comfort to EU/UK co -
financing investors of Camco REPP  SCSp . 
 
The general partner of Camco REPP SCSp will be Camco REPP GP S.a.r.l, a Luxembourg société à 
résponsabilité limitée (a Luxembourg limited company) wholly owned by Camco Management Limited.  
 GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp  
It is proposed that the GCF participate in Camco REPP SCSp via a special purpose vehicle (“GCF REPP 
Holdings  SCSp ”) established as a Luxembourg SCSp under the 1915 Law for which the GCF would be 
investor of record and no other limited partnership interests would be issued. GCF Proceeds would flow via

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 32 
 
 the AE to GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp  and then be contributed to Camco REPP SCSp . Camco Management 
Limited would be the sole shareholder of the general partner of GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp  . This GP/LP 
structure would be tax efficient for the GCF for the underlying participation in REPP 2. The funds received 
by GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp   would be contributed into Camco REPP SCSp via the Subscription and 
Partnership Agreement. Other investors would also contribute to Camco REPP SCSp or its feeder vehicle 
Camco REPP 2 F eeder SCSp as limited partners  and Note holders .  The Subscription and Partnership 
Agreement, binding on all limited partners, will be based on the Accreditation Master Agreement (“AMA”), 
the Term Sheet set out in Annex 14 and the Funded Activity Agreement (“FAA”) and provide for the 
investment terms, governance structure, policies and procedures, reflow waterfalls and respective rights of 
the limited partners –  including for distributions and the return of capital. A draft will be prepared subsequent 
to the approval of the FP by the GCF Board.  
Other feeder vehicle(s) in Camco REPP SCSp Holders of senior equity interests and senior note interests in Camco REPP SCSp are expected to participate 
in Camco REPP SCSp via one or more feeders which are also anticipated to be formed as Luxembourg SCSps under the 1915 Law.  
Side Letters  
A Side Letter is anticipated to be entered into with the GCF to implement certain aspects of the Term Sheet in Annex 14. Since side letters are likely also to be required by other co -financing investors in Camco REPP, 
the Term Sheet contains a most favoure
d nation provision (“MFN”) to protect the position of the GCF in 
Camco REPP. Th is MFN right is included in the Term Sheet in Annex 14 and provides  that during the term 
of the FAA, Camco REPP SCSp shall not enter into any additional, or modify any existing agreements with 
any existing or future investors to the effect of establishing rights or otherwise benefiting such investor in a manner more favourable in any material respect to such investor than the rights and benefits established in 
favour of the Fund pursuant to the Subsidiary Agreements, unless, in any such case, the Fund has been 
provided with such rights and/or benefits or waived its entitlement to such rights and/or benefits.  
Subsidiaries/Portfolio HoldCos  
To ensure that GCF Proceeds are provided only to Host Countries (being countries that have issued a Letter of Non- Objection), Camco REPP  SCSp   will establish two subsidiaries (in Luxembourg or other tax neutral 
or tax positive  form), likely to be Luxembourg sociétés à résponsabilité limitée (S.à r.l.) with Camco REPP 
SCSp, acting by its general partner, as the sole member: Portfolio HoldCo 1 S.a.r.l and Portfolio HoldCo 2 
S.à r.l .  
Portfolio HoldCo  1 S.à r.l  will only execute investment agreements for projects in Host Countries , i.e. for 
which the GCF has received Letters of No Objection. These countries are Cameroon, Democratic Republic 
of Congo (DRC), Lesotho, Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia at the time of 
submission but this list may expand in future if further Letters of Non -Objection are received and an 
amendment to the Programme is subsequently approved by  the GCF Board.  
Portfolio HoldCo 2  S.à r.l  will execute investment agreements in the full range of REPP 2  eligible c ountries 
(non- NOL countries in sub- Saharan Africa with a focus on LDCs). According to the REPP 2 eligibility 
requirements, the list of non- NOL countries may include any other countries in sub- Saharan Africa  (provided 
that in the case of investments in the Host Countries, only after Portfolio Holdco 1 S.à r.l   is 75% committed 
for investment and in respect of these investments, the Fund and other Junior Class Interest (as such term is defined in the Term Sheet in Annex 14) holders would be drawn pro rata to their undrawn commitments) . 
 
The Subscription and Partnership Agreement, constitutional documents of the HoldCos and intra- group 
Camco REPP SCSp structuring will ensure that the capital contribution of the GCF will be deployed (and 
returned) solely in relation to the investments of Portfolio HoldCo 1  S.à r.l  in Host Countries. The anticipated 
split between the Portfolio HoldCos is set out in the financial model, but for the purpose of clarity, non- GCF 
investors will also be investing in projects in Host Countries via Portfolio HoldCo 1  S.à r.l   and this could 
include participation of other Junior Interest Class holders. However, GCF Proceeds may only be invested


---
*Page 37*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 33 
 
 in Host Countries through Portfolio Holdco 1  S.à r.l . As such, if an investment of Camco REPP SCSp is 
located in Host Countries and in other countries, it may only be made through Portfolio Holdco 2  S.à r.l .  
The dual Holdco structure provides GCF the protections necessary to ensure separation of funds, while 
minimizing transaction costs and maintaining a simplified governance agreement (under a single 
Subscription and Partnership Agreement).  
Management  
The general partner of Camco REPP SCSp (which will be a Camco -owned entity, Camco REPP GP S.à r.l.) 
has primary responsibility for management of the partnership. However, as an AIF, Camco REPP SCSp 
(and, subject to the terms of the long form documentation and further Luxembourg legal revi ew and advice, 
GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp ) will appoint an external third- party service provider as its AIFM.  Under AIFMD, 
the AIFM will be responsible for risk management and portfolio management of REPP 2. The AIFM will 
delegate certain of its duties to Cam co Management Limited  pursuant to an Investment Management 
Agreement. We anticipate these duties will include portfolio management.  
On the basis that:  
a. The Accredited Entity is a party to the AMA and will execute the FAA with the GCF;  
b. The Accredited Entity will be the sole shareholder of  Camco REPP GP S.à r.l  and a party to it’s  LPA;  
c. The third party AIFM will be appointed as alternative investment fund manager by Camco REPP 
SCSp pursuant to an alternative investment fund management agreement;  
d. The Accredited Entity will be appointed as investment manager by Camco REPP SCSp and will be 
the sole shareholder of the general partner  of Camco REPP SCSp, which will be a party to the 
Subscription and Partnership Agreement  
e. Portfolio HoldCo 1 S.à r.l. and Portfolio HoldCo 2 S.à r.l. will adhere to the Investment Management 
Agreement,  
each of the above is a Subsidiary Agreement as defined in the AMA (and see “Roles and Responsibilities” 
below)

Portfolio HoldCo Structure 
The dual -HoldCo structure is designed to address the twin aims of addressing the broader market demand 
for funding for smaller -scale renewables and building on the success of REPP Phase 1, while maintaining 
the strict separation of funds and reflows required under the FAA. The dual HoldCo structure is a simple and cost-effective way of ensuring separation between investments (and resulting cash flows) in Host Countries 
(Portfolio HoldCo 1 S.à r.l. ) and non- Host Countries (Portfolio HoldCo 2 S.à r.l.
). GCF Proceeds, as 
channelled via the AE, through GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp  into Camco REPP SCSp will be available to 
Portfolio HoldCo 1  S.à r.l. only.  
The capital structure (i.e. same blended finance structure of junior equity, senior equity and senior debt) is 
broadly duplicated in both HoldCos (subordination is identical), as shown in the indicative table below. Senior 
debt distributions are made in proportion to funding commitments from the Camco REPP SCSp  to each 
Portfolio HoldCo. Investments and reflows are made independently in each HoldCo.  
It is possible that the amount of commitments of different classes could be different in each of Portfolio 
HoldCo 1  S.à r.l.  and Portfolio HoldCo 2  S.à r.l. , but with effect from the Final Closing Date Portfolio HoldCo 
1 S.à r.l   shall not have a greater ratio of Junior Class Interests to Senior Class Interests as compared to 
Portfolio HoldCo 2 S.à r.l. , subject always to the restriction that Junior Class Interests may not (with effect 
from the Final Closing Date) exceed 40% of Total Capital Commitments.

Track record  
Camco is a climate and impact fund manager, leading the clean energy transition in emerging markets. 
Having spent over thirty years in the renewable energy sector, we offer practical and valuable financing 
solutions, pairing the discipline of a development  bank with the agility of a private company. Camco was 
formed in Nairobi in 1989, is a regulated small Alternative Investment Fund Manager in the UK and is a 
Green Climate Fund Accredited Entity.   
As a climate and impact fund manager, Camco’s  track record includes:  
• Co-advisor of the USD200 million Green Africa Power (GAP) mandate, 2014- 2017.  
• Sole manager of the Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP Phase 1): GBP48  million first 
close in 2015, GBP40  million second close in 2019.  
• Sponsor and manager of Spark Energy Services, a USD50 million, commercial and industrial energy 
efficiency and renewable energy facility focused on key African markets . Spark is funded by private 
and commercial funders and is currently in financial close; and  
• Currently fundraising for Transforming Island Development for Electrification and Sustainability 
(TIDES), a USD100 million renewable energy and energy access facility for Pacific Islands.  
Over a 30 -year track record, Camco impacts include:  
• 60+ projects completed  
• 15.5 million people provided with new and improved electricity access  
• 600 MW installed capacity realized; and  
• USD900 million in third party funding mobilized 
Camco’s key strengths include:  
• A long- standing presence in the region, with offices in west, east and southern Africa;  
• Detailed local knowledge from the ground up, combined with international reach;  
• Extensive, deep relationships with –  and understanding of -- developers, policymakers, donors, DFIs 
and private finance; and  
• Experience across a range of key disciplines including policy, regulation, market and project 
development, technical assistance, equity, debt, monitoring & evaluation, asset management and 
exits.  
• Deep experience of financing small -scale renewables in Sub- Saharan Africa.  
Flow of Funds  
Disbursements  
• The GCF will commit USD50 million , with GCF Proceeds provided to Camco Management Limited 
as AE in six annual disbursements, in accordance with Annex 14 (Term Sheet) ; 
• The GCF will pay fees to the AE in 6 equal instalments in accordance with Annex 14 (Term Sheet);  
• The GCF Proceeds received by Camco Management Limited as AE under the FAA will be transferred 
from the GCF Account to a dedicated account in the name of GCF REPP Holdings  SCSp ; 
• The GCF Proceeds will be transferred contemporaneously as a capital contribution to Camco REPP

1. Motivation for GCF funding 
The proposed capital structure for REPP 2  is outlined in the table below.

The capital structure for REPP 2  is designed to help to address key market failures  for smaller -scale 
renewables across sub -Saharan Africa, including:  
• A lack of early -stage or development funding for smaller -scale renewables; and  
• A limited risk appetite from both public and private funders for early -stage and smaller -scale projects.  
Approved GCF funding programmes for climate mitigation  through renewables are typically focused on 
larger -scale projects. These programmes have provided only limited support for early -stage funding, 
particularly for smaller project sizes (Annex 23). There is also relatively limited geographic overlap between 
the proposed Host Countries  for REPP 2  and programmes which are already funded by the GCF. Climate 
mitigation funding from GCF for REPP 2  thus has the potential to be strongly complementary to existing 
funded activities .  
In terms of climate adaptation , the GCF recognises “the urgent and immediate needs of vulnerable 
countries, in particular least developed countries (LDCs), small island developing States (SIDS) and African States” and has recently reaffirmed this decision.
27,28 Despite this, the scale and scope of funding from the 
GCF for adaptation in sub -Saharan Africa is relatively small. For the African region, adaptation makes up 
9% of total approved funding within the GCF portfolio, with cross -cutting programmes making up a further 
13%.29 Of the energy programmes reviewed in Annex 23, only two are cross -cutting programmes involving 
renewables. GCF funding for REPP 2  and a cross -cutting programme will thus contribute to the GCF’s 
strategic priorities and will make a materially positive impact to build climate resilience across the region. 
There is no lack of financing for large- scale, commercially viable renewable energy projects across sub-
Saharan Africa. The analysis presented in Annex 23 shows a different picture for smaller -scale projects as 
well as nascent renewable energy markets in LDCs and middle- income countries, where significant funding 
gaps exist. The same analysis highlights the paucity of funding available for early -stage growth capital of off -


---
*Page 45*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 41 
 
 grid renewables and development funding for grid -connected projects. In general, private capital providers 
do not offer funding to cover development costs or do so on strictly commercial terms which tend to 
discriminate against (“crowd out”) local develope rs. REPP 2  will seek to support smaller -scale renewables 
and local developers, providing funding for growth during project development and addressing funding gaps 
for construction. In the process, REPP 2  will both address a key barrier and have the potenti al to be highly 
complementary when working with other sources of public and private capital. REPP 2 ’s focus on funding to 
achieve scale and sustainability for off -grid companies, combined with its relatively unique risk appetite for 
early -stage, smaller -scale renewables, will make its funding strongly additional –  acting to crowd in other 
sources of public and private funding for construction.  
REPP Phase 1 is funded through the United Kingdom’s Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office (FCDO). An initial capitalisation of GBP 48 million was, due to the early success of the programme, followed 
by a further commitment of GBP 40 million. Despite this, the current funding cycle for REPP Phase 1 is 
coming to an end and the FCDO has no obligation to provide funding to REPP 2 .  
Despite this , REPP Phase 1 / FCDO has agreed in principle to provide matching funding of up to USD50 
million in junior equity funding, alongside the GCF, for REPP 2 . A term sheet is in an advanced stage of 
discussion.   
The AE is in advanced discussions with several private investors (such as Calvert Impact ) as well as 
commercial  investors (such as FinnFund,  which has indicated that the source of its funding will be from 
private capital markets, with funding provided to REPP 2 on commercial terms). B oth investors  have 
indicated their interest in REPP 2 through Term Sheets and Heads of Terms.  
Junior equity funding from both the GCF and REPP  Phase 1 / FCDO will be catalytic in securing the 
fundraising targets for REPP 2 . In fundraising conducted to date, private and commercial funders have 
consistently indicated a desire to secure funding for the junior equity tranche (which effectively de -risks all 
more senior investors ) before considering their own funding decision.  The process of s ecuring the initial 
funding targets for REPP 2 and crowding in private and commercial funders into the structure has been 
delayed for these reasons.  Requirements and commitments for co -investment, including co mmercial co -
investment, are outlined further in Section C.1.  
The target size of REPP 2  ($250m) was set to meet several objectives:  being large enough  to build a 
diversified portfolio (37 transactions) , thereby  minimiz ing concentration risk , being large enough to attract 
commercial and private investors  (as typically investors don’t want to commit a large portion of fund size), 
and being able to meet  the significant market needs as exemplified by the large pipeline of transactions 
originated by Camco ($1bn+) .   
2. Platform financing instruments  
REPP 2  is a blended finance facility featuring a  junior equity  tranche, equity and debt. The proposed capital 
structure for REPP 2  (and the GCF’s position within it) , along with  the selection of financing instruments 
used to complete the capital stack  have been designed to crowd in additional sources of both public and 
private capital.  
The junior equity tranche, which will act as a first loss buffer,  is designed to deliver incrementally greater 
impact and further mobilisation of third- party funds (both public and private) from more risk -averse investors. 
The sizing (at least 30% of total available capital) and subordinated position of this tranche in the REPP 2  
capital stack will provide protection for all investors in more senior tranches. It is proposed that the GCF 
invest junior equity on a pari passu  basis alongside other donors  and DFIs. For clarity, the protection 
provided by the GCF junior equity investment will be limited to other investors in the NOL portfolio ( Portfolio 
HoldCo 1  S.à r.l ). The junior equity tranche will target low, single- digit returns in the base case.


---
*Page 46*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 42 
 
  
30 GCF 2020: Updated Strategic Plan for the Green Climate Fund: 2020-2023, GCF document GCF/B.27/21 
https://www.greenclimate.fund/document/gcf -b27-21  The senior  equity tranche  is designed to provide more commercially oriented equity returns. Camco is  
targeting 40% of the total capital requirement in the form of senior  equity from a combination of private 
investors, international financial institutions (IFIs) and development finance institutions (DFIs), all of whom 
will effectively be crowded into the capital structure of REPP 2  through the protection of returns provided by 
the junior equity tranche.  
The senior debt tranche  will enjoy maximum credit enhancement from the equity tranches. The senior debt 
tranche is expected to be financed by commercial debt providers.  
3. Level of concessionality  
The GCF’s strategic objective is to catalyse climate finance for mitigation and adaptation which is new, 
additional (in the sense that a project or programme would not have occurred in the absence of GCF 
funding), adequate and predictable.30 Accordingly, REPP 2  will ensure that its investments are additional 
and contribute to the establishment of a more viable and active market for smaller -scale renewables in Host 
Countries .  
In financing smaller -scale renewable energy projects and businesses, REPP 2  recognises the need to strike 
a balance between the need for concessionality, the levels of real and perceived risk and the desire to grow 
a sustainable market for renewables. In the African context, these risks  are higher, given that the market is 
as yet unproven, and the perception of risks is also higher than other markets. In order to mobilize capital in the sector the balance of risk and return provide d needs to match the risk appetite and requirements  for all 
investors.  
Small- scale renewable energy businesses in African LDCs struggle to attract funding in general, and 
particularly private and commercial funding. The fact that barriers and significant funding gaps persist is 
demonstrated through the experience of REPP Phase 1, the broader market barriers identified in Section 
B.2(a)  and the need to develop a tranched capital structure for REPP 2 with a layer  of junior equity below 
senior equity and notes layer s to attract  private and commercial investors  as much as possible, given limited 
appetite of such investors for the sector .  
At the level of the underlying assets funded by REPP 2, and in providing gap financing to smaller -scale grid-
connected projects, REPP 2  will help developers and sponsors of these projects to overcome a range of 
barriers , notably limited access to finance and to financing on terms that allow these projects to become 
commercially viable. Traditional investors, such as banks and utilities, often consider small -scale projects to 
be too risky or too expensive to finance.  In addition, d evelopment finance institutions and other typical 
investors in the sector typically require minimum ticket sizes of USD10- 20 millio n, which is often  larger than 
the financing needed by small -scale projects.  The economics of small -scale projects are often marginal,  and 
the project development and construction risks are often higher than for large projects, with the potential for 
revenue and profit generation limited by the smaller installed capacity of the plant. For these reasons, REPP 
2 must take a more flexible approach  to address the financing gap, with a risk -adjusted return expectation 
that is lower than what is typically required by the market.   
To address these challenges, REPP 2  offers flexible financing with a risk -adjusted return that is lower than 
what is typically required by the market, in order to help reduce the barriers to financing small -scale 
renewable energy projects in Sub- Saharan Africa and promote the development of  these projects in the 
region.  
Targets for leveraging addition private and commercial capital at the level of the underlying assets funded 
by REPP 2 are also commensurate to the level of appetite from  such investors.  The target of leveraging 
USD393  million in private and commercial funding in the underlying investments represents 50%  of the 
overall target for third party funding mobilised (USD786 million). The target is derived from the experience


---
*Page 47*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 43 
 
 of REPP Phase 1  and is set conservatively  in that the proportion of third party funding from private and 
commercial sources is no higher than that already achieved in REPP Phase 1. While the AE is motivated to 
increase this proportion over time, the target is set conservatively in recognition that the risk appetite f rom 
private and commercial funders remains low and it remains a difficult task to  leverage additional private 
investment.   
In this context, GCF funding is highly catalytic, in attracting  the existing fund-level co -financing targets from 
private and commercial investors and in leveraging additional co -financing at the level of the underlying 
investments.  
REPP 2  acknowledges that it cannot price for risk on a purely commercial basis. If commercially viable 
projects and a ready supply of commercial funders already existed, the funding gaps identified in Section 
B.2(a) and A nnex 23 would not exist and there would be no need for a facility such as REPP 2 . The learnings 
from REPP Phase 1 and other related facilities active across sub -Saharan Africa is that small -scale 
renewable energy businesses struggle to access private capital on terms which leave their projects 
commercially viable and that public financing in this space is still required. In this context, funding for REPP 2 
will contribute to the continued growth and increasing sustainability of commercial renewable energy 
sectors in Host Countries .  
If REPP 2  adopted a purely concessional approach to pricing, the effect would be to negatively disrupt a 
nascent market for renewables in Host Countries . A purely concessional pricing approach would lack 
financial rigour and have the unintended consequence of crowding out private sector funding. The approach 
to concessionality for REPP 2  will thus strive to achieve a balance between the need for a considered level 
of concessionality, in order to build the market and crowd in private sector funding, and the need for rigour 
and more commercial pricing approaches, where the market, investor appetite and project economics allow 
for it.  
The concessionality of the GCF junior equity funding is defined to pro tect other more senior investors in the 
capital stack  and provide an appropriate level of return for the GCF, while simultaneously crowding in 
(catalysing) other private and commercial investors . The junior equity tranches in Portfolio HoldCo 1  S.à r.l   
and Portfolio HoldCo 2  S.à r.l   (sized and priced identically) are designed to provide first loss protect ion to  
other investors in the capital stack . The financial model demonstrates that the sizing (40% of total capital) 
and pricing of the junior equity tranche is met while also facilitating targeted returns for the equity providers. 
For s enior debt providers , the structure and pricing of the capital stack also supports  current market  pricing . 
4. Risk -sharing 
The proportion of GCF and other public funding to private funding sources targeted for the REPP 2  fundraise 
(a ratio of 1:4) represents a realistic goal given the scale of private financing that Camco expects can be 
mobilised into this sector, for the activities proposed in this Programme.  
For the GCF, where an invest ment  in the junior equity tranche is proposed, risk will be shared pari passu  
with other investor s.

*Page 48*

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3 .0 | PAGE 44 
 
 B.6. Exit strategy  
1. Overall  exit strategy 
REPP 2  is a fixed -term (2 0-year) fund. The limited partners of Camco REPP SCSp  may agree to extend the 
objects of the REPP 2  as further set out in Annex 14 (Term Sheet) . 
Junior equity investors will earn a targeted return  over the 20- year term of REPP 2. Senior equity investors 
will earn a targeted return in the base case. Senior debt will be repaid in full (principle plus accrued interest) 
over a weighted average  tenor of 7 years.  The Subscription and Partnership Agreement will, on cust omary 
terms,  contain a range of exit scenarios  in re limited members  including insolvency of the limited member or  
transfer for value by the limited member . The dissolution mechanics for GCF REPP Holdings  and Camco  
REPP SCSp  will be set out in Holdings GPA and Subscription and  Partnership Agreement,  respectively . 
Following the completion of the activities of REPP 2  (including the dissolution of the Portfolio HoldCos), the 
final disbursements under the cash waterfall  and the completion of any residual obligations  of any Executing 
Entity pursuant to a Subsidiary Agreement,  a recommendation would be made by Camco REPP GP S.a.r.l,  
as general partner of  Camco REPP SCSp  to the limited partners of Camco REPP SCSp for dissolution. In 
the absence of a voluntary dissolution, Camco REPP SCSp will be dissolved at the end of its term as set 
out in its deed of incorporation.  Pursuant to . GCF REPP Holdings will be dissolved in a similar manner upon  
the satisfaction of all the obligations of the Holdings GP under the Holdings GPA.  
For debt instruments , the baseline scenario is repayment in full (principal plus accrued interest) over the 
loan tenor.  Where possible, REPP 2  will seek to reduce or exit its debt investments through refinancing 
opportunities , either for individual transactions or at the portfolio level . 
The baseline scenario for equity investments is realisation in full of principal repayment plus returns over the targeted investment period.  Where possible and profitable, REPP will seek an early exit from equity 
investments through new fundraising rounds and / or sell -down of existing shareholding to new investors. 
The portfolio of active transactions for REPP 2  will be actively managed in order ensure orderly repayment 
of debt and equity returns and to proactively identify and execute opportunities for new investment, 
refinancing or sale.  
2. Pricing approach  
From a pricing perspective, the REPP 2  approach ensures an appropriate blend of concessional and 
commercial pricing. This approach accounts for its additionality mandate and seeks to minimize market 
distortion as far as possible – crowding in other sources of private and public funding rather that crowding 
other funding out through a pricing approach which is too concessional.  
Where debt and equity investments are priced on a more concessional basis, the goal will be explicitly to 
support business growth in order to achieve long- term commercial sustainability.  
Where REPP 2  makes equity investments in off -grid companies, opportunities for co -investment and sell-
down exist as these businesses grow (later fundraises) – which may represent opportunities for REPP 2  to 
achieve partial or full exit or continue as a more- diluted equity sponsors.  
Where REPP 2  provides development loans, these loans will include convertibility options which are 
designed to compensate the facility for the additional risk taken during development. This approach also 
enforces appropriate rigour among developers and sponsors.


---
*Page 49*
---

B 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 45 
 
  
 Where business or project economics allow, REPP 2  will invest on purely commercial terms. This approach 
ensures minimum market distortion, maximises pricing rigour in line with market benchmarks and ensures 
that funds can be returned or repaid to REPP 2  and can achieve approach return for risk.  
3. Paradigm shift 
REPP 2  helps to innovate and transform the market for smaller -scale renewable in each of the Host 
Countries . Benefits including skills and technology transfer, development of an active market with direct 
private sector participation are all permanent. These elements achieve the targeted paradigm shift potential.  
With the expectation of SHS systems, all technologies will have a lifetime over 20 years , therefore impacting 
countries transformation to resilient low carbon world . SHS companies are expected to succeed in their 
business mode, offering affordable energy to vulnerable communities , and hence providing long- term service 
beyond technical lifetime of the system.  Monitoring and verification of the effectiveness of the projects i s 
conducted in accordance with industry -best practices as described in Section E. 7.  REPP 2 performance will 
be reported to its stakeholders, through annual  reports on its key KPIs, which are published on the REPP 2  
website. Through organic growth of host countr y financial markets and with REPP 2  demonstrational  effect 
it is expected that other developers and funds will adopt a similar approach.

A technical assistance component of REPP 2  will be funded separately to the Programme on a grant -
funded basis  through the Technical Assistance Facility (to be established as a separate legal entity).  
Until the TAF is established and operational, a portion of REPP 2  operating costs will be allocated to 
support market development, skills and capacity building and technology transfer.33  
REPP 2  funding for skills development and capacity building within Host Countries  as well as for the 
smaller -scale renewables sector as a whole will be used to:  
• Build the capacity, skills and experience of renewable energy project developers , both local 
and international, as well as management and staff of early -stage off-grid companies ; 
• Contribute to a stronger enabling environment for renewables through building institutional 
and regulatory capacity  and strengthening policy and regulatory frameworks ; and 
• Ensure business model innovation and technology transfer occur.  
These activities will be funded out of normal operating costs of REPP 2  (with contributions from all 
investors, including the GCF), based on a final budget that is expected to be approved by the general 
partner of  Camco REPP SCSp .

 D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
D.1. Impact potential  
1. Programme targets aligned to GCF indicators for reduced emissions and 
increased resilience  
Targets for the REPP 2  are as follows:34 
GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered  
• Total GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered (Core Indicator 1):  The target for 
GHG emissions reduced or avoided over the lifetime is  12,720,297  tCO 2e.35 
• Installed renewable energy capacity (Supplementary Indicator 1.3):  The installed capacity target for 
the Programme is 3 30MW. 
• Renewable energy generated (Supplementary Indicator 1.4):  The renewable energy generation 
target for the Programme is 8 12,771  MWh/year.  
Direct and indirect beneficiaries reached  
• Total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries reached (Core Indicator 2):  The target for the total 
number of direct beneficiaries, which is the number critical community services customers  people  
benefiting of productive use of energy equipment that builds resilience , as well as people benefiting 
from isolated grids and mini- grids that improve the resilience of energy infrastructure  is estimated  ex-
ante at  370,244 . 
• The numbers of direct  female beneficiaries are estimated to be 1 11,073  and  the number of direct 
male beneficiaries is estimated to be 259,171  
• The target for the total number indirect beneficiaries, which is the number of people with improved 
energy connections  through energy systems made resilient , reached through the Programme is  
estimated ex -ante at  359,603. The numbers of indirect female beneficiaries are estimated to be  
179,801 and the same amount of indirect male beneficiaries is expected.   
• Beneficiaries adopting improved and/or new climate -resilient livelihood options (Supplementary 
Indicator 2.1):  The target for the number of beneficiaries adopting improved and/or new climate-
resilient livelihood options through the Programme is 27,652 people . The numbers of female 
beneficiaries are estimated to be 8,295 and the estimated number of male beneficiaries is 19,356 . 
Infrastructure and built environment  
• Value of physical assets made more resilient to the effects of climate change and more able to reduce 
GHG emissions: The target for improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure USD37 million . 
2. Mitigation outcomes  
Outcome 1: Reduced GHG emissions from renewable energy installations  
The mitigation targets will be estimated using a technology split based on REPP 2 ’s transaction pipeline, 
average technology -specific electricity generation estimates, UNFCCC- approved grid emission factors and 
average technology lifetimes. The energy baseline is the total annual electricity generated or renewable 
energy supplied by the project activity in line with the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) methods: AMS -
I–A - Electricity generation by the user (version 19 ), AMS -I.D – Grid-connected renewable electricity 
generation (version 18), and AMS -I.L – Electrification of rural communities using renewable energy (version 
4). All mitigation targets will be aligned with host -country targets and priorities.

 3. Adaptation outcomes  
Outcome 2: Improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure 
The value of the energy infrastructure made resilient is estimated based on the estimated value of the 
pipeline of projects in countries in which the energy system is currently vulnerable due to 1)  a reliance on 
hydropower in countries which are prone to drought  and 2 grid instability due to widespread power outages 
and poor grid maintenance and management  and exposure of grid to climate impacts . This methodology is 
consistent with GCF core indicator 3 (value of physical assets made more resilient to the effects of climate 
change).  The Annex  2 provides country specific vulnerabilities  of the energy sector.  
Currently, in average 20  % of all REPP 2  on-grid investments  are anticipated to assist in building resilience  
of energy infrastructure.  Resulting to U SD  37 million  value of assets made more climate -resilient. Please 
see Annex  22 for detailed calculation and assumptions.  
Outcome 3: Improved climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities  
Improved climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities will be achieved through the provision of 
access to clean energy, which improves the ability of off -grid communities  to respond to climate  impacts and 
build resilience .  
The number of people who will adopt improved livelihood options has been estimated with reference to the 
REPP Phase 1 experience, specifically relating to the number of productive uses of energy (PUE) activities 
and microbusinesses operated by off -grid companies. Using this data, it is estimated that 5% of off -grid 
customers will be microbusinesses and approximately half of these microbusinesses  are anticipated to 
implement PUE activities that build resilience i.e. include solar water pumping and irrigation , produce 
refrigeration, electric  milling , improved access to markets through mobility  etc. Similarly based on R EPP 
Phase 1.0 data it is estimated that 1% of off -grid customers will be providers of critical services e.g., clinics 
and hospitals, fire stations, schools, drinking water stations, cell phone towers etc.  
4. Enabling outcomes  
Outcome 4: Increased private sector investment  
Finance mobilised for renewable energy generation benefits local developers, builds a stronger local market 
for smaller -scale renewables and helps to build gender equity.  
REPP 2  will work to mobilise the private and public f inance necessary to grow a robust and viable market 
for renewables by applying innovative climate funding solutions to overcome historic barriers to finance and 
demonstrate the bankability of projects to crowd in other investors. In addition, REPP 2  will facilitate 
knowledge transfer and innovation through demand- driven capacity building efforts to unlock investments 
and support successful project implementation. REPP 2  is anticipated to mobilise  USD786 million additional 
finance towards climate -resilient renewable energy infrastructure.  
USD7 0 million – or 30%  – of REPP 2  funding will be targeted  to assist the USD7 0 billion funding gap facing 
female entrepreneurs in Africa.36 REPP 2  will aim to dedicate a portion of its funding to renewable energy 
companies in line with 2X Challenge’s Gender Lens Investment (2X GLI) criteria. This means the company 
must: 
• be founded by a woman or women or be majority -owned by women; and / or  
• have at least 30% women in senior management; and / or  
• provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women; and / or  
• have a workforce that is at least 30% women, together with a progressive human resource policy .

---

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 51 
   
 Outcome 5: Contributing to stronger technology deployment, innovation and market 
transformation  
REPP 2  will contribute to stronger enabling market environment for renewables through building capacity  
and financing  a stronger level and faster technology deployment, innovation and market development in 
Host Countries. To achieve this, REPP 2  will: 
• contribute to technology deployment, dissemination, development or transfer and innovation (Core 
Indicator 6) : REPP 2  will contribute to more and more rapid technology deployment  by providing 
targeted support to renewable energy companies and local financial institutions during early and 
construction stages of the assets.  REPP 2  will also finance the deployment of renewable energy 
technology in nascent markets , often financ ing first-of-a-kind transactions and business models . The 
innovative and transformational nature of each transaction will be  asse ssed during the project 
approval process based on approved selection  criteria  defined in the REPP 2.  Operations Manual  
(Annex 21)  and 
• contribute to market development  and transformation at the sectoral, local, or national level  (Core 
Indicator 7): REPP 2  will stimulate sectoral development that would otherwise lag or not occur 
(additionality ) and help to establish or build a market ecosystem that will allow the ongoing growth of 
small and distributed renewables in Host Countries  and across the region ( transformational ). REPP 
2 will take a bottom -up approach, working closely with its investees and by extension, at a deal level, 
Host Country  governments and regulators to  help to develop a viable renewable energy sector, and 
facilitating the development of a sustainable financing market for smaller -scale and off -grid 
renewable energy projects. REPP 2  will help to build skills and establish disciplines which underpin 
a commercially -viable market for smaller -scale renewables through its support for local developers 
and based on its application of sound commercial investment principles.  
Progress towards this outcome will be monitored on an ongoing basis and reported via the mid- term and 
final reports to the GCF . 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential  
1. Potential for scale, replicability and sustainability at the Programme and 
market level  
The REPP 2  Programme  seeks  to mobilise additional public and private funding and achieve scale and 
sustainability for the smaller -scale renewable energy market across sub -Saharan Africa.  
REPP 2  aims to deliver a paradigm shift for the small -scale renewables sector within Host Countries  and 
across the region as a whole, with demonstration potential more broadly across emerging markets. The 
Programme also aims to deliver a paradigm shift at the level of individual projects and renewable energy 
businesses, where additional funding is needed to achieve scale and sustainability for off -grid companies 
and where local developers of grid- connected projects need funding and support to develop technically - and 
commercially -viable projects.  
In both senses, REPP 2  builds upon and scales up the early successes of REPP Phase 1 by mobilising 
finance from public , private and other commercial sources, using its blended finance approach. 
The potential of REPP 2  to deliver this paradigm shift can be demonstrated through the following:  
• The market study presented in Annex 2 identifies the scale of demand currently unmet for funding of 
smaller -scale renewables across the region. A  prior assessment identifies a total funding demand 
and potential to scale up financing in excess of USD900 million, with more than USD565 million of 
that target located in Host Countries  identified in this Funding Proposal;  
 
36 IFC 2019. MSME Finance Gap: Assessment of the shortfalls and opportunities in financing micro, small, and medium enterprises in 
emerging markets


---
*Page 56*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 52 
   
 • REPP 2  support for innovative37 technologies , services and business models  in nascent markets  
provides an opportunity to demonstrate technical and commercial viability, implement new regulatory 
frameworks  and crowd in public and private finance; 
In this regard, REPP 2  will scale up and replicate existing successes achieved by REPP Phase 1, 
such as in Burundi, where REPP Phase 1 support for the first solar PV IPP in the country 
demonstrated the viability of a renewable energy technology in new market and led to further 
expansion of its market  for renewable energy IPPs .38,39 Another example can be given from Sierra 
Leone, where REPP Phase -1 investee provides electrification and access to PuE equipment through  
an innovative rental model, in which customers rent out solar -charged lithium ion batter ies in 24 -hour 
increments to service low income customers ;40   
• By providing early -stage funding and technical support  to local developers  in Host Countries , REPP 
2 will develop a stronger enabling market environment  for smaller -scale renewables.   
• The design of the REPP 2 facility ensures that GCF and other more senior funders can be repaid in 
full, inclusive of targeted returns. The blended finance approach of REPP 2 is designed to de -risk 
investments. With an expected aggregate portfolio loss of 20% , the junior  equity providers provide 
protection to the equity investors , while their investment is still fully returned at the end of the fund 
Senior debt providers, including private debt providers, enjoy most risk- reduction through the blended 
finance structure. The REPP 2 risk appetite, broader risk management procedures, project selection, 
deal structuring, and pricing and portfolio management approach are also designed to ensure 
sustainable returns for investors; and  
• The platform approach of REPP 2  helps to deliver specific climate change mitigation and adaptation 
outcomes which contribute to climate -resilient development pathways  consistent with national 
priorities and plans for Host Countries. Such support, and specifically the demonstration impact of a 
more commercial approach (which directly targets crowding in of private capital), works as a blueprint 
for other markets, including LDCs, across sub -Saharan Africa and further within emerging markets 
globally.  
2. Potential for scale, replicability and sustainability at the project and 
investment level  
• For REPP 2 , investments in off -grid companies help to expand to nascent markets, achieve growth, 
commercial scale and long- term sustainability;  
• For grid connected projects, funding and support from REPP 2  supports the development and 
implementation of technically - and commercially viable projects which operate for design lifetimes  of 
20 years and longer , often first -of-its-kind in the host country. The demonstration impact from such 
funding combined with the knowledge, skills and technology transfer to local developers, funders, 
institutions and regulatory bodies help to build the enabling environment for small -scale renewables, 
based on commercial  best practise;  
• At a community  level, REPP 2  especially for off -grid electrification builds climate resilience for 
women, children and other vulnerable groups; and 
• By the providing funding at scale for both off -grid and grid- connected renewables, REPP 2  helps to 
reduce GHG emissions and build climate resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change for 
people and communities in rural areas, as well as improve the climate resilience of energy 
infrastructure. These represent durable and significant contributions to climate- resilient development 
pathways which are consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans.

 D.3. Sustainable development  
1. Sustainable Development Goals  
REPP 2  contributes to the following SDGs:  
• SDG 1: No Poverty;  
• SDG 5: Gender Equality;  
• SDG 7: Affordable, Clean Energy;  
• SDG 8: Decent Work and Economic Growth;  
• SDG 11: Sustainable Cities and Communities  
• SDG 13: Climate Action  
• SDG 17: Partnership for the Global Goals  
The ultimate beneficiaries of REPP 2  funding for off -grid projects are the communities and businesses who 
will gain often first -time access to a clean, reliable and affordable source of electricity, which will contribute 
adaptive capacity and build climate resilience at a local level. Grid-connected projects supported by REPP 
2 will provide clean electricity to the grid that will contribute to national GHG emission reductions  helping to 
build the resilience of national energy infrastructure. Local private sector companies developing small- scale 
renewable energy projects will also benefit from REPP 2  funding and related capacity building leading to 
quality jobs . REPP 2  will mobilised additional  funding towards to climate action .  
2. Environmental co -benefits (co-benefit 4) 
The potential environmental and social impacts of all projects will be managed according to the REPP 2  
Environmental and Social Policy and Procedures (to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP 
SCSp ) and the Environmental and Social Management (ESMS) Framework (Annex 6). Both the Policy and 
Procedures and the ESMS will adhere to IFC Performance Standards. At the project / investment level, a 
specific environmental and social management system (ESMS) will be established to mitigate and manage 
any negative impacts. REPP 2  capacity building activities and early -stage funding will improve environmental  
and social  performance within portfolio companies  (co- benefit 4. The improved performance  will be 
measured through the establishment and implementation  of high quality  ESMS and will lead to benefits such 
as improved waste management practices, mitigating  negative impact to biodiversity,  soil and water 
resources. In addition to  GHG mitigation, additional co- benefits, such as improved ambient air quality are 
expected to occur. However, these are not quantified.  
3. Social co -benefits including health co -benefit  
At the household level, the small renewable energy projects that REPP 2  will offer multiple environmental 
and social co- benefits. R EPP 2 funding and support will lead to improved quality of life through  communit ies 
and critical services connected to electricity (co- benefit 3). Co- benefits include improved access to lighting , 
healthcare, lighting, refrigeration, improved access to information and more productive use of energy (PUE) 
opportunities. These co- benefits will allow further socioeconomic development of H ost Countries and their 
off-grid communities . There are particular co- benefits for women and girls , where greater energy access 
allows them to participate in new income generation and educational activities . In general, i mproved access 
to renewable energy through REPP 2  will act as a driver for long- term economic growth in local communities, 
increasing their resilience to climate change. These co -benefits will contribute towards the achievement of 
SDG 1.


---
*Page 58*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 54 
   
 4. Economic co -benefits 
Renewable energy projects have the potential to create employment opportunities, both during development 
and operation and across numerous skill sets, from engineering and construction to sales and customer 
services (co-benefits 1) . As above, first-time access to energy also generates economic co -benefits through 
new opportunities for social and economic development through extended working hours and greater 
efficiency  in the workplace. REPP 2  will work to ensure all jobs created through the projects it supports 
provide decent work, respect labour rights, and improve skills in line with the REPP 2  Environmental and 
Social Policy and Procedures and the ESMS Framework, and supporting the achievement of SDG 8.  
5. Gender -sensitive development co-benefit s (co-benefit 2) 
The positive impacts identified above will specifically  benefit women, since across rural communities in Africa 
in particular women and girls are the primary energy users in the household, responsible for sourcing fuel, 
paying for it and managing its use. REPP 2  will be committed to building capacity on gender  equality  in line 
with the gender action plan of  REPP 2  (see Annex 8). This will be achieved by assisting investee companies 
in establishing and implementing gender action plans . All companies  are required to establish action plans 
which  will h elp to increase awareness  and improve the equality situation at company and project 
implementation level.   
In addition,  a target of 30% of REPP 2  funding will be directed to assist the USD42 billion funding gap female 
entrepreneurs face in Africa in line with 2X GLI criteria. These co -benefits will contribute towards the 
achievement of SDG 5 – Gender Equality.  
 
D.4. Needs of recipient  
6. Vulnerability of the country and beneficiary groups  
Country -specific climate change hazards, vulnerabilities, and impacts include:41 
• Cameroon: Hazards include drought, heavy rainfall events, flooding, heavy winds, high temperatures, 
and heatwaves. Energy infrastructure is vulnerable to precipitation, extreme rainfall and flooding, as well 
as drought, and sea- level rise . Rural electricity access is low, 25% and 26 % of population lives in poverty 
making them specifically vulnerable to climate impacts; 
• DRC: Hazards include intense rainfall, erosion, floods, heatwaves, and seasonal droughts. Reliance on 
the use of traditional biomass significantly increases the climate vulnerability of the population  Rural 
electricity access is extremely  low, 1% and majority (70 %) of population lives in poverty making them 
extremely vulnerable to climate impacts ; 
• Lesotho : Hazards include more frequent, severe droughts and increased flooding. The energy 
infrastructure, particularly hydropower, is highly vulnerable to increasing temperatures. Reliance on 
subsistence agriculture significantly increases the climate vulnerability of the rural population. Rural 
electricity access is low 35% and 32 % of population lives in poverty making them vulnerable to climate 
impacts ; 
• Madagascar:  Hazards include intensification of cyclones and associated flooding, sea- level rise, 
intensification of droughts in the south and west, and a more intense wet season elsewhere. The energy 
sector is vulnerable to a reduction in precipitation and cyclones destroying infrastructure. Over -reliance 
on the use of firewood further increases the level of vulnerability . Rural electricity access is low extremely  
low 11% and majority (81  %) of population lives in poverty making them extremely  y vulnerable to climate 
impacts ;  
• Malawi:  Hazards include floods, droughts, strong winds, intense rainfall, riverine and flash floods and 
tropical cyclones. Vulnerabilities include poverty, rapid population growth, dependence on the agricultural 
sector reliance on rain -fed agriculture and biomass as well as lack of financing and w eak institutional

 capacity to manage climate change . Rural electricity access is extremely low 6.6% and majority ( 70 %)

• Niger:  Hazards include higher temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme events,

8. Absence of alternative sources of financing  
Adoption of renewable energy technologies is a priority under all target country NDCs, with additional funding 
needed to address climate change mitigation and adaption challenges. Despite this, the analysis presented 
in Sections B.1 and B.2 demonstrates th e significant funding and regulatory barriers faced by these countries 
 
42 World Bank,  World Bank Open Data , last accessed 24/09/2022


---
*Page 60*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 56 
   
 to achieve these ambitions. Commercial funding is not readily available , and most investors look to invest in 
more developed and stable markets.  
9. Needs for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity  
REPP 2  contributes to the development of an enabling environment in Host Countries  by supporting 
technology transfer and capacity development of local developers. Through its investments, which are often 
first-of-a-kind, REPP 2  can also support institutional knowledge development in the Host Countries by 
engaging with government stakehol ders – directly or through its investees – to discuss the regulatory 
frameworks and standard project documentation, sharing a financier’s perspective on what is required to 
ensure ‘bankability’ of these documents from an international standard. This could subsequently help  attract 
private sector investors to fund the renewable energy priorities which are key to target country climate 
change responses. Examples include: a) providing a private sector perspective on the current policy and 
regulatory framework for mini- grids in Nigeria to further strengthen the successful and timely implementation 
of the Nigeria Electrification Programme; b) advising the Zambian government on the design of  a micro -
generation programme which would successfully attract private investment; supporting the Government of 
Niger in strengthening private sector engagement in renewable energy development . As it further develops 
its relationship with national stakeholders, Camco will provide advice and support for national stakeholders 
going through the GCF accreditation process  (such assistance is already being discussed in Nigeria  and 
Lesotho).  
D.5. Country ownership  
REPP 2  will place a strong focus on ensuring country ownership of its activities. While it will  apply a bottom -
up approach to its operations, driven by demand from renewable energy project developers and companies, 
the Programme will also place considerable emphasis on the alignment of its investments with national -level 
climate, energy and sustainable development priorities and ensuring that effective consultations with key 
stakeholders (including both government and non- government entities) take place to inform  the 
Programme’s design.  
1. Alignment with and contribution to national priorities and climate strategies  
In 2020, Camco undertook an analysis of the key policies in all REPP Phase 1 countries of operation and 
assessed the extent to which the portfolio was aligned with key national policy targets and priorities. The 
assessment identified a high level of alignment overall.43 It has also helped to improve how REPP Phase 1 
aligns its investment decisions with national policies. The same approach  will be critical to ensuring country 
ownership of REPP 2 .  
REPP 2 ’s diverse technology focus and support for the increase of renewable energy generation capacity 
and diversification of energy systems is strongly aligned with all Host Countries ’ NDCs.  Most of these 
countries also consider renewable energy development as a strong adaptation measure through the 
improvement of energy infrastructure resilience and – by improving livelihoods, with particular attention given 
to women and vulnerable groups - the resilience of local populations.  
Through its investments, REPP 2  will support the numerous national priorities  outlined below. A more 
detailed analysis is provided in Annex 2.  
Cameroon  
• Reduce emissions by 35% by 2030 through increased renewable energy capacity and expanded energy 
access (conditional, Updated NDC).  
• Reduce energy sector’s vulnerability through focus on small -scale and distributed generation (National 
Climate Change Adaptation Plan).
