2. Engagement with NDAs and other stakeholders  
In 2021, Camco began the process of obtaining no objection letters (NOLs) from the respective National 
Designated Authority (NDA) in most of the countries that will be supported through REPP 2  GCF’s 
contribution. This involved introductory letters and calls in which the concept of REPP 2  and its planned 
activities and operational model were explained. The REPP 2  draft Concept Note was shared with key 
stakeholders in February 2022, followed by presentations and/or email exchanges where specific points 
raised were discussed, with feedback integrated into the final version of the Concept Note and this Funding 
Proposal . The Funding Proposal has also been shared and discussed with the NDAs.  The ESMF was shared 
in July 2023.  
As part of the engagement process, Camco consulted respective NDAs and other relevant sectoral 
stakeholders, particularly ministries of energy and rural electrification agencies, virtually and/or in- person (in-
person meetings were held with NDAs and/or NDA advisors of Cameroon, DRC, Lesotho, Madagascar, 
Niger, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia , as well as several sectoral stakeholders). The aim of these 
consultations was  to ensure an alignment of interests and develop a strong basis for continuous engagement  
during the approval process of this Funding proposal  and throughout the REPP 2  operational timeframe, 
which will include annual progress updates and ad hoc engagement as required to discuss specific 
investments. NOLs have been secured from all Host Countries . Additional countries may be added to the 
programme after initial  GCF  Board approval, provided they submit an NOL, and the addition is approved by 
the GCF Board.  In addition to public sector engagement, Camco has engaged with multiple private sector 
stakeholders and civil society representatives. 
Country ownership of REPP 2 , once operational, will be ensured through regular dialogue and long- term 
relationship building with national governments and other relevant national stakeholders (identified by the 
NDAs). This will include:  
• Annual updates to NDAs and sectoral stakeholders on the progress of REPP 2 activities and broader 
discussion on market development;  
• Ad hoc engagement with relevant government agencies, management units of sector support 
programmes, and/or non- governmental institutions and civil society organisations to ensure 
alignment and inform the requirements outlined in the REPP 2  E&S and gender -mainstreaming 
policies;  and 
• Ad hoc engagement as part of investment due diligence (DD) process . 
Once the Programme is operational, REPP 2  will promote its activities internationally and in Host Countries  
through industry associations and its on -the-ground network and partners to ensure local companies in those 
markets are aware of - and have the opportunity to benefit from - the REPP 2  offering.


---
*Page 63*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 59 
   
 Engagement of ultimate beneficiaries at the project site will be led by REPP 2  investees. REPP 2  has a 
responsibility of ensuring all its investees continually engage with all stakeholders identified within the project 
area of influence (both directly and indirectly), to fully understand the shortfalls against its standards and 
international best practice, and identify potential risks, which can effectively be managed. All REPP 2 ’s 
investees will be required to design and effectively implement a Stakeholder  Engagement Plan, as part of 
their ESMS (see REPP 2 ’s Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) in Annex 6 for further 
information).  
3. Suitability of the Accredited Entity  
As a climate and impact fund manager, Camco seeks to deliver on its vision for “A brighter future, today” by 
supporting the clean energy transition in emerging markets. With  local offices and staff in West, East and 
Southern Africa, Camco leverages its on -the-ground presence, sector expertise and more than 30 years of 
experience to deliver value as both the REPP Phase 1 manager and as a GCF Accredited Entity . 
As the REPP Phase 1 manager, Camco has been integral to the programme’s design and implementation  
and is leading the re -design and fundraising activities for REPP 2 . Internally, Camco resources the REPP 
Phase 1 management role separately from delivering its obligations as a GCF Accredited Entity. From a 
governance perspective, this separation of roles and responsibilities is important to maintain independence 
and a f ocus on delivery.  
Camco (as a GCF Accredited Entity), REPP 2  and the GCF, leveraging their respective skills, experience 
and expertise, all seek to achieve meaningful and lasting climate change mitigation and adaptation impacts. 
Camco, which is a signatory to the UN Global Compact, recognises its responsibility and opportunity to 
support both the Paris climate targets and the Sustainable Development Goals. Both remain core objectives 
of our work.  
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness  
1. Rationale for a blended finance approach  
The supply of private (and public) capital is scarce for smaller -scale renewables across sub-Saharan Africa, 
and the limited risk appetite of (particularly) private commercial funders exacerbates this situation. In order 
to deliver REPP 2 ’s targeted mitigation (via renewable energy) and adaptation (via climate resilience ) 
impacts, the finance mobilisation must expand beyond the single grant -based donor approach of REPP 
Phase 1 to the blended finance approach of REPP 2 . This paradigm shift in funding structure is necessary 
to provide finance at scale and assist the targeted countries in achieving their climate goals.   
2. Adequacy and reasonableness  
As outlined in detail in Section B.5, the proposed capital structure for REPP 2  - and the GCF’s position within 
it - have been designed to crowd in additional sources of both public and private capital. The GCF investment 
in the junior equity tranche would also be shared by another investor with these investments mobilising 
additional public and private investment in more senior equity and debt tranches.  
REPP 2  acknowledges that it cannot price for risk on a purely commercial basis. Since small -scale 
developers often struggle to access private capital on terms that leave their projects commercially viable, 
public financing in this space is still required. In this context, funding for REPP 2  will contribute to the 
continued growth and increasing sustainability of commercial renewable energy sectors, as well as the 
delivery of climate mitigation and adaptation impacts in Host Countries .  
On the other hand,  if REPP 2  adopted a purely concessional approach to pricing, it may have the unintended 
consequence of negatively disrupting a nascent market for renewables in Host Countries  crowding out 
private sector funding.  REPP 2 ’s approach to concessionality will thus strive to achieve a considered balance 
between the need for concessionality, in order to build the market and crowd in private sector funding, and


---
*Page 64*
---

D 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 60 
   
 the need for rigour and more commercial pricing approaches, where the market, investor appetite and project 
economics allow for it.  
3. Efficiency and effectiveness  
At the fund level, GCF’s funding would mobilise an additional USD 200 million from public and private sources 
in the same or in more senior tranches of the capital stack. Expressed as a ratio of funding expected to be 
mobilised in more senior tranches (USD150 million), this would represent a funding mobilisation ratio of 
3:1. Including funding expected to be mobilised into the junior equity tranche alongside the GCF, this ratio 
would be 4 :1.  
At the level of investments planned to be made by REPP 2 , the GCF investment would be expected to 
mobilise  total finance (total project costs  less REPP2 investments ) in excess of USD786 million.  This would 
represent an investment leverage ratio (total project costs / GCF funding) of 20:1 .  
REPP 2  will aim to mobilise an additional USD50 million from private investors  into both the equity and senior 
debt tranches. This would represent a private sector leverage ratio  of 1:1. The analysis presented in 
Section B.2 identifies limited access to finance in general, and very limited access to private sector capital 
in particular, as key barriers for the small -scale renewable energy sector in sub- Saharan Africa. Given the 
limited risk appetite from private and commercial funders, especially for smaller -scale renewables where 
funding quantum are lower and the potential for returns relative to the level of costs are constrained, it 
appears unrealistic to target a significantly higher private sector leverage ratio.  
The capital structure of REPP 2  is designed to address these key market failures by catalysing funding into 
the sector. This goal is strongly aligned to the GCF’s strategic objective to catalyse climate finance for 
mitigation and adaptation which is new, additional,  adequate and predictable.  
The indicative REPP 2  pipeline can be used to estimate  cost efficienc ies (total installed project cost, including 
fixed financing costs , per MW of installed capacity ), per technology. In general, cost efficienc ies for small -
scale grid- connected projects and off -grid electrification programmes will be lower  than global benchmarks 
for large utility -scale projects, due to the effects of economies of scale as well as additional costs relating to 
supply chain and logistics, when purchasing equipment in smaller quantities and needing to maintain 
operations over a diverse range of sites, often in rural areas. The higher total installed cost for small -scale 
renewables also reflects the higher cost of capital for such businesses and projects, another financing barrier 
which REP P 2 seeks to overcome. Total installed cost estimates from the REPP 2  indicative pipeline and 
relevant benchmarks are shown below.

Other efficiency and effectiveness benefits of REPP 2  
• For the equity tranche, targeted returns in the base case are appropriate given the experience from 
REPP Phase 1 and relevant market benchmarks.  
• For the GCF and other investors in the junior equity tranche, targeted returns are appropriate  in the 
base case. These returns are aligned with a blended finance approach to mobilise additional public 
and private capital in more senior tranches.  
• For the senior debt tranche, targeted returns in the base case are appropriate given representative 
pricing and the level of protection provided by the blended finance structure of REPP 2 . 
• The estimated cost per tonne of carbon dioxide equivalent (PMF -M core indicator 2) is USD18. 35 
per tCO 2e.47 The lower installed capacity of small- scale projects limits their generation potential and 
consequently the potential to generate emission reductions . In general,  total project costs (capital 
and operating expenditure) are unavoidably high relative to the emission reduction potential of the 
plant.

Replicability  Nascent markets for 
renewable energy are 
characterised by 
limited availability and access to finance, inflexible and high 
costs of finance from 
those willing to provide it, limited skills and experience 
of local developers, 
and untested markets where policy and regulatory 
frameworks are 
unclear, untested or uncertain.  
All of these factors 
limit the potential to 
replicate examples of successful renewable 
energy investments Medium  The target scenario is a situation in which REPP 2  demonstrates the 
successful application of blended finance and innovative financing and support mechanisms in a resilient renewable energy sector.  
Renewable energy markets in Host Countries  and across the region are 
viewed as bankable, with private lenders and investors more willing to 
participate to on more standard 
commercial terms. 
 REPP 2  support for a company or

Sustainability  Lack of early -stage

 Medium  The target scenario is a situation in which off -grid companies can achieve 
scale and long- term sustainability for 
their businesses.  
For on -grid projects the target 
scenario is one in which developers 
and sponsors can attract sufficient volumes of commercial funding, on commercially viable terms and can 
operate the installations in a manner 
which can services debt obligations and genera te commercially 
sustainable returns for investors.  
For the smaller -scale renewable 
energy sector as a whole, the target scenario is one in which the appetite for risk is increased as real and 
perceived risks are addressed and 
the supply of commercial funding is increased, ensuring that the market can opera te on more commercial 
terms without the need for ongoing interventions.  The REPP 2  facility is designed to be 
‘semi- evergreen,’  to ensure that GCF 
and other commercial funding can be repaid. REPP 2 ’s capital stack is 
designed to effectively de-risk 
investments by commercial funders, 
through the provision of a junior equity tranche provided by the GCF, REPP Phase 1 reflows and other 
donors. REPP 2 ’s risk appetite, 
broader risk management 
procedures, project selection, deal structuring, and pricing and portfolio 
management approach are designed to ensure sustainability for invest ors. 
Furthermore, the ongoing support 
provided by REPP 2  will also help to 
build capacity, establish a track record, and attract other finance to achieve sustainability by providing 
growth capital in nascent markets.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
1. Monitoring and  reporting 
Monitoring and reporting will be conducted in accordance with industry best practice, following the CDM standards mentioned above and the guidance 
from Global GHG Accounting and Reporting Standard for the Financial Industry.  The monitoring methodology to estimate ex -ante and to calculate 
ex-post has been presented in the Annex 22B.  
In addition to the arrangements laid out in the Accreditation Master Agreement and the Funded Activity Agreement between Camco and the GCF, 
the implementation of REPP 2  will involve:  
• Monitoring of programme’s key performance indicators (KPIs) as per section E5;  
• Monitoring of key environmental and social (E&S) indicators to ensure project -level compliance with the REPP 2  ESMS Framework, to be 
approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SCSp;  
• Monitor the implementation of gender action plans (GAP) and gender disaggregated data;  
• Monitoring of portfolio companies’ financial performance; and  
• Periodic monitoring and reporting of KPIs and E&S indicators. 
 
REPP 2 ’s monitoring and evaluation (M&E) process and requirements are outlined in the REPP 2  Environmental and Social Policy and Procedures  
and the REPP 2  Operations Manual, to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SCSp. The M&E process will include:  
• Training investees in REPP 2 ’s M&E process and requirements by the time of signing a Term Sheet;  
• Ex-ante estimates of investees’ KPIs included in the Support Agreement;  
• Agreeing a monitoring plan with investees as part of their ESMS, which will be part of the obligations pursuant to the Suppor t Agreement with 
each investee . This will detail the indicators each investee will monitor and report to REPP 2  on quarterly basis ;


---
*Page 85*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 81 
  
  
 • Quarterly monitoring of portfolio companies ’ financial performance;  
• Collecting KPI and E&S data and supporting documents on a quarterly basis with each investee via the REPP 2  Impact Data Management 
System ( presented in Annex 11B ), to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SCSp. The investees will report on site-specific 
data to REPP, with the exception of SHS companies, which will report country specific numbers. This allows aggregation of monitored data 
and indicators by investee, country and technology. An annual investee review  which is used to assess the fitness of investees ESMS 
implementation and to verify KPIs reported. This review is conducted by REPP 2  Manager.  
 
REPP 2  will provide KPI updates to the AIFM, REPP 2  Investment Committee and REPP 2  Advisory Board on a quarterly basis and a full M&E report 
in the form of a logframe on an annual basis.  
An important aspect of REPP 2 ’s risk management process and impact verification will be the annual review of investments . The annual review will 
assess fitness of ESMS implementation and verify the reported impact indicators. The annual reviews will be based on document reviews ( As an 
example, the review will revive customer records to verify number of customers and employment records and contracts to verify  number of employees 
reported. The records will be provided by the investee company and reviewed by R EPP managers impact team. If the review raises any 
discrepancies, concerns or material issues a site visit is conducted.) and interviews and will measure, compare and analyse a project’s performance 
against the baseline and targets set during the due diligence process. This will include review of investees GAP against the set targets. If substantial 
material issues are discovered, a site visit may be conducted as an additional part of the annual review. The results of the annual review will confirm 
whether support can be continued in its current form, if modifications of the REPP 2  support are needed, or if withdrawal of REPP 2  support is 
required.  
2. Evaluation  
REPP 2 ’s evaluation process is outlined in the Independent Evaluation of Camco Investment Mandates policy.  The evaluation is an independent 
review commissioned by REPP 2  halfway through the investment period (mid- term review) as well as at the end of the investment mandate (final 
review) to ensure activities compliance with Camco 2 policies and procedures. An open tender procurement procedure will be followed to select the 
appropriate service provider to conduct the independent evaluation.  Evaluations will be conducted based on following principles:  
• Independence:  Evaluations must be conducted independently from the delivery and management of financial assistance and project 
implementation.  
• Credibility:  Evaluations must be credible and based on reliable data and observations. Evaluation reports should reflect consistency and 
dependability in data, findings and lessons learned, with reference to the quality of the procedures and analysis used to col lect a nd interpret 
information.  
• Impartiality : Evaluations must give a comprehensive and balanced presentation of the strengths and weaknesses of the activity being 
evaluated. The evaluation process should reflect impartiality at all stages and consider the views of all stakeholders.


---
*Page 86*
---

E 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 82 
  
  
 • Transparency:  Transparency involves clear communication on the criteria applied, the evaluation approach and methods, and the intended 
use of the findings. Documentation related to evaluations must be freely available,  easily  accessible and readable for transparency and 
legitimacy. 
• Integrity:  Evaluators must honour the dignity, well -being and self -worth of individuals and acknowledge the influence of culture within and 
across groups. Evaluators must respect the right of institutions and individuals to provide information on the facts confident ially.  
• Gender equity : Evaluations must assess whether – and if so, how - men and women are affected by changes to natural resource use and 
decision- making resulting from the funded activity.  
• Competencies and capacities:  Evaluations require a range of expertise that may be technical, environmental, cultural, ethical or within a 
social science. Evaluators must have sufficient experience and skills in the appropriate fields.  
3. Evaluation reporting of Camco (Accredited Entity) to GCF  
Camco will provide the GCF with:  
• Annual activity performance reports on the status of the Programme throughout the relevant reporting period;  
• A mid -term evaluation report at the midpoint of the implementation period of the Programme; and  
• A final evaluation report at the end of the implementation period of the Programme as per the funded activity agreement.

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 83 
   
 F. RISK  ASSESSMENT  AND  MANAGEMENT  
F.1.  Risk factors and mitigations measures  
Selected Risk Factor 1  Co-Financing  Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Medium  High  
Description  
The risk that  an insufficient  amount of co- financing is raised  to meet GCF co- financing requirements 
or deliver the targeted impacts for REPP 2 . There is an additional risk that the terms on whic h co-
financing can be secure d, particularly in the current high- inflation, rising interest -rate environment,  
limit the potential for  REPP 2  to be commercially viable  and deliver targeted impacts and returns for 
investors . 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Fundraising efforts on behalf of REPP 2  are ongoing and at an advanced stage  
• In the event that the amount of co-financing raised is below the targeted level, the capital structure 
for REPP 2  is sufficiently flexible to ensure that all GCF requirements in terms of co- funding ratios 
will be met 
• The REPP 2  financial model incorporates an expectation of higher pricing and return expectations 
in light of current macro -economic trends  
• Discussions are ongoing with potential investors (public and private) to secure financing on terms 
which reflect the REPP 2  mandate and goal to build a more viable market for small -scale 
renewables in Africa – particularly when macroeconomic conditions exacerbate existing barriers 
such as access to finance.  
Mitigation measures are expected to manage and reduce the probability of occurrence of this risk 
factor.  
Selected Risk Factor 2 Investment Performance Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
The risk that assets in the REPP 2  portfolio underperform investment expectations and that targeted 
returns for investors are not achieved as a result.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Robust asset selection processes in accordance with defined investment criteria, including 
REPP 2  IC approval  
• Comprehensive risk assessment and due diligence procedures in line with REPP 2  risk 
management policies and procedures  
• Ongoing assessment of risk management framework and procedures as well as operations and maintenance at asset level  
• REPP 2  oversight and input on key strategic decision- making for equity investees through 
representation on project company boards  
• Ongoing monitoring of asset performance and revenue- drivers  
• Ongoing communication with investees, combined with annual review cycle for investments, including site visits


---
*Page 88*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 84 
   
 Mitigation measures are expected to manage and reduce the probability of occurrence of this risk 
factor.  
Selected Risk Factor 3 Developer / Investee Performance Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Project developer or investee company management fail to execute on company growth plans (off-
grid investments), achieve financial close or complete construction, achieve commercial operations 
within targeted timeframes and against agreed budgets.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Milestone- based disbursement of funds linked to achievement of pre -agreed growth targets (for 
off-grid investments) and pre -defined contractual obligations (for development -stage funding and 
construction of grid- connected projects)  
• Ongoing communication, oversight and review of investment progress, including via company 
board representation where an equity investment has been made  
• Support and capacity building to company management and local project developers  
• Support and capacity building (as appropriate) to regulators and other government institutions 
crucial for project or business development  
The milestone -based disbursement approach is expected to reduce the risk of REPP 2  losses. 
Capacity building initiatives are expected to help to build institutional, regulatory and business skills 
and experience, which reduce the risk of project / investment failure and enhance potential for 
commercial viability and long- term sustainabi lity. 
Selected Risk Factor 4 Exit / Refinancing Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Risk that REPP 2  is unable to exit or refinance a transaction according to original expectations  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Explicit consideration of exit and refinancing plan as part of all investment proposals, with REPP 
2 IC approval  
• Pricing and deal structuring approach aligned to market benchmarks wherever commercially possible 
• Refinancing plans agreed and approved by refinancing parties (including approvals, wherever possible) before disbursement  
• Active portfolio and asset management with dedicated role for this purpose  
• Ongoing oversight and review of performance at an asset and portfolio level to maximise profitable exit, sell -down and refinancing opportunities  
Mitigation measures described above are expected to reduce the probability of occurrence of this 
risk factor.

 Selected Risk Factor 5 Conflict of Interest Risk 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Medium  Low 
Description  
The risk that a conflict of interest occurs for Camco between its obligations as the manager of REPP 
2 and its obligations as Accredited Entity on behalf of the GCF. 
Similar potential for conflict of interest in terms of management services rendered to any other platform 
by Camco and the REPP 2  platform, particularly where these have the potential to result in losses to 
investors participating in REPP 2  as well as one or more other facilities managed by Camco.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Camco maintains a comprehensive Conflicts of Interest Policy, governing the risks outlined above 
• Where a potential conflict is identified, appropriate measures will be implemented to manage and 
mitigate the conflict  
• The REPP 2  Conflicts of Interest of Policy will b e approved by its the general partner of Camco 
REPP SCSp and supervised by the Advisory Board and AIFM, once constituted and maintained on an ongoing basis  
• Camco, as fund manager, will appoint and maintain an independent REPP 2  coordinator and 
(separately) a GCF coordinator  
• Camco REPP SCSp , as Executing Entity, will be supervised by the AIFM who will ensure 
compliance with the Luxembourg Law of 10 August 195 on Commercial Companies  
The measures outlined above are expected to reduce the probability of occurrence and the potential 
impact of this risk factor . 
Selected Risk Factor 6 General Operational Risk 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Technical and operational  Low Medium  
Description  
Risk of loss or other damage resulting from insufficient or failed policies, processes and procedures, 
or a failure to implement and maintain existing ones . 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Develop detailed and comprehensive policies, processes and procedures covering all 2 REPP 
activities and maintain these on an ongoing basis  
• Ensure that policies, processes and procedures are maintained and recorded in the REPP 2  
Operations Manual (Annex 21)  
• Provide ongoing training to REPP 2  staff, IC and  Advisory  Board  
• Ensure systems and IT infrastructure are appropriate to meet the operational, monitoring and reporting needs of REPP 2  on an ongoing basis  
• Minimise key person dependencies and maintain a detailed business continuity plan for REPP 2  
• Ensure appropriate segregation of duties amongst REPP 2  staff.

 Selected Risk Factor 7 Offtaker Credit Risk (grid- connected projects)  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Credit  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Risk of payment delay or default associated with utility offtakers for grid- connected projects . 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Rigorous analysis credit risk during project selection and deal structuring with approval provided 
by the REPP 2  IC. 
• Structure and ensure both innovative and traditional forms of credit risk mitigation for payment liquidity and default, sourced from partner organisations which provide risk mitigation products.  
Mitigation measures can minimise and transfer offtaker credit risk to parties which are better -
positioned to manage it. Where cover is purchased, this provided effective risk mitigation to both 
project companies and REPP 2  investees.  
Selected Risk Factor 8 Demand Risk (off -grid companies)  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Credit  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Risk that estimates of demand and revenue growth achieved for off -grid companies are lower than 
initially projected, resulting in delays or failure to achieve growth targets and become sustainable. 
Persistent failure to grow demand and revenue will lead to  below -par returns and losses for REPP 2  
in a worst -case scenario.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Ensure rigorous assessment is undertaken by the off -grid company of customer ability - and 
willingness -to-pay, prior to investment decision 
• Ensure that drivers of demand and revenue growth are well -understood and that growth 
projections are realistic  
• Seek additional / alternative revenue sources to meet projected payment obligations, through 
product and business model innovation, productive use of energy applications, securing anchor 
consumers to boost revenues, etc.  
• Provide capacity building support for off -grid companies to develop skills and expertise in 
estimating future demand and managing demand / revenue growth risk  
• Provide strategic oversight and input (via board positions) to ensure costs are managed carefully 
and that business is focused appropriately on demand and revenue growth 
• Provide support for development and implementation of policy and regulatory frameworks and 
well as donor support programmes which provide tariff or result -based subsidies for off -grid 
electrification in Host Countries  
Mitigation measures can be effective in reducing demand risk.


---
*Page 91*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 87 
   
 Selected Risk Factor 9 Currency Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Forex  High Medium  
Description  
Where mismatches exist between revenues received by investees or borrowers in local currency and 
their hard currency funding obligations, there is a risk that they will be unable to meet those obligations 
(in part or in full) as a result of currency devaluation. The consequences for REPP 2  may include a 
failure to achieve targeted returns for its investors and, in a worst -case scenario, financial losses at 
the portfolio level. The risk is exacerbated by the high cost and lack of liquidity of hedging for eme rging 
market currencies, especially in comparison to the lower revenues generated by smaller renewable energy projects.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• In general – and particularly for grid- connected projects – REPP 2  will invest and provide loans 
denominated in hard currency, effectively transferring currency risk to investee companies and 
borrowers. For grid- connected projects, where project companies can negotiate power purchase 
agreements denominated in hard currency, this risk can be passed on to the offtaker. Where the PPA is denominated in local currency or where off -grid companies generate revenues in local 
currency, currency risk lies with the investee / borrower. 
• In rate cases where project economics allow, currency risk may be reduced through FX hedging, with hedging costs passed on to borrowers / investees.  
• REPP 2  will typically only consider offering investments in local currency where the investment or 
project can afford the cost of FX hedging or where alternative mechanisms to facilitate local 
currency lending are available (such as on- lending facilities via local financing partners).  
• REPP 2  may also consider implementation of portfolio hedging solutions, effectively hedging FX 
risk for investors and a reduction in the costs of hedging for individual investees or borrowers through portfolio aggregation and scale. 
Selected Risk Factor 10  Currency Transfer and Inconvertibility Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Forex  Medium  Medium  
Description  
In relation to currency risk, the additional risk that funds received by companies in local currency in a 
host country cannot be converted into foreign currency and transferred internationally in a timely 
manner. Delays in this process may result in losses  for the investee or borrower due to currency 
movements, breach of contractual obligations. For REPP 2 , the result may be lower -than-expected 
returns and losses in a worst -case scenario.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Secure government guarantees or assurance regarding access to foreign currency and transfer procedures in Host Countries  
• Where available, secure transfer and inconvertibility protection as part of a political risk insurance package 
• Work with local authorities (including central banks) to develop, implement and familiarise local 
staff procedures for the processing (preparation, approval, etc.) of currency conversion and 
transfer requests  
The measures can reduce the probability of occurrence of delays and losses.


---
*Page 92*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 88 
   
 Selected Risk Factor 1 1 Environmental and Social Safeguards  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Reputational Low High 
Description  
The risk that environmental and social safeguards (ESS) fail to address relevant environmental and 
social risks, or that failures in the implementation of those safeguards lead to negative and / or 
unintended environmental or social impacts.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Development of an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) framework at the 
facility level, as well as an ESMS and Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP) at investee 
level. All related policies and procedures to be in accordance with internat ional best practice 
standards as well as REPP 2  and GCF policies  
• Preparation of detailed environmental and social impact assessments for each Invested by 
qualified independent consultants  
• Careful assessment of environmental and social risk throughout the transaction approval process 
in accordance with the REPP 2  Environmental and Social Policy and ESMS Framework, from pre -
selection through to approval by the REPP 2  IC of a transaction funding proposal  
• Undertaking careful due diligence, including a site visit prior to execution and disbursement of 
funds  
• Extensive and ongoing stakeholder engagement at investee level – particularly for vulnerable 
people such as women, children and the elderly  
• Extensive and ongoing training and capacity building for developers as well as management and 
staff of off- grid companies to build skills and expertise in relation to managing environmental and 
social risk  
• Engagement and training provided to institutions in Host Countries  responsible for the oversight 
and compliance with national policy, to develop and implement environmental and social 
safeguards and policy requirements which are appropriate for the scale and risk associated with 
each technology asset type 
• Early -stage development funding to prepare project level ESIAs and ESMS  
• Ongoing monitoring, reporting and evaluation on environmental and social risks during 
construction and once plants become operational in accordance with the Section E. 
The measures described above can adequately minimise and manage environmental and social risks 
and the implementation of environmental and social safeguards.  
Selected Risk Factor 12 Forced and child labour  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Reputational Medium  High 
Description  
There is a risk that forced and / or child labour is used  in the supply chain of silicon- based solar panels 
solar and lithium -ion-batteries.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• REPP 2  is committed to following the conventions  of the International Labour Organisation (“ILO ”), 
as well as the IFC P erformance Standards with regard to forced labour and child labour.  
• REPP 2 will adhere to GCF environmental and social policy and local relevant laws in terms of the 
prohibition of forced labour in any form, including slavery and human trafficking.  
• REPP 2  takes a firm stand against all forms of forced labour and expects the same of its primary 
suppliers, partners, contractors and subcontractors.


---
*Page 93*
---

F 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 89 
   
 • REPP 2  will not tolerate child labour under circumstances. A clear distinction between child labour 
and utilisation of young worker s shall be established in accordance with  internationally -recognized 
standards. Only people who are 18 years or older  will be permitted to be employed on REPP 2  
funded projects. These commitments will be absorbed by all REPP 2  investees and projects 
funded under REPP 2  through the funding agreements which each investee. 
• REPP 2  investees will be required to prepare, implement and maintain a clear and transparent 
Supply Chain Procurement Plan (“SCPP”) , setting out the measures which must be taken to 
prevent forced and child labour. The SCPP should clarify that the policies preventing forced and  
child labour  apply to all subsidiaries of a company and their respective supply chains. These 
policies will also stipulate any training needs of human resource and/or procurement personnel 
on how to identify forced labour in practice and seek appropriate remedies  where it is identified. 
• REPP 2  and the  AE will participate in and contribute to the e stablishment  of supplier code of 
conduct principles , covering compliance with international labour and human rights laws, including 
the prohibition of forced and child labour, and occupational health and safety.  
• REPP 2  will d evelop, implement and maintain supply chain risk assessment procedures and a 
due diligence screening mechanism to mitigate the risk of forced and child labour . These 
procedures will be implemented in the project approval, due diligence and review procedures 
which are applicable to all  invest ments .

Selected Risk Factor 15 Policy and Regulatory Risk 
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Risk of a failure to align or comply with host country policy and regulatory requirements, resulting in 
delays during project development or a failure to obtain necessary permits and licences to proceed 
with construction or begin commercial operations. Additional risks to project or business development 
associated with policy and regulatory uncertainty, including gaps in policy and regulatory frameworks as well as slow or inconsistent application of policies and regulations.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Detailed analysis of policy and regulatory frameworks in all Host Countries  conducted on an 
ongoing basis, informed through engagement with government counterparties and their 
development partners.  
• Careful assessment of risks posed by policy or regulatory uncertainty to project or business 
development, construction or operations – prior to decision to invest, with a clear mitigation plan 
if the investment is made  
• The broad scope of REPP 2  mandate (SHS, mini/isolated grids, on- grid) will allow it to select 
investments where policy and regulatory risk is acceptable, while continuing to monitor the 
enabling environment for other market segment to position its future investments.  
• Ongoing monitoring of policy and regulatory risks throughout the investment lifecycle  
• Training and skills development for business owners and project companies to manage policy 
and regulatory uncertainty  
• Targeted interventions within Host Countries  to support development and implementation of 
policies and regulations, where Camco and REPP 2  experience can add value.  
Mitigation measures are designed to reduce policy and regulatory uncertainty, which contributes to 
broader market development.

Selected Risk Factor 1 7 Climate Risk  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Risk that funding provided by REPP 2 fails to deliver the intended climate change mitigation and 
adaptation / climate resilience impacts. Related risks include resource risk and reputational risk to 
REPP 2 and its investors where unforeseen climate change impacts to infrastructure as well as 
vulnerable people and communities result in unintended consequences, including reduced climate 
resilience and maladaptation. 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
• Incorporate comprehensive climate risk assessment in the REPP 2 Risk Management Policy and 
Procedures, to be approved by the REPP 2 
• Internal (desktop) climate risk assessment to be conducted prior to investment approved by the REPP 2 IC and confirmed during due diligence, including via site visit  
• Climate risk assessment to be conducted as part of all ESIAs to assess the impacts of climate change in aggravating environmental and social risk  
• Appropriate levels of ongoing monitoring, reporting and evaluation of climate risk for all assets in the REPP 2 portfolio 
• Training and skills development for off- grid companies and project companies in identification, 
assessment and management of climate risk (to infrastructure, environment as well as vulnerable people and communities). Particularly attention to be focused on avoiding maladaptation.  
REPP 2 interventions are designed to contribute to efforts by Host Countries  to mitigate and adapt to 
climate change, building climate resilience for energy infrastructure and for vulnerable people and 
communities in the process. Mitigation measures identified here can enhance the potential for positive climate change impacts and minimise potential for unintended outcomes such as maladaptation.

 F. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1.  Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)   
An Environmental and Social Policy and Procedures (E&S Policy) and an Environmental and Social 
Management Framework (Framework) have been developed for REPP 2 to manage the environmental and 
social (E&S) impacts and risks of investments made by REPP 2. The  E&S Policy and Framework are in line 
with IFC Performance Standards, and the sustainability principles advocated by the UN Global Compact. 
The Framework is presented in Annex 6.  
E&S risk and impacts of smaller renewable energy projects funded by REPP 2 are anticipated to be limited. 
The magnitude of these impacts is expected to be low and contained within the footprint of the projects. The 
impacts should be largely reversible and readily mitigated through the mitigation measures implemented by investees as identified in their project -specific Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS). 
REPP 2 will not support investments if material negative E&S impacts cannot be satisfactorily mitigated.  
The E&S risk screening process for REPP 2 (elaborated in the Framework) is:  
• Step 1 : The initial E&S risk screening occurs prior to investment approval and involves a Risk 
Assessment Tool and an E&S Initial Screening Checklist to pre- define risk category and eligibility of 
the project;  
• Step 2 : An E&S onboarding with the investee company takes place if the project is approved for 
further consideration and once the initial E&S risk screening has been completed to ensure the company understands E&S requirements and processes  for REPP 2 ; 
• 
Step 3 : An E&S Gap Analysis  (red flag report ) will be completed by the REPP 2 Manager. This will 
entail a review of the project’s documentation (i.e., ESIA and ESMS to the extent that it is available). 
In case of early -stage growth and development funding, the funding may be used for the development 
of the ESIA or the ESMS. In this case REPP 2  will assist in reviewing the Terms of Reference for 
EIAS/ESMS or GAP work and the selection of consultants. In addition, key milestones for 
disbursement of early -stage growth and development funding are agreed; and    
• Step 4: Once the ESIA and ESMS have been finalised, REPP 2  conducts a due diligence site visit 
to verify E&S risks. E&S risks outlined in the ESIA, such as identification of indigenous peoples, 
potential or necessary resettlement, security risks, protected biodiversity impacts and human rights assessments, will be  highlighted in the E&S Red Flag Report, and the extent of the risk will be verified 
during the due diligence trip. The project risk categorisation will also be reassessed to ensure that all project activities align within their allocated category. All pro jects funded by REPP 2  will undertake a 
due diligence trip. The due diligence trip will also serve to assess the investee’s internal E&S 
resources, competence and aptitude for managing E&S risks in accordance with REPP 2  E&S 
standards.  
REPP 2 will be responsible for the implementation of the E&S Policy and the Framework and shall apply an E&S risk categorisation to each potential investment. REPP 2 will have a dedicated impact team which will 
review each individual investment’s E&S risk,  ensure the sufficient quality of the ESIA and the ESMS, assess 
the investee’s capacity and competency to manage E&S risk, and assist them with capacity building, if 
required. The roles and responsibilities related to the implementation of the Framework are described in greater detail in Annex 6.  
The monitoring of REPP 2 will involve:  
• Monitoring of key performance indicators;  
• Monitoring of key E&S indicators to ensure project -level compliance with the Framework;  
• Periodic reporting; and  
• Annual investee performance reviews to assess the fitness of its ESMS.


---
*Page 98*
---

G 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 94 
   
 The monitoring and reporting obligation s will be included in each  Investment Agreement between REPP 2 
and the investee, who will submit monitoring reports through an impact data management system on a 
quarterly basis. Reports will be reviewed by REPP 2, and corrective action measures discussed with the 
investee where relevant.   
In its capacity as an AE, Camco has been undertaking extensive stakeholder engagement with NDAs, 
energy ministries, agencies and regulators, as well as the private sector and relevant NGOs. This 
engagement will continue throughout the Programme’s implement ation. All REPP 2 investees will be 
required to conduct appropriate site- based stakeholder consultations with communities and project 
beneficiaries in line with the Framework. Stakeholder engagement outcomes and strategy are further 
outlined in Section D5 and Annex 7.  
REPP 2 will require that a grievance mechanism for stakeholders is established to receive and facilitate 
resolution of affected communities’ concerns and grievances about a project’s E&S performance. The 
process s hall be easy, accessible and free of charge, and conducted  in a confidential manner should this be 
preferred. The investee will provide guidelines on how grievances, claims or suggestions from the community 
are promptly managed and effectively resolved to all stakeholders (inclusive of employees, c ommunity 
members, service providers, and suppliers). In addition, Camco in its capacity as an AE, provides a whistle -
blower  hotline and grievance form on its website that will allow submission of grievances in an anonymous 
manner.  
REPP 2 will recognise the critical importance of respecting the rights of Indigenous Peoples and of avoiding 
any adverse impacts on them. The Framework includes an Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP), which will be applicable in all REPP 2 investments where Indigenous Peoples are present and/or have or had collective 
attachment or rights to the land. The IPP has been guided by the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous, 
IFC Environmental and Social Performance Standards 7, and the Green Climate Fund Indigenous People Policy and is presented in Annex 6. As per the IPP, a stakeholder engagement will take place with Indigenous 
Peoples, and in the case of negative impacts the principle of free, prior and informed consent will be applied.
 
G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)   
1. Gender assessment  
A gender assessment for REPP 2  was undertaken to explore the relationships between men and women, 
their access to resources and the constraints they face relative to one another in REPP 2 Host Countries 
within the context of climate change and energy access.  
The REPP 2 investment mandate will extend towards some of the most economically and socially vulnerable 
countries in Africa, with the majority (>50%) of the population living below the International Poverty Line in more than half of the Host Countries . However, there is some variation in how REPP 2 Host Countries  rank 
in terms of different gender indexes, as outlined below:  
• Gender Development Index (GDI), which focuses on health, education and economic resources: 
Two-thirds of the countries show low levels of gender equity , whereas Lesotho, Madagascar and 
Malawi all score higher on GDI value, approaching or exceeding the world average.  
• Gender Inequality Index (GII), which measures gender inequalities in reproductive health, 
empowerment and the labour force: All Host Countries  experience higher level of gender inequality 
than the world average and DRC, Niger, Nigeria and Sierra Leone are all below the sub-Saharan 
African average.  
• Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI), which measures gender parity across economic participation 
and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment.53 
 
53 World Economic Forum (WEF), 2022. Global Gender Gap Report 2022 .


---
*Page 99*
---

G 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 95 
   
 Compared with the sub-Saharan Africa as a whole (which ranks in the middle ahead of Middle East 
and North Africa, and South Asia), Cameroon, Lesotho and Madagascar rank above average, while 
most Host Countries  remain on the lower spectrum on gender equity  values.  
According to a 2022 World Bank et al  report on progress towards SDG 7, sub -Saharan Africa remained the 
least electrified region in 2020. While availability of country -level gender -disaggregated data on electricity 
access in the region is limited, thereby making it difficult to assess how this lack of electricity access relates to gender, research by IRENA has shown that women- owned households and enterprises find it harder to 
access modern energy.
54 
While regional and country -level gender -disaggregated data for the energy sector remains scarce, research 
at the global level shows that the share of women employed in the renewable energy sector (32%) is higher than in the oil and gas industry (22%).
55 However, the share of female employees across sub- Saharan Africa 
is substantially higher in non- technical jobs (30 -38%) than in STEM jobs (13%). While women’s participation 
in company leadership globally is substantially lower than men, it is positive to see that there are more 
women on the boards of renewable energy companies in Africa (27%) than on boards in general (Africa’s 
average is 18%).  
At the same time, female renewable energy entrepreneurs are struggling to access the financing and 
business support they need to grow their companies. Although Africa has a  higher share of female- owned 
businesses  than anywhere else - which demonstrates the active and important role women play in the 
continent’s commercial sector - the African Development Bank has estimated that there is a USD42 billion 
financing gap for African women entrepreneurs across business value chains.56 
2. Gender action plan (GAP)  
Access to energy can help individuals, households and business to complete certain time- consuming tasks 
in more efficient manner. Access to electricity may free up time from fuel collection and household tasks to 
enable longer periods of study time or other productive use of time, and thereby decreas e gender gaps in 
key educational and economic areas. A dditionally, access to energy, for example  access to light ing, 
information technologies or connectivity , are linked to  an increase in safety and security  and the perception 
of personal wellbeing.57 Further, in REPP 2 we believe that the funding of women -led and focused 
businesses , empower s women to invest and build companies, teams, products and markets that will produce  
transformati on change for generations to come. This transformational change, driven by gender focused 
financing, is crucial to overcom ing the gender equality gap.  
The REPP 2 GAP includes two main actions that work towards bridging the gender equity  gap:  
Action 1: Integrate a gender equity perspective into the design, implementation, monitoring and 
assessment of all renewable energy activities financed by REPP 2. This means requesting all investees 
to put in place sub- project level GAPs (Activity 9: Non -financial support to promote gender equity) with 
actionable targets to improve gender equality within their operations and project implementation will 
positively im pact the industry and its beneficiaries.  This involves:   
1. Analysing gender equality  in the investee company, country of operation, and targeted sector ;  
 
54 IRENA, 2022. Renewable Energy Market Analysis. Africa and Its Regions . 
55 AfDB and UN Women, 2021. Green Jobs for Women in Africa; IRENA, 2020. Global Landscape of Renewable Energy 
Finance 2020 . 
56Global Entrepreneurship Monitoring. 2018/2019 Women’s Entrepreneurship Report . AfD B,2017. Unleashing women's 
entrepreneurship through strategic partnerships . 
 
57 Supported by an initial project -level data collection conducted in Goma, DRC:

 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 96 
   
 2. Establishing an investee -specific GAP by identifying gender performance indicators and gender -
disaggregated targets against an established baseline; and 
3. Integrating the outcomes of (1) and (2) into a monitoring plan. 
 
REPP 2  will provide investee companies with supporting in assessing gender baseline and 
establishing action plan. Such support includes:  
i. Gender equality toolkit for renewable energy companies - an example, provided via the 
REPP 2 gender toolkit ;  
ii. Gender mainstreaming training provided in form of webinars and REPP Phase 1 A cademy 
(i.e. closed training provided to REPP investee only - as an example can be provided 
previous webinars  and REPP Phase 1 Academy training (see appendix 2);  and 
iii. Individual support to investee from the REPP 2 Manager’s impact team in from of review 
of gender baseline and action plan. 
Action 2: Dedicate a target of 30% of all funding towards companies that meet 2X GLI criteria.  These 
are companies that:  
• Are founded by a woman or women or are majority -owned by women; and / or  
• Have at least 30% women in senior management; and / or  
• Provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women; and / or  
• Have a workforce that is at least 30% women, together with a progressive human resource policy . 
The funding can be  in from of early -stage growth funding for off-grid companies ( Activity 1), development -
stage funding for grid- connected projects  (activity 2), finance for expansion of SHS businesses ( Activity 3), 
finance for construction of mini -grid portfolios (activity 4) or gap funding for construction of grid- connected 
projects  ( Activity 5)  and can be in from of hybrid equity instruments  or as  senior or subordinated loans, 
subject to the company’s funding needs.  
These actions are anticipated to lead to the following impacts:  
• Total female beneficiaries reached : 290,875  
• Total female beneficiaries adopting improved livelihoods and/or new climate resilient livelihood 
options, through resilient PUE options : 8,295  
• Promotion of gender equity  within renewable energy development companies by working towards 
investing USD7 0 million in companies meeting the 2X GLI criteria. 
• Achievement of numerous co- benefits, including 23,040 female jobs generated, improved lighting 
1.12 million women and girls, improved access to information for 547,687 women and girls , and 26 
developers with improved ESG practices (including GAPs).  
 
The detailed gender assessment and action plan are presented in Annex 8.  
G.3.  Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
REPP 2 will adopt and build upon the policy framework, governance and system architecture that REPP 
Phase 1 has implemented and refined since its inception in 2015. Where necessary, this will be expanded 
and further refined to align with the GCF’s governance standards and will be overseen by Camco in its role 
as Accredited Entity.  
REPP 2 will produce an annual business plan outlining investment and portfolio targets for the year, as well 
as an operational budget produced on an activity basis. This will allow the budget -setting committee to 
determine the activities required to execut e the strategy for the coming year, leaving REPP 2 to effectively


---
*Page 101*
---

G 
 GREE N CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 97 
   
 undertake the activities and deliver value within the budgetary constraints set. The business plan will also 
summarise the previous year’s performance and any actions resulting from analysis of the results.  
REPP 2 will prepare monthly portfolio reports that include:  
• Pipeline, deal -flow and progress against origination targets for the platform 
• Deals in documentation and updates on transactions as they move closer to being contracted and 
ready to disburse  
• Portfolio valuation and movements in disbursements, accrued income, revaluations, impairments and 
disposals  
• Metrics showing portfolio weightings and performance against targets and risk thresholds, as 
required (e.g., technology, country, off -taker etc.)  
 
The portfolio reports will allow effective monitoring of the platform’s performance by the governing 
committees and stakeholders, ensuring corrective action is taken and assisting in developing strategy which 
steers the platform towards its long- term objec tives. 
REPP 2 will also prepare quarterly management accounts, comprising a profit and loss account, balance 
sheet and cashflow statement. These will be prepared in accordance with the relevant Generally Accepted 
Accounting Principles, which are the same accounting standards under which the audited annual report and 
financial statements will be prepared.  
Accompanying the Cashflow Statement will be a Cash Forecast, prepared quarterly and providing a detailed 
overview of the platform’s liquidity requirements during the investment period. It will also include a forecast 
of cash returns from the portfolio over  the life of the platform.  
REPP 2 will engage a firm of accountants to undertake an external audit of the platform’s annual financial 
statements. This will confirm independently that the financial statements provide a true and fair view and 
provide transparency to governing committe es and stakeholders, highlighting areas of importance and 
anything which may require attention.  
REPP 2 will open and control bank accounts into which it will receive funds and make disbursements. The 
bank accounts will be reconciled in the REPP 2 accounting system daily or on an ad -hoc basis.  
Disbursements will be controlled through a process that ensures all appropriate milestones have been 
completed. For example:  
• Conditions prior to disbursement in applicable finance agreements have been met  
• For payments to suppliers or service providers, service level agreements or valid contracts exist 
between the platform and beneficiary  
• Projects have fulfilled monitoring obligations and investees are compliant with any platform policies 
and training requirements  
• Beneficiaries have undergone appropriate KYC / AML and sanctions checks  
Cash controls will be established which include dual authority for setting up and approving payments, and 
which have payment limits for approving transactions of different sizes. Material transactions will require 
sign-off from the Depositary Agent . 
REPP 2 will adopt and apply the Accredited Entity's approved procurement policy, which is already in use 
by REPP Phase 1. The policy will be applied throughout the Programme’s activities. Please refer to Annex 
10: Procurement Policy & Plan for further details.
