 Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment  of 
FP222 
Proposal name:  Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP 2)  
Accredited entity:  Camco Management Limited  (CAMCO)  
Country/(ies):  Cameroon, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Lesotho, 
Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia  
Project/programme size:  Medium  
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
Note: This assessment report is based on a review of the updated and revised proposal received 
from the accredited  entity  (AE) for the thirty -seventh meeting of the Board (B.37). It follows the 
assessment report based on a n earlier version of the funding proposal submitted by the AE for the 
thirty -sixth meeting of the Board ( B.36),  during which it had not  been  endorsed by the independent 
Technical Advisory Panel ( TAP) . This revised a ssessment report reflects the updates and revisions 
the AE has implemented in response to  the earlier  independent TAP  assessment report  for B.36. It 
also reflects the clarification discussions held between the AE, the Secretariat and  the independent 
TAP on the side  lines of B.36  in Songdo , Korea , and the two follow -up video s calls between the 
parties on 9 August 2023  and 4 September 2023.  
1.1 Overview   
1. In its assessment , the  independent TAP  is guided by d ecision B.33/12  of the GCF Board , 
which explains the principles for demonstrating the impact potential of GCF -supported 
activities as well as other appraisal and assessment guidance in considering these  investment 
criteria and as endorsed by the GCF Board.  
2. The Renewable Energy Performance Platform 2 ( REPP 2 ; the fund)  is a new USD 250  
million  blended finance fund  established  as a société en commandite spéciale  (SCSp) or special 
limited partnership  in Luxembo urg entitled Camco REPP SCSp  and promoted by Camco 
Management Limited  (CAMCO ). The main purpose of the fund is to invest in  the early -stage 
development and construction of on-grid as well as off -grid small -scale renewable energy 
projects in sub-Saharan  Africa , with a focus on least  developed countries . The current list of 
target markets for REPP 2  includes Cameroon, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Lesotho, 
Madagascar, Malawi, the Niger, Nigeria,  Sierra Leone, and Zambia.  This list may grow, subject to 
further no-objection letters that the AE may receive from additional African countries.   
3. REPP 2 builds on a predecessor fund , the Renewable Energy Performance Platform 1 
(REPP 1) , which  CAMCO  has been implementing since 2015 with a commitment of 88 million 
pounds sterling (GBP) in grant funding from the Foreign, Commonwealth and Development 
Office (FCDO)  of the United Kingdom  of Great Britain and Northern Ireland . Not all funding 
provided by FCDO has been drawn by REPP 1  and the fund is still active but no longer make s 
new investments (except possibly into REPP 2).   
4. The purpose of th e present funding proposal is to request  GCF for fund ing in the form of  
junior equity participation in REPP 2 in the amount of USD 50 million , which CAMCO, as the AE , 
would invest in REPP 2  as a fiduciary for GCF  through a dedicated special purpose vehicle (GCF 
REPP Holdings) . CAMCO  serves as both AE and executing enti ty (EE) for this funding proposal ,


---
*Page 120*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 120 
 
 
 
 whereby Camco REPP 2  SCSp  would appoint Camco REPP GP S. à.r.l. (wholly owned by Camco 
Management  Limited ) as general partner at first close of the fund .  
5. The junior equity funded by GCF is expected to allow REPP 2  to: (i) leverage private , 
commercial and international financial institution  (IFI) funding tranches into its capital stack 
(both senior equity as well as notes ), based on a risk allocation where losses on the REPP 2 
portfolio would first be absorbed by the junior equity  (and not pari passu  across all equity 
investors in REPP 2 ); and (ii) provide attractive commercial terms to investees due to the fact 
that remuneration of the junior equity tranche does not reflect risk -adjusted pricing .  
6. The main purpose of REPP 2  is to drive a paradigm shift in the development of small-
scale renewable energy projects in sub-Saharan  Africa. REPP 2 proposes to invest in the 
following project types : 
(a) On-grid small -scale renewable energy generation projects (independent power 
producers ) with an installed capacity of up to 25  MW  for small hydropower, and  solar 
power projects  and 50MW for wind ( categor ies B and C risk s only)  and a target 
allocation of 50  per cent  in the REPP 2 portfolio ; and  
(b) Off-grid energy ventures based on renewable energy , including mini- grid operators and 
solar home system (SHS) companies , with  a target allocation of 40  per cent  for mini-
grids and 10 per cent  for SHS companies in the REPP 2 portfolio . 
7. REPP 2 will provide a range of financial instruments to invest in these projects , focusing 
on five different strategies  that seek to address key gaps in the financing market : 
(a) Early -stage growth funding for off -grid companies.  This would include hybrid equity 
(subordinated debt, convertible notes and other similar instruments) to support mini-
grid operators and S HS companies with development expenses , thus addressing the 
shortage of early -stage high -risk equity in the market;  
(b) Development -stage funding for grid-connected projects.  Similar to the above , this 
strategy would include hybrid equity provided to project developers to enable them to 
move new renewable energy projects along the development cycle, with an option to convert financing to construction loans at financial close , recognizing that developers 
have difficulty tapping the commercial market due to perceived and real risks  in project 
development ; 
(c) Expansion of SHS businesses.  This would  include equity as well as debt finance for SHS 
companies that want to grow their businesses and/or to expand into new markets  and 
those  that currently do not have access to financing in the commercial market due to 
their risk profile ; 
(d) Construction of mini -grid portfolios. This would include hybrid equity as well as 
subordinated and senior loans to mini- grid operators to finance construction of new 
facilities to grow their footprint and client base . This funding would also be for mini- grid 
operators  that cannot access commercial sources of finance due to their challenging 
businesses models and undeveloped/untested regulatory environments in target 
markets;  and 
(e) Construction finance.  Through this strategy, REPP 2  would provide hybrid equity, 
subordinated or senior loans to finance construction of small -scale on -grid renewable 
energy projects that otherwise find it challenging to mobilize the required financing 
from the commercial market.  
8. Apart from providing financial instruments to its investees, REPP 2 will further support 
investees/ projects through : (i) risk mitigation support (e.g. facilitating access to political risk 
insurance and off -taker credit enhancement/risk insurance available in the market ); (ii) 
development of strategies to mitigate hard currency risks for investees; (iii) development of


---
*Page 121*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 121 
 
 
 
 skills and capacit ies of local entrepreneurs and project developers; ( iv) support to 
environmental and social risk management by investees (e.g. providing  assistance in the 
development of environmental and social management systems) ; and ( v) promotion of gender 
equity (e.g. providing assistance i n the development of investee- specific gender action plans , 
which  will be mandatory for all projects ). 
9. REPP 2  also features a technical assistance facility (TAF) that will : (i) support investees 
and entrepreneurs with early -stage development grants to enhance their project development 
capabilities; (ii) work with governments/regulators in target markets to develop a conducive 
enabling environment for investment in small -scale renewable energy projects; and ( iii) provide 
assistance to female entrepreneurs to help close the gender gap in the renewable energy 
industry. The TAF is not a subject of this request f or funding from GCF ; a funding application in 
the amount of USD 5  million  for the TAF is pending with FCDO . The independent TAP  
commends the AE for raising funds for the TAF from other sources . 
10. The proposed fund life is 20 year s, with a 7 -year investment perio d during which it  
would invest in approximately 37 transactions (of which 26 are expected to be innovative 
precedence transactions in target markets) . At the level of REPP 2  itself (and using the proposed 
USD 100  million  junior equity commitment from GCF and REPP 1/FCDO  as leverage) , the fund is 
expect ed to mobilize USD 150  million  in additional funding from private sector investors as well 
as development finance institutions (DFIs ) and IFIs  (mobilization ratio of 1:1 .5).1 At the level of 
the individual assets that the fund will invest in, REPP 2 expects to mobilize a total of USD 786 
million in investment , whereby approximately 50  per cent  (i.e. USD 393  million ) is expected to 
com e from private sector and commercial sources (i.e. DFIs investing on commercial terms), 
translating into an indirect private sector/commercial funding leverage of the junior equity of 
roughly 1:4. ( The independent TAP  assume s that the remaining 50  per cent  of funds mobilized 
at asset level are public sector (non -commercial) contributions,  though the funding proposal 
does not provide details on the source of these funds. ) 
11. In terms of impact , the AE expects REPP 2  to achieve the following targets:  
(a) 330  MW of new renewable energy capacity installed  (translating into 130,580  MWh  of 
electricity produced ); 
(b) 12.7  megaton nes of carbon dioxide equivalent ( Mt CO 2 eq) of avoided or reduced 
greenhouse gas ( GHG)  emissions  (full REPP 2 investment portfolio over technology 
lifetime) ; 
(c) New or improved access to clean energy for  more than 7.7 million people;  
(d) Improved climate resilience of nearly one million beneficiaries;  and 
(e) 76,801 jobs (of which 23,040 jobs  will be for females ). 
1.2 Impact potential                 Scale:  Medium  to high 
12. The funding proposal credibly demonstrates that, if successful, REPP 2 has the potential 
to engender considerable mitigation , adaptation and sustainable development benefits in target 
countries. Overall, the theory of change, results framework and calculation/attribution 
 
1 The funding proposal distinguishes between three different sources of funding that REPP 2 hopes to attract to the 
project : (a) private sector capital  (defined by independent TAP  as funding from entities other than those (i) where 
the majority of shares or ownership in that entity (i.e. more than 50%) are held by public sector entities; or (ii) that 
benefit from an explicit state guarantee or other government support) ; (b) DFI capital invested on commercial 
terms , where such entities source or raise capital on their own balance sheets on a commercial basis including 
directly from private capital markets ; and ( c) IFI capital (possibly with somewhat lower return thresholds than ( a) 
and (b), though the funding proposal does not provide further information on the precise sources/nature of IFI 
contributions).


---
*Page 122*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 122 
 
 
 
 methodologies proposed for assessing the overall mitigation , adaptation and sustainable 
development impacts are transparent and based on best practice /market  standards  (see annex 
22 B of the funding proposal ). The independent TAP  was able to reconstruct the high -level 
impact targets provided in the funding proposal based on the background information that was 
provided by the AE.  The funding proposal also contains reasonably detailed assessments of 
climate  change hazard s for all target countries and highligh ts climate change vulnerabiliti es (see 
annex 2  of the funding proposal ) that support the overall climate rationale for this funding 
proposal . 
13. Regarding  the expected mitigation impact , the overall GHG emission reduction/  
avoidance target over the lifetime of the program me is expected to be 12,720,297 t  CO 2 eq. The 
calculation is based on the expected pipeline of projects that the f und may finance  (330  MW in 
total across a mix of technologies/ installation sizes ), the expected energy generation (812,771 
MWh  per year  for the entire portfolio)  and the relevant grid emission factor s across target 
jurisdictions, following the clean development  mechanism  methodology – including AMS -IA: 
electricity generation by the user (version 19) ; AMS -ID: grid-connected renewable electricity 
generation (version 18) ; and AMS -IL: electrification of rural communities using renewable 
energy (version 4). In practice, the actual projects funded by REPP 2 will likely be substantially 
different from the ( anonymized ) pipeline of projects contained in the funding proposal, but the 
overall targets presented appear reasonable and achievable. All mitigation targets will be aligned with host -country targets and priorities . The AE has provided detailed studies of the 
target countries, underscoring the relevance of investment in renewable energy c apacity as a 
key mechanism to either replace or avoid fossil fuel- based energy generation alternatives.  
14. More specifically, REPP 2 is expect ed to realize adaptation impact via two channels:  
15. First, by investing in small -scale renewable energy generation (including off -grid 
businesses), REPP 2 is  expect ed to create more resilient power systems by way of 
diversification  and decentralization:  
(a) Diversification away from large -scale hydropower generation that is increasingly 
subject to heightened variability in water availability due to climate change impacts ; and  
(b) Increased decentralization through investment in off -grid solutions, thus reduc ing the 
reliance of energy users on power grids that are becoming increasingly unstable due to 
extreme weather events resulting from climate change.  
16. Second , through investments in off -grid solutions ( e.g., mini- grids, SHS companies), the 
fund expect ed to increase the climate resilience of people and communities (including  micro, 
small and medium -sized enterprises ) by providing them with access to clean energy as an 
alternative to unsustainable fossil fuel/biomass solutions. The total number of beneficiaries 
REPP 2 is expect ed to reach extends to 370,224, based  on its current pipeline.   
17. While the independent TAP  recognizes the potential for REPP 2 to produce these 
positive climate outcomes  under a best -case scenario, there are also various challenges that 
fund will have to successfully navigate to leverage its full potential . 
18. One of the key factors driving  the fund’s potential to have impact at scale is the ability of 
the AE/EE to mobilize significant co -investments from other funding providers beyond the 
requested GCF junior equity contribution to ultimately grow the fund size to USD 250  million . In 
addition to raising USD 100 million in junior equity from public/catalytic investors (including 
the proposed USD 50 million contribution from GCF) , the AE expects to raise an additional USD 
100  million  in senior equity and USD 50  million  in notes (debt) from private  sector , commercial 
and IFI  funding sources , with a resulting leverage ratio of 1:1.5 . The independent TAP  notes 
various concerns regarding  the ability of the AE/EE to mobilize sufficient funding for senior 
equity and  notes for REPP 2 , specifically :


---
*Page 123*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 123 
 
 
 
 (a) First -time manager and risk profile : While the AE has experience with renewable 
energy investment in s ub-Sahara n Africa, the only real reference is the predecessor fund 
to REPP 2 ( REPP 1 ), which  was funded entirely by grants . (The independent TAP  was 
informed that CAMCO is separately preparing a USD 50 million commercial and 
industrial  fund focusing on promoting solar investments; that fund is in the process of 
closing .) REPP 1  has shown delayed implementation and subdued commercial returns 
(5.1 per cent  on portfolio,  which is  to some extent a consequence of the strong 
develo pmental mandate that was provided to REPP 1  by its only capital provider , 
FCDO ). Consequently , private sector investors as well as DFI s that invest on a 
commercial basis will consider the EE a de facto  first -time manager . In that context, the 
size of the fund (USD 250 million) will  be considered large, with an aggressive risk 
profile (small -scale investment in countries with ratings of “B” or below)  in an overall 
complicated market environment (e.g. , due to rising interest rate levels  and 
deteriorating macro fundamentals in REPP 2 target markets) .  
(b) Returns for investors and liquidity options : The return expectations REPP 2  is 
offering both for senior equity as well as  notes will further narrow the field  of interested 
parties . In its dialogue with  the independent TAP , the AE noted  that a higher hurdle rate 
for the senior equity would be preferable (especially with a view to enticing private 
sector investors) but highlight ed that this could also be achieved through a refinancing 
of the loan book at year 12. In addition to boosting returns, a  refinancing of the loan 
book at year 12 would provide investors (who typically would not want to lock in a 
funding commitment for a period of 20 years ) with a liquidity  option , something the 
independent TAP  also considers crucial for the AE to attract especially private sector 
investors to REPP 2 . The AE highlight ed that there were expression s of interest from 
some commercial banks to participate in refinancing and highlight ed that it seeks to 
negotiate a guarantee (potentially through  Swedish International Development 
Cooperation Agency ) to provide certainty to senior equity funders that the refinancing 
of the loan book will happen. The independent TAP  strongly encourages the AE to 
prioritize discussions with potential providers of a refinancing guarantee as the independent TAP  believes that,  without certainty on early liquidity , the fund will have 
difficult y attracting investors . If a refinancing of the portfolio is successful (possibly 
enabled by a refinancing guarantee from  the Swedish International Development 
Cooperation Agency  or another party), REPP 2 would also make a significant 
contribution to secondary market development , something that is sor ely lacking i n 
much of the African continent.   
(c) Fund life  and fee structure : The original funding proposal received for B .36 foresaw a 
fund life of up to 25 years , which the independent TAP  deemed unduly long  with a view 
to raising capital . While  the independent TAP  recognizes that an extended fund life is 
required to provide long -dated debt to target investees , the proposed 25 -year life would 
have further increase d the risk profile of REPP 2  for private  sector  as well as commercial 
DFI funders . It also would have create d a situation where the total fee pool would 
become very large, representing a significant portion of capital commitments.  (For 
clarity, the independent TAP  considers the proposed fee percentages as “within” market . 
The challenge is that the long fund life drives the absolute volume of fees that would 
have to be financed out of fund returns .) In the revised proposal, the AE has reduced the 
fund life to 20 years , which addresses the challenge to a certain extent . The independent 
TAP also notes that the GCF benefits from a Most Favo ured Nation clause in the t erm 
sheet that requires the fund to pass on any reduction in fee levels (e.g. because of  
negotiations with other public and private funders) to GCF.  
19. Overall, the  independent TAP  believes that  few investors will have the ability to consider 
investments  of this nature, leaving a narrow funding market. In the current macro environment, 
even DFIs investing on a commercial basis may find it difficult to commit sizable tickets to REPP


---
*Page 124*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 124 
 
 
 
 2. The AE has provided a fundraising update to the Secretariat and  the independent TAP  that 
suggests that , despite these overall challenges , there is some decent investor interest in the 
fund, as well as a reasonable response from DFIs and IFIs who are currently evaluating 
investments in senior equity or notes for a first close of the fund with a target volume of USD 60  
million . While t he independent TAP  commends the AE for the significant efforts it is putting into 
fundraising in a difficult market environ ment , the independent TAP  also  notes that there 
remains a risk that the AE will not be able to raise the entire USD 250 million target volume for 
the fund, thus making the achievement of the overall impact targets difficult if not impossible.   
20. Translating additional generation/investment in off -grid companies into actual 
use of clean energy. The main investment strategy of the f und, as noted above, is to invest in 
on-grid and off -grid small -scale renewable energy projects. In the case of investment s into on -
grid renewable energy independent power producers , the positive mitigation impact appears 
relatively straightforward . Most power systems in  the target countries currently lack sufficient 
generation capacity  (and in extreme cases load -shedding) ; therefore,  the additional renewable 
energy will be absorbed/used , thus avoid ing use of fossil fuel alternatives . However, for 
investments in off-grid companies, the logic may not be that straightforward. The AE implies in 
the funding proposal that investments in mini -grid operators and SHS businesses will , by 
definition , translate into new connections and actual use of electricity. On the other hand, d ata 
on the ability and willingness to pay in the target markets of REPP 2  show that many 
households (and many microbu sinesses) will find it difficult to access energy service offers due 
to the high initial connection charges (in particular for mini -grids , which is 40 per cent  of the 
target allocation  of REPP 2 ). Others, and especially vulnerable groups, will likely struggle to 
meet the credit profile expectations of most SHS businesses (10 per cent  of the target allocation  
of REPP 2 ). If that indeed materializes, and investments don’t translate into new connections 
and use of clean electricity, the  fund will struggle not just in achieving its mitigation but also its 
adaptation targets.  
21. This is a challenge not specific to REPP 2 , and addressing it is to some extent outside its 
immediate sphere of influence. independent TAP  notes with appreciation that the AE, in the 
revised funding proposal , makes a dedicated commitment (under activity 6) to work with its 
clients to promote access to existing schemes that provide access subsidies (e.g. Sustainable 
Energy for All's Universal Energy  Facility) , with a particular focus  on vulnerable groups and 
micro -businesses . The independent TAP  is also very  much encouraged by the fact that the AE is 
actively engaged in the design of various minimum review guarantee schemes relevant for min i-
grids, and thus has deep insight into the evolving landscape of potential guarantee providers  
that can then also be leveraged to its clients . The independent TAP  recognizes that REPP 2, as a 
fund that works on commercial principles, cannot provide connection subsidies or minimum 
revenue guarantees from its own balance sheet.  
22. Overall,  the independent TAP  regards the f und’s impact potential as medium to high . 
1.3 Paradigm shift potential                  Scale : Medium  
23. The main mission of REPP  2 is to deploy capital into a series of small -scale renewable 
energy generation projects  (up to 37  transactions) . The success (or impact) of the fund should 
thus in the first instance be judged against  the quality of the portfolio  that it will build , 
considering its mitigation , adaptation and sustainable development outcomes as well as its 
commercial performance.  
24. However, the funding proposal suggests that REPP 2 will also offer transformational 
potential that goes beyond the immediate impact of its investee portfolio , principally through 
three channels :


---
*Page 125*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 125 
 
 
 
 (a) First, the funding proposal highlights that REPP 2 will build local developer and 
business capacity, thus resulting in a larger and higher quality deal flow that will 
ultimately precipitate the growth of the local renewable energy industry beyond the 
fund  and provid e the basis for general industry growth  and follow -on investments ; 
(b) Second, the funding proposal notes that the increase in renewable energy capacity will 
result in enhanced energy security  in the context of increased climate hazards , thus 
contributing  to climate -proofing the energy sector ; and  
(c) Third , according to the funding proposal , the use of clean energy produced by REPP 2 
investees will trigger more income -generating activities, help improve social service 
delivery  and thus drive overall socio economic development and growth.  
25. The independent TAP  recognizes the importance of these “ transfo rmation channels” as 
part of the theory of change but  would like to emphasize that , in its view , the transformational 
impact (and thus paradigm shift) of REPP 2 in the small -scale renewable energy market of its 
target countries will depend to a large extent on three interrelated factors : 
(a) The ability of REPP 2 to develop and utiliz e appropriate transaction models that work 
for small -scale investments and the challenging market contexts that the fund will 
operate in , as well as models that could provide the basis for replication and scali ng up ;  
(b) The ability of REPP 2  to attract private sector investment (as defined in footnote 1 
above)  at the level of the fund on the back of a clear product offering and reasonable 
risk-return profile.  Attracting such private sector investment is an important test of the 
extent to which the market understands the REPP 2 product , and a crucial basis for any 
scaled follow -on investments from the private sector ; and  
(c) The ability of REPP 2 to successfully leverage especially local capital providers ( local 
commercial banks, pension fund managers , etc.) into projects that REPP 2 will finance in 
the on -grid or off -grid space. For the growth of the small -scale renewable energy market 
to go to scale and to become “self- perpetuating” (i.e.,  not reliant on international funding 
with concessional terms ), the local funding market will  ultimately  have to come to the 
table .  
26. On the first factor , in the funding proposal t he AE emphasizes the importance of 
deploying tailored financing approaches  to small -scale renewable energy projects in the target 
region (and has significant experience with this from REPP 1) . In the revised proposal, the AE 
provides some tangible examples of its approach to “project finance light ,” emphasizing the 
philosophy of innovation and flexibility that it will bring to the transactions  it will pursue . 
However, there is a tension between being  flexible in tailoring bespoke solutions and, at the 
same time , having the ambi tion to establish replicable transaction models in the market that 
could then be adopted and scaled up by others. For fundraising with private sector investors, 
the focus on flexibility and bespoke solutions may also be a challenging selling proposition as 
they typically look for a clear and efficient “product suite” to be executed. Finally, the 
independent TAP  believes  it will be difficult to refinance a portfolio of loans to projects at a later 
stage (as discussed above) if the transaction structures vary widely.  
27. The AE has noted that t he fund (based also on its experience with REPP 1) will establish 
specific investment standards (e.g. for its lending to SHS businesses), and will not allow a 
proliferation of transaction models. The independent TAP  welcomes th is and encourages the 
AE, as part of its knowledge management and outreach with the market, to share lessons 
learned and best practices of what type s of transaction models and standardized documentation 
work and have potential for replication and scale. This c ould be of particular relevance in the 
work  of the AE  with the local funding market  (as foreseen under outcome 5: contributing to 
stronger technology deployment, innovation and market transformation ).


---
*Page 126*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 126 
 
 
 
 28. On the second  factor , for reasons already highlighted above, the independent TAP  
believes that few private sector investors will consider investing in REPP 2. As a result, this will 
leave DFIs and IFIs  (International Finance Corporation , African Development Bank , etc.) as the 
main sources of funding, with GCF then effectively providing risk mitigation and subsidy 
(because of the submarket hurdle rate for junior equity investors) to these DFIs/IFIs rather 
than to private sector investors. The AE acknowledges that realit y in the revised  funding 
proposal  and notes that , due to the difficulties of raising funding  (especially from the private 
sector ) for REPP 2, it does not stipulate specific private sector fundraising targets at the level of 
the fund. Also, the GCF investment criteria do not prescribe any co -financing requirements or, 
for that matter, private sector leverage targets for GCF investmen ts. However, the independent 
TAP believes that the fund’s ability to attract private sector investment is an important 
precondition for it to effect paradigm shift. A scaling up of the scope and impact of the programme , or a replication of its approach in other regi ons, is only likely if the approach 
demonstrates its ability to leverage private sector investors into the capital structure of the 
fund.  
29. In this context, the independent TAP  welcomes the introduction of a tiered pricing 
structure for different investor categories  in the revised and updated term sheet ( annex 14 of 
the funding proposal ) that may provide additional incentives for private sector investors to 
make funding available.  If implemented, t his would reflect the reality that private sector 
investors in the current macroeconomic environment will find it otherwise difficult to make a 
long -term funding commitment to REPP 2 without attractive returns. Even considering the risk 
mitigation provided by the junior equity layer, a  senior preferred return of 15 per cent  cannot 
be considered excessive. The independent TAP  recommends includ ing a clear definition of 
private sector investors (following the definition in footnote 1 of this a ssessment report) to 
ensure that any additional senior preferred returns accrue only to private sector investors.  
30. In order to ensure that REPP 2 can unfold its full paradigm shift potential, the 
independent TAP  recommends to the Board to include the following condition to its potential 
approval of this funding proposal:  “Other than in relation to the first disbursement of the GCF 
Proceeds, delivery by the Accredited Entity to the Fund, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of written evidence confirming that Co -financing from private sources of an 
amo unt not less than ten per cent (10%) of the tota l capital commitments to Camco REPP SCSp 
(including the requested disbursement) is either already committed or expected to be 
committed, to Camco REPP SCSp, at the same time as the GCF Proceeds from the requested 
disbursement. Such private sources shall ex clude co -financing which is ultimately provided by 
entities (i) where the majority of shares or ownership in that entity (i.e. more than 50%) are 
held by public sector entities; or (ii) that benefit from an explicit state guarantee or other 
government supp ort agreement.”    
31. More broadly speaking, the mobilization of private sector funding for climate action is 
identified as an important objective of the GCF Private Sector Strategy. In that context, the 
independent TAP  tries to focus on the specific private sector leverage targets (e.g., at portfolio 
level, or for certain categories of investments) presented in GCF funding proposals. However, 
writing a mobilization target into funding proposals is not the same as actual  mobilization in the 
real world, and in the markets as we find it. We would suggest that the Secretariat explore ways 
to better diligence claims of private sector leverage in funding proposals , as basis for the 
independent TAP  review. We would also suggest exploring incentive mechanisms for AEs to 
ensure that they industriously pursue these private sector leverage targets after having secured 
GCF funding. GCF is not the only funding institution grappling with this issue; many public 
climate finance providers are trying to develop pathways to turn billions into trillions by way of 
leveraging private sector capital. The independent TAP  believes that, in order for GCF to 
successfully achieve the Private Sector Strategy’s objective “to catal yse private climate finance 
in a manner fully aligned with a country- driven approach to meet developing countries’ needs 
and the objectives of the USP” (updated Strategic Plan for GCF), specifying leverage targets and


---
*Page 127*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 127 
 
 
 
 introducing incentive mechanisms to drive these targets could be one powerful way to underpin 
the Private Sector Strategy with concrete action.  
32. On the third and final factor , in the funding proposal the AE suggests that,  at the level of 
individual assets , it expects to  raise roughly USD 393 million (or 50 per cent  of the total funds 
expected to be mobilized by REPP 2) from private sector and commercial sources. (The funding 
proposal is unclear  about  where the balance in mobilized funding would come from.  The 
independent TAP  assumes that it  would  come from public sector sources,  such as development 
aid, concessional funding from development banks,  and government contributions. ) Considering 
the complexity of the asset profiles and the target markets, this relatively low share of mobilized funding from private sector and commercial sources may not come as a surprise.  The estimate is 
based on the actual track record of REPP 1 , which has mobilized USD 460 million in co -
financing, with roughly 50  per cent  of that coming from private sector and commercial sources. 
The independent TAP  would strongly encourage the AE/EE to make every effort to increase the 
share of co-financing  from local private sector sources.  The independent TAP  recognizes that  
local  private sector financing is harder to obtain and more expensive (thus driving, for example,  
the levelized cost of electricity  for small -scale on -grid renewables and other small -scale 
projects) , but the independent TAP  believes  it is essential to ensuring that the local funding 
market engage s in this market segment  to achieve paradigm shift . The proposed refinancing of 
assets after 10 –12 years via a commercial bank is an additional way to achieve the objective of 
involving commercial banks  but, as discussed earlier, it is far from assured.  
33. The independent TAP  appreciates the differentiated and detailed treatment of the issues 
surrounding local currency financing by REPP 2 in the funding proposal . The independent TAP  
agrees that local currency risk can be more efficiently managed at the level of the fund than at 
the level of individual assets.  The independent TAP  also recognizes that the fund needs to 
balance, on the one hand, providing financing products that clients/projects can afford (thus 
generally favo uring local currency financing) and , on the other hand , ensuring that the  risk 
profile of REPP 2 remains attractive particular ly to private  sector  investors (which would mean 
carrying as little currency mismatch on the balance sheet as possible). In actual practice, it would appear that , for a large part of the targeted portfolio,  the AE/EE will transfer  the currency 
risk to investees (as hedge/swap opportunities are limited/too expensive and the programme may not be able to carry the associated risk). The independent TAP  encourages the AE to also 
consider partnerships with local investors in some key markets to determine whether there may be alternative ways for the f und to provide local currency solutions to projects . In addition, 
the AE could explore partnerships with other concessional capital providers ( such as the 
European Commission, Nordic Development Facility , and  KfW Development Bank ) to raise  
additional local currency shares to mitigate local currency risk at level. This is being 
implemented very effectively in other blended finance funds such as the SANAD Fund , the 
European Fund for Southeast Europe , and  the Green for Growth Fund.  A sustain able approach to 
local currency investing is another key factor driving the potential for paradigm shift.  
34. The EE proposes to engage with governments and regulators in key target markets with 
the aim  to structure transactions for REPP 2  that may require government/regulatory 
consents /changes . The EE also intends to provide support to governments/regulators in target 
markets via the TAF  (which is outside the scope of this proposal ) to address enabling 
environment constraints , although the precise scope of that remains somewhat unclear. In light 
of this , the independent TAP  acknowledges that the EE goes beyond the scope of the mandate of 
a typical investment manager  and recognizes that structuring small -scale renewable energy 
transactions in the target marke ts wi ll be challenging  and will require consistent engagement 
with public sector authorities . The funding proposal argues that the “precedence” nature of the 
transactions that the f und will support will create a model for follow -on transactions to occur. 
The funding proposal, as well as the question -and-answer  documentation , also point s to 
transactions in the REPP 1 portfolio and how such model transactions with REPP 1 involvement 
had been instrumental in triggering additional investment activity in the respective markets


---
*Page 128*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 128 
 
 
 
 (e.g. in Burundi after the investment of REPP 1 into the Mubuga Solar Project). However, the 
independent TAP  believes that , to drive transformational change, the f und’s investments and 
activities would have to provide a credible pathway for follow -on investments by systematically 
addressing enabling environment constraints in target markets. This is not the focus of the f und 
(and may well be outside its sphere of influence) , but the constraints  ultimately limit t he 
potential  of REPP 2  to advance regulatory/legislative frameworks to systematically promote 
investment in small -scale renewable energy projects.  
35. Overall , the independent TAP  rate s the potential for REPP 2 to effect paradigm shift as 
medium, with potential for improvement if the issues highlighted above are adequately 
addressed . 
1.4 Sustainable development potential             Scale:  High  
36. The funding proposal presents a credible pathway for REPP 2 to generate significant 
sustainable development benefits:  
(a) Economic co -benefits:  The funding proposal notes that the programme is expected  to 
create more than 76,801 jobs. The impact model provided by the AE (annex 22  of the 
funding proposal ) as well as the explanations provided in the Q+A session  point to the 
reality that a significant share of this estimate is based on indirect job creation effects from  new generation capacity supported by REPP 2  investments. The funding proposal 
notes that “The ex -ante estimate of the number of jobs is based on the average numbe r 
of jobs required by MW [megawatt] build, multiplied by MWs financed. The job numbers 
are technology specific and have been driven from IRENA: Renewable Energy and Jobs, 
Annual Review 2021 .”; 
(b) Social co-benefits : The funding proposal highlights multiple social co -benefits resulting 
from programme investments, mostly due to  improved access to lighting, the 
electrification of health facilities, access to refrigeration, etc. The independent TAP  notes 
that those benefits (much like the e conomic co -benefits) only accrue if the investment in 
generation capacity or off -grid companies translates  into more connections and actual 
use of clean energy by beneficiaries ; and 
(c) Gender -sensitive development co -benefits : Finally, the funding proposal argues that 
the benefits from additional investment in small -scale renewable energy generation will 
disproportionately benefit women and girls  as, at present, they tend to be responsible 
for sourcing alternative sources of fuel (e.g. firewood) , and obtaining access to clean 
electricity will enable them to  use their time more productively (e.g. schooling). REPP 2 
will also make g ender action plans mandatory for all investees, and drive c ompliance 
with the 2X gender lens investing  criteria.  Because  the due diligence on the f und 
pipeline has not commenced yet, it is unclear how the projects in the pipeline currently perform against the 2X gender lens investing  criteria.  However, in the funding proposal, 
the AE states that “ at the end of 2022 phase -1 of REPP had committed $21m, which is 
30%, of its funding to female -owned and led companies, which indicates that REPP  2 
will similarly be able to source 2X aligned deals ”; and  
37. In accordance with  item 7 (results management framework)  of the GCF t erm sheet, all 
impacts delivered through REPP 2 are captured in the logical framework, which forms the basis 
of all monitoring and reporting under the p rogramme . 
38. Overall,  the independent TAP  rates the sustainable development impact potential of 
REPP 2  as high . 
1.5 Needs of the recipient               Scale:  High


---
*Page 129*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 129 
 
 
 
 39. The funding proposal credibly demonstrates the increasing climate change hazards 
afflicting the target cou ntries, and the significant vulnerabilit ies of relevant populations (section 
6 and annex 2  of the funding proposal ). While climate change hazards and vulnerabilities vary 
across and within these countries, there is no doubt that all of them are  significantly impacted 
by climate change . These impacts are  aggravated by the realities of other socioeconomic 
development challenges in the s ub-Saharan African region  (as also emphasized by the Sixth 
Assessment Report  of the United Nations Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change) . 
40. While the poorest segments of society in  the target markets may not be able to access 
clean electricity , either on -grid or off -grid , even after REPP 2 support  due to economic 
constraints (ability to pay) , there are indirect benefits that will accrue (e.g. electrification of 
social institutions like schools and health cent res).  
41. The funding requirements for renewable energy generation (on -grid and off -grid) in the 
region are considerable and well -documented (closing the energy access gap in sub -Saharan 
African countries will require an estimated annual investment of USD  28 billion up to 2030, 
according to the  International Energy Agency) . Considering slow growth , low tax revenues and 
stretched government budgets /high sovereign indebtedness  in the target markets, the public 
sector will not be able to mobilize the required infrastructure investment . Multilateral 
development banks and other development partners are providing support but not at the scale 
required to close the investment gap. Local commercial sources of funding (e.g. commercial 
banks, pension funds) exist but typically do not finance infrastructure projects due to lack of 
familiarity with the asset class and /or statutory investment restrictions. The independent TAP  
recommends that the EE make a systematic effort in its investment activities to engage with local commercial capital provider s, which would also open opportunities for leveraging local 
currency to projects. The independent TAP  also encourages the EE to use the TAF strategically 
to strengthen links with local capital providers and leverage them into transactions.  
42. Overall, the independent TAP  rates the potential for REPP 2 to serve the needs of the 
recipients as high . 
1.6 Country ownership                Scale:  Medium  to high 
43. Coherence with existing policies : The funding proposal ( annex 2) provides 
comprehensive evidence that REPP 2 is broadly aligned with the priorities of host governments 
in target markets with regard to:  
(a) Nationally determined contributions  and associated implementation targets ; 
(b) National climate policies ; 
(c) National energy p olicies and legislative/regulatory environment for private investment 
in renewable energy ; 
(d) General national development policies ; and 
(e) National gender policies.  
44. While the scope and depth of alignment varies across countries , the overall impact and 
investment focus of REPP 2 is aligned  and supportive of national objectives.  
45. Capacity of the AE: Since 2015, the AE has been implementing REPP 1, a fund similar in 
objectives ( investment in small -scale renewable energy generation) but with some notable 
differences . While REPP 1 is entirely funded by grants  (no commercial investor involvement  in 
the capital structure of the fund) , REPP 2 invests by committing debt or equity products (but  it 
cannot be considered a fully commercial fund vehicle ). REPP 2 also features a n extended 
selection of target countries.


---
*Page 130*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 130 
 
 
 
 46. The independent TAP  recognizes that the AE will be able to leverage many learnings 
from REPP 1 to REPP 2 , but also views the significant changes in the capital structure (especially 
with expected participation of private sector and commercial investors) and the extended 
geography of REPP 2  as new challenges the AE must successfully manage.  
47. The independent TAP  also notes that REPP 2  – while predominantly a debt fund -  would 
make investments with quasi -equity instruments. Prior experience would suggest that driving 
an investment portfolio consisting of different asset classes is generally challenging . Different 
asset classes require a variety of skill  sets and , typically , equity investment involves a very 
different approach  and mindset  than debt investment s. In combination with the complexity of  
the target markets and considering the size of the fund, the AE/EE will have to successfully 
navigate the resulting challenges.  
48. As previously noted, the AE will also function as the EE for REPP 2 . As detailed in the 
funding proposal and the term sheet, C AMCO  would both invest any potential GCF contribution 
as fiduciary into the f und  and would also be contracted by the f und as its investment manager  
under the terms of the proposed investment management agreement . In the funding proposal , 
the resulting potential conflicts of interest are discussed in section F (Risk Assessment and Management). During the question -and-answer  session with the AE , it noted that , as a regulated 
manager , CAMCO has strong conflict of interest policies and procedures in place, and that it is 
confident that it would adequately manage potential challenges. However, i t is worth pointing 
out that the realities of ethical walls (virtual barriers) in a small organization (~30 employees) 
are at least questionable . In addition , it is worth pointing out that the expected fee income from 
managing REPP 2 would constitute a very significant source of income for CAMCO  (~30  per cent  
according to their estimates) , which would exacerbate the potential challenge. This challenge is 
not specific to this funding proposal but constitutes a general issue that the GCF Secretariat would need to consider in its overall approach.  
49. The independent TAP  considers the alignment of REPP 2 with host country government 
priorities high , but assesses the AE track record as medium. Overall, the independent TAP  rate s 
the country ownership of the funding proposal as medium -high . 
1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness               Scale:  Medium  to high 
50. As also noted in the funding proposal , the estimated cost per t  CO 2 eq mitigated/avoided 
is relatively high  (USD  18.35 per t  CO 2 eq). This is not surprising considering the fact that the 
inve stment focus is on small -scale renewable energy projects that typically feature higher fixed 
investment cost  than large -scale infrastructure , resulting in higher levelized cost and thus lower 
efficiencies with regard to emissions avoided/reduced.  
51. The long fund life ( 20 year s) and  the likely implications of this for fundraising potential 
with private sector and commercial investors  were discussed  earlier in this assessment  report . 
As a potential junior equity investor, GCF would be the first in and last out of the f und, while 
private sector and commercial equity providers may find liquidity earlier (if a viable path is 
developed). This is a very long -term commitment; however, it is impossible to evaluate the 
counterfactual (what GCF could do with the funds if the  fund life was shortened ). 
52. The independent TAP  assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal 
as medium- high . 
II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
53. The independent TAP  endorse s this funding proposal.


---
*Page 131*
---

 
GCF/ B.37/02/Add. 13 
Page 131 
 
 
 
 54. Overall, the funding proposal  is ambitious and  has potential to generate significant 
mitigation, adaptation as well as broader sustainable development benefits for the target 
countries through projects that will receive investments from the fund. In focusing on the small -
scale segment of the renewable energy market, REPP 2 has the potential to play an instrumental 
role in closing the well -documented gap , especially in access to high -risk equity capital that is 
required for more project development (and ultimately investment) activity t o occur. The focus 
of the f und on least developed countrie s, and thus on markets that have mostly seen little or no 
private investment thus far in small -scale renewable energy projects , is particularly welcome.  
55. The independent TAP  also welcome s the graduation from grant funding for the capital 
stack of the f und (as was the case in REPP 1) to a blended finance structure that opens the 
potential for private sector and commercial leverage at portfolio level. The independent TAP  
views this as important to help engender the emergence of a truly sustainable market for small -
scale renewable energy in s ub-Saharan  Africa by gradually making private sector investors in 
particular more familiar with investing in this new asset class in jurisdictions that are 
unfamiliar  to them . CAMCO  has considerable experience in the target region and thus appears 
well -positioned to execute the investment mandate of REPP 2 . 
56. In order to ensure that REPP 2 can unfold its full paradigm shift potential, the 
independent TAP  recommends to the Board to include the following condition to its potential 
approval of this funding proposal:  
57. “Other than in relation to the first disbursement of the GCF Proceeds, delivery by the 
Accredited Entity to the Fund, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of 
written evidence confirming that Co -financing from private sources of an amo unt not less than

requested disbursement) is either already committed or expected to be committed, to Camco 
REPP SCSp, at the same time as the GCF Proceeds from the reque sted disbursement. Such 
private sources shall exclude co -financing which is ultimately provided by entities (i) where the 
majority of shares or ownership in that entity (i.e. more than 50%) are held by public sector entities; or (ii) that benefit from an e xplicit state guarantee or other government support 
agreement.”

Impact potential  
The iTAP assessment of the impact potential as medium -high is noted with appreciation.  
The AE acknowledges the challenges highlighted by the iTAP in mobilizing significant co -
investment for REPP 2. The AE is pleased to note that since the date of discussions with iTAP, 
the Renewable Energy Performance Platform has executed a term sheet for a junior equity 
interest of USD50m to REPP 2. Further positive discussions with potential investors have 
continued since the date of the submission of the revised FP to iTAP.  
Paradigm shift potential  
The iTAP assessment of the paradigm shift potential as medium is noted with appreciation.  
As noted in paragraph 27, the AE is committed to sharing the lessons learnt and best practices 
developed during the lifetime of REPP 2, particularly in relation to workable transaction 
models and replicable documentation.  
The AE agrees with the iTAP assessment that the leveraging of private capital is key to the 
paradigm shift potential of REPP 2. In relation to the proposed condition in paragraph 30 (repeated in paragraph 57), the AE strong believes that it is involvement of the private sector 
by final close that is the appropriate measure of success – 
particularly in the current 
challenging fundraising environment. Accordingly, the AE proposed to iTAP a slight revision of 
the proposed condition as follows:  
“The final disbursement of the GCF Proceeds  shall be conditional on the delivery by the Accredited 
Entity to the Fund, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of written evidence 
confirming that Co -financing from private sources of an amount not less than ten per cent (10%) 
of th e total capital commitments to Camco REPP SCSp (including the requested disbursement) is 
either already committed or expected to be committed, to Camco REPP SCSp, at the same time as 
the GCF Proceeds from the requ ested disbursement. Such private sources shall exclude co -
financing which is ultimately provided by entities (i) where the majority of shares or ownership 
in that entity (i.e. more than 50%) are held by public sector entities; or (ii) that benefit from an 
explicit state guarantee or other government support agreement .” 
This revision was not accepted by iTAP and the AE accepts the condition as proposed by iTAP.  
The AE welcomes the suggestion of iTAP contained in paragraph 31.  
The AE notes the encouragement of the iTAP to increase the role of local private finance 
sources and will seek to do so in implementing REPP 2. Similarly, the AE will actively seek to 
build partnerships with providers of local currency solutions (paragraph  33).

3 Gender Assessment  1 Executive summary  
 
This gender assessment explores the relationships between men and women, their access to resources and the 
constraints they face relative to one an other in REPP 2 target countries within the context of climate change 
and energy access.  
REPP 2’s target countries have made progress in integrating the gender dimension at the political, strategic and 
legal levels and in some indicators REPP 2 target countries  rank better than the world average. However, there 
are still significant gaps in achieving parity and women and girls are disproportionately affected by inadequate 
energy infrastructure and services , as well as unequal chances  to engage in economic opportunities presented 
by renewable energy access. The following key barriers  have been i dentified:  
• Women face hurdles  accessing modern energy  
• Women’s employment opportunities in renewable energy are limited  
• Women’s access to funding at the individual and company level is limited . 
The Gender Action Plan (GAP)  presented in Section 6 consist s of two main actions : 1) dedicate 30% of REPP 2 
funding  towards companies that meet the 2X Challenge’s G ender Lens Investing (2X GLI) criteria , and 2) 
integrat e a gender equality perspective into the design, implementation  and monitoring of all renewable energy 
activities funded by REPP 2. These activities will  assist in designing products and services for the most 
vulnerable groups of the population, as well as  overcom ing the existing inequalities that exist in the renewable 
energy sector , which are  presented in more detail in Section 4.  
 
2 Overview of the target 
countries  
 
REPP 2’s investment mandate will extend towards some of the most economically and socially vulnerable 
countries in Africa, most of which fall into the low human development category under the 2021 Human 
Development Index (HDI) ; specifically,  Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, 
Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Lesotho ). Despite Cameroon making it into the medium human development cohort, it 
is still ranked 151 out of 191 countries and frequently fall s below group averages on key development indicators 
as presented below in Table 1.1

Indexes presented in Tables 1 and 2 place REPP 2 target countries among the poorest in the world , with 
women being disproportionately disadvantaged in terms of the opportunities and benefits that the countries and 
their economies have to offer, which results in lower values in general development indicators for women 
compared to men. This highlights the importance of gender -disaggregated data for the assessment of women’s 
empowerment and economic development in the target countries.  
Lack of gender -disaggregated data is especially distorting in generalised indicators such as the World Bank’s 
International Poverty Line . Recent studies show that the way the indicator is calculated fails to consider  
differences between men and women.7 The World Bank’s approach to assessing poverty relies on two main 
variables : consumption and income. The values for these variables are obtained through household surveys, 
which provide information on consumption and income at the household level, whereas statistics on the 
population living below the International Poverty Line seek to assess poverty at the individual level. Hence, 
individual indicators are calculated by dividing household -level data by the number of household members.  
The World Bank recognises that this approach involves several important assumptions, such as that household 
resources are shared equally across family members; that family members have identical needs ; that two 
individuals with the same per capita consumption level enjoy the same living standard; and that there are no 
differential costs of reaching a given welfare level per person for households of different sizes.8 These 
assumptions do not correspond to realities, as gender inequalities often make women and girls more vulnerable 
than men and boys to poverty. This is largely due to prevailing gender norms, the intra‐household division of 
assets, work and responsibilities , and power relations that drive these disparities.9 
With this in mind, REPP 2’s gender assessment and respective GAP have been drawn up based on available 
gender- disaggregated data, which has allowed us to place women and men in the context of their respective 
countries and communities and to assess gender inequalities pertaining to the energy sector. The data source s 
 
7 World Bank, 2018. Gender Differences in Poverty and Household Composition through the Life -cycle: A Global 
Perspective . 
8 World Bank, 2015. A Measured Approach to Ending Poverty and Boosting Shared Prosperity: Concepts, Data, and 
the Twin Goals . 
9 World Bank, 2018. Gender Differences in Poverty and Household Composition through the Life -cycle: A Global 
Perspective .

marginalised groups with limited applicability to REPP 2. 
3 Gender gap  
Gender equality is measured by a number of international indexes , as presented below. Despite relative 
variability , African countries generally seem to have the lowest scores not only in terms of human development, 
but also gender equality and other related  indicators.  
This assessment consider s the most recent indicators from UNDP Human Development Reports, World 
Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Reports  and OECD’s Social Institutions and Gender Index Regional 
Reports for Africa, as well as a comprehensive body of data and qualitative assessments gathered and published  
by the World Bank Group. A lthough these sources provide a comprehensive outlook onto gender issues of the 
region, including REPP 2 target countries, it is recognised that these indicators and assessments d o not fully 
address the role of women in the energy sector (as service providers, employees , and end users), since they 
lack sufficient country -level statistical data. Where such data is not available, the assessment herein has been 
made based on generalised data for the region and/or broader sectors (such as industry as opposed to energy) 
as well as latest available country -level data derived from other verified sources. 
3.1 Indicators an d indexes  
Gender Development Index (GDI ) measures gender inequalities in three basic dimensions of human 
development: health (based on life expectancy at birth), education (expected years of schooling and mean years of schooling) and command over economic resources (GNI per capita) . 
Gender Inequality Index (GII)  measures gender inequalities in other three basic dimensions: reproductive 
health (maternal mortality ration and adolescent birth rate), empowerment (share of parliamentary seats held 
by each gender and population with at least some secondary education) and the labour market (labour force participation rate), are two main gender inequality indexes calculated by UNDP in its human development  
reports.
10 
Global Gender Gap Index  (GGGI)  benchmarks the current state and evolution of gender parity across four 
key dimensions, namely economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment).
11  The GGGI adopts a more holistic approach, integrating GDI and GII indicators 
as well as additional indicators relevant for the energy sector, such as level of enrolment in tertiary education, involvement of women as professional and technical workers, number of female legislators, senior officials and 
managers etc . 
REPP 2 target countries  perform differently in these indicators as presented below in Table 3  and 4 . 
GDI: Target countries have varying indicators of gender equality with two -thirds of the countries showing low 
levels of gender equality with absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10 %.12 Whereas Madagascar,

equality and place it into a respective GDI group. Countries with absolute deviation from gender parity of 2.5% or

Group 1. Group 2 (medium -high equality) includes countries with 2.5-5% deviation, and Group 3 (medium equality)

3.2 Health and wellbeing  
Despite the progress that has been made in recent years  (see Table 5 ), REPP 2 target countries still experience 
high maternal and infant mortality rates, as well as a lower life expectancy at birth compared to world averages.  
Harmful practices such as gender -based violence and child marriages are also persistent across the countries, 
which lack sufficient legal basis for the protection for women. With the exception of DRC and Malawi, a ll of the 
target countries have legislative exemptions  regarding the minimum legal age of marriage, such as the consent 
of parents, guardians, judges and/or courts.19 For instance, in Sierra Leone , where  marriage is governed by the 
customary law as well as the statutory law, the Registration of Customary Marriage and Divorce Act 2007 allows 
for underage customary marriages if they happen with parents’ consent.20

3.3 Education 
According to the available data, relative progress has been made in REPP 2 target countries  and the rest of Sub -
Saharan Africa with regards to overall access to , and the attainment of , education by both men and women. At 
the same time, this progress has not been significant enough for the indicators to catch up with world averages 
and the recorded gender gap remains persistent across all levels and fields of education.  
Fewer  than half of REPP 2 target countries (Cameroon, Lesotho, and Madagascarhave female literacy rates 
comparable to those of the male population while also closely approaching or exceeding world averages. In 
other countries, women are at least 20% less likely to be literate, with only 27% of Niger’s adult females being 
able to both read and write with understanding a short simple statement about their everyda y life, as presented 
in Table 7 below.

gap between males (30.2%) and female s (27.1%).25

hen it comes to tertiary education, Sub -Saharan Africa is performing poorly with regard to gender parity, with

remained at approximately 1.4 value in Lesotho, leaving men at a disadvantage.30

Out of the few women who do enrol in tertiary education in Sub -Saharan Africa, most opt for traditionally more 
“female” fields, such as arts and humanities or business, administration and law. According to the data collected 
in the Global Gender Gap Report  2022 and presented in Tables 9 and 10, all STEM specialties, as well as 
engineering, manufacturing, construction, natural sciences, mathematics and statistics have a significantly lower 
female enrolment rate compared to the male one.  
Gender stereotypes and societal views on traditional gender roles in Sub -Saharan Africa have been shown to 
play a role in such distribution of enrolment between men and women. A 2019 IFC report, for example, found  
women who enrol led in technical or engineering program mes were viewed unfavourably.33  
All of the above contribute to the  disadvantaged position of women when considering employment in the 
energy sector . Women’s l ow literacy rate s and general  lack of education plus the low STEM enrolment rates of

3.4 Economic opportunities  
3.4.1 Employment  
In most REPP 2 target countries , working -aged women are less likely than men to engage in the labour market, 
either by working or actively looking for work, as presented in Table 11. Female unemployment i s also generally 
higher than male unemployment , and has increased compared to 2014 indicators, likely as one of the negative 
consequences of the COVID -19 pandemic.

vulnerability, increased informality and lower job status compared to men.

3.4.2 Access to land  
Women’s ability to participate in a labour market, especially as entrepreneurs or own -account workers, as well 
as their ability to access financial services largely depends on ownership of assets, and real estate in particular.  
Asset ownership has a multidimensional impact on the life and economic empowerment of women. The ability 
to administer assets and use them for productive gain purposes, the ability to use the assets as collateral for 
credit or as a resource base for starting a business by enhancing liquidity through rental or sale, independence

Assets ownership in Africa and involves the issue of women’s legal ability to own real estate. As described in 
Section 3.6 below, three of REPP 2 target countries  (Cameroon, DRC and Niger) significantly limits women’s 
rights to own immovable property and grants a husband all administrative rights over assets during marriage.  
In practice, available statistical data, presented in Tables 14 and 15, shows that there is a significant 
disproportion in the amount of land owned and/or effectively managed by men and women in REPP 2 target 
countries. In particular, women do not have a substantial share in agricultural holding s, which amounts to 
making major decisions regarding resource use and exercises management control over the agricultural holding, not necessarily due to ownership rights. In addition, even though distribution of agri cultural landownership by 
gender in certain countries is more favourable for women (e.g. , 57.3% of landowners in Malawi were recorded 
to be women), the area of land actually owned solely by women and the value of this land is statistically smaller than the area and value of land owned solely by male landowners, as presented in Table 15.  
In addition, even in the countries where women are legally  granted equal rights to own and administer the 
property, matters related to real estate and family are in some of the host countries governed by customary law, which operates in parallel to statutory law and often disadvantages women.

3.4.3 Financial inclusion  
Despite  an overall increase in account ownership and access to formal financial services, vulnerable groups such 
as women, poor people and less educated adults continue to make up the majority of people excluded from the

17 Gender Assessment  formal financial sector. Women are often excluded from formal banking services because they lack official forms 
of identification, do not own a mobile phone or other form of technology  that enables mobile banking , and have 
lower financial capability.50 In fact, nearly 60 % of unbanked adults in Sub -Saharan Africa who took part in a 
2022 survey said they did not have enough money to open an account in a financial institution. The other 
reasons for not having a mobile money account were the lack of a mobile phone and the lack of necessary 
documentation. There barriers are especially critical for women, who have lower economic security, are less 
likely to be able  to obtain a mobile phone or experience a heavier documentation burden compared to men. In 
addition, many REPP 2 target countries lack sufficient legal protection for women willing to enter formal 
financial services sector , as described in Section 3.6 . 
This gender gap is particularly evident from the statistics, which indicate  that the gap in account ownership as 
being between 5 to 20 perce ntage points for half of REPP 2 target countries, as well as generally decreased 
instances of women’s borrowing from formal financial institutions and increased reliance on savings club s, (see

3.6 Legal status of women and gender stereotypes  
All REPP 2 target countries have made significant improvements in promoting gender equality in law. (Based on 
the World Bank Group study, DRC isin the world’s top 10 most improved economies since 1970 in relation to 
gender equality in law. )57 Furthermore, the  statutory law does not differentiate between genders in granting 
individual rights to a person in all of REPP 2’s target  countries.  
Nevertheless, all target countries are characteri sed by a dual legal system, which allows for customary law to 
largely govern societal life  - especially with regard to family and marital matters as well as property and 
economic inclusion  - rendering statutory provisions on gender equality inapplicable and unenforceable.  
Women are disadvantaged in a vast number of areas due to their persistent de-valuation by local customs and 
gender stereotypes, the effects of customary law , and also gaps in statutory law. Common restrictions that 
prevent economic development and empowerment of women in REPP 2 target countries are further described in 
Table 18 below.

Right to movement and 
mobility  Cameroon, Malawi, Niger and Nigeria do not grant women the same freedom of 
movement as is afforded to men.58 In Cameroon, although both genders are 
recogni sed as equals by the Constitution, the Civil Code designates the husband 
as the head of the household, who has the final decision on the family’s domicile, contradicting the woman’s constitutional rights to freedom of movement and residence within the border.
59 
In addition, married women cannot obtain a passport in the same way as men in 
Cameroon, Madagascar, Malawi and Nigeria.60 In Malawi and Madagascar, they 
are required to submit a marriage certificate with their husband’s name 
included.61 
Formal employment 
sector  Under Cameroon’s  Labour Code both men and women have equal rights to 
work. At the same time, the Family Code allows the husband to challenge his 
wife’s rights to work if it is in the interest s of the marriage and the children.62 
In Nigeria, statutory law does not contain a specific prohibition of gender -based 
discrimination in the workplace.63 
Remuneration and 
employment in 
industry  Among REPP 2 target countries, only Malawi and Niger have legislation mandating equal remuneration for work of equal value.
64 
Cameroon, DRC, Madagascar and Niger impose restrictions on women’s ability to 
work in a job deemed dangerous, while Cameroon, Madagascar, Nigeria, Sierra 
Leone and Lesotho restrict women’s right to work in industrial jobs.65 
Head of household  In Cameroon66, DRC67, Madagascar68 and Niger69, under both statutory and  
customary law  a woman can become a head of a household only under special 
circumstances, thus depriving women of rights and opportunities which head of 
a household status grants.

20 Gender Assessment  Restriction  Description  
Entrepreneurship and 
access to financial 
services  Gender -based discrimination with regards to access to credit is not prohibited in 
Cameroon, Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, and  Lesotho.70 
In addition, women typically lack knowledge of legislation governing credit 
relationships, have limited access to financial resources and have neither credit 
history nor banking skills to attempt to access formal credit on their own.  
Opening a bank account for women in Cameroon and Niger is also more 
complicated for women than it is for men, as women are required to obtain their 
husband’s permission.71 
Assets A number of restrictions on property ownership by women is in place in REPP 2 
target countries. Most of them pertain to cultural norms and customary law, 
which forbid women to own and/or inherit property, especially land. Also, in 
Cameroon, Madagascar and Niger even the statutory law recogni ses a husband’s 
ultimate decision -making power with regard to administering assets during 
marriage .72 
Examples of the type of restrictions women face include:  
In Cameroon, both civil and customary legal regimes do not allow married 
women to own or manage land and assets. Under civil law, marital property is 
under the authority of the husband who may manage and make decisions 
without the consent of his spouse. Under customary law, only men have the 
right to own the land ; women are often regarded as the property of the husband 
and are thus excluded from land ownership.73 
In DRC, no distinction is made in the provision of land rights between women 
and men  under statutory law . Yet under customary law, traditional chiefs, who 
are largely men, hold the authority on land rights guided by patrilineal lineages. 
By these principles, inherited land is almost always given to sons rather than 
daughters and women’s access to land becomes dependent on their husband. 
Also, given deeply embedd ed stereotypes surrounding the roles of women and 
men, a married couple’s property is often regis tered in the husband’s name, and 
it is more likely to see joint land titling among unrelated men than husband and 
wife.74 
 In Zambia, where c ustomary land accounts for the majority of land (96%)  and 
governed by traditional authorities, only men have the right to own and 
power to dispose of customary land, while wife or children may only have 
access to it.75 
 
According to the 2019 Social Inclusion and Gender Index ’s country profiles, REPP2 target countries  for which 
data is available have medium (DRC, Lesotho) to very high gender -based discrimination in social institutions,

21 Gender Assessment  which beyond the structural and legal barriers also include norms, attitudes, stereotypes and fundamental 
customs and traditions restricting women’s independence and economic empowerment.76 
SIGI 2021 Regional Report for Africa77 summari ses the role played by these discriminatory social institutions in 
women’s access to employment and other economic opportunities as follows:  
• Discriminatory gender roles within the household constrain women’s access to the 
labour market.  In addition to an increased burden on women in the form of unpaid domestic and care 
work, the proportion of people  declaring that it is not acceptable for a woman in their family to do any 
paid work outside of the home is significant, ranging from 15% in Nigeria to 28% in Niger. Earning 
money and providing for the family is considered an integral part of masculine identity, and not seen as 
favourable in women.  
• Perception of women’s abilities and discriminatory educational norms tend to confine 
women to low -status jobs in specific sectors of the economy.  In Africa, men tend to be 
overrepresented in “manly” sectors such as construction, mining and quarrying or transport, storage and 
communication. Women are predominant in sectors such as accommodation, wholesale and retail trade or 
household services. This is also evident from the quantitative data analysed in Section 3.4 above and 
takes its origins from the choice  of educational path and vocational trainings. The implications for 
economic empowerment are far -reaching, as women in Africa seem to be largely drawn to sectors that 
are less profitable or where wages are lower simply because they are considered “female” sectors.  
• Men’s traditional status and roles, household decision- making practices and 
discriminatory inheritance practices limit women’s ownership of agricultural land and 
constrain their economic independence. Customs and traditions related to land ownership are 
deeply rooted in African societies and often in practice supersede formal civil or common law in REPP 2 
target countries.  
• Various discriminatory social norms and biases related to access to markets, finance, training and networks hamper women’s entrepreneurship in Africa. 
These norms and biases  
result in limited attempts by women to even enter the credit market due to self -perceived lack of 
creditworthiness, as well as in actual constraint s over access to credit due to lack of sufficient economic 
resources , lower income and high informality rates in employment.  
 
4 Gender inclusivity in the 
energy sector  
 
Energy, especially electricity, is an invaluable basic commodity that the quality of people’s livelihoods is 
indispensably dependent on. From the gender perspective, the energy sector can be viewed as an important 
provider of the resource base necessary for the everyday life and economic activity of either the household as
