potential opportunities for women’s empowerment through employment and entrepreneur ship. 
 
4.1 Women as employees 
The energy sector is dominated by men . Women are underrepresented  in the sector. It is also a relatively small 
sector in Sub -Saharan Africa and  therefore  not a big employer on the country - and region -wide scale  compared 
with the world’s most developed countries . The African Development Bank has estimated that in 2021 , 
electricity, gas  and water activities account ed for an estimated 1.8 % of GDP in Sub -Saharan Africa.78 Total 
employment in the sector constitutes a small percentage  of the total employment in the  target  countries, 
ranging from 0.6% of Malawi’s total workforce to 4.6% of Lesotho ’s, as presented in  Table 19. The distribution 
of the already limited number of jobs on offer in the sector  by gender  shows significant disparities, with female 
employment generally not exceeding one third of total sector employment and dropping to one tenth of the 
sector  in the case of Lesotho.

Despite the currently insignificant value added by the energy sector to the GDP of African countries, the 
renewable energy subsector has been booming. Between  2007- 2019, African countries were attracting 65% of 
the total off-grid renewable energy investment.80 The positive effects of such investment included not only an 
increase in electrification levels for rural and remote communities, but also enhanced opportunities for the 
economic empowerment of women.  
While regional and country -level gender -disaggregated data for the energy sector remains scarce, especially in 
relation to the renewable energy subsector, IRENA reports that at the global level the share of women 
employed in the renewable energy sector (32%) is higher than in the oil and gas industry (22%).81 Renewable 
energy projects and companies are therefore more likely to attract women professionals than traditional energy

23 Gender Assessment  companies. The study conducted by the UN Women and AfDB on women ’s employment opportunities in the 
“green ” economy argues that the main reason for this is the multidimensionality of renewable energy (when 
compared with conventional energy production), which presents more employment opportunities for women in 
non-STEM positions, such as finance, law, environmental sciences, economics, etc.82 Moreover, a range of 
support services, such as consulting, research and development, and the creation of permits, regulations and 
policies, are projected to emerge along with the sector’s development.83 This allows for an easier increase in 
female employment and economic independence without challenging the skills -related gender stereotypes that 
persist in REPP 2 target countries.  
 
4.1.1 Female employment profile in renewable energy companies  
The share of female employees across Sub -Saharan Africa is higher in non- technical departments, such as 
administration (38%), communications (34%), finance (30%) and sales (31%). At the same time, technical STEM and non -STEM departments only have  a 13% and 16% share of female employees respectively , 
according to IFC research .
84 
Women are also more likely to be employed in renewable energy companies that provide solutions in small -
scale, decentralis ed energy systems (37% of full -time employees) compared to large- scale, on -grid, centrali sed 
energy systems (22% of full -time employees).85  
When looking at executive positions, women on average ma ke up 30% of chief executive officers (CEOs), 16 % 
of vice presidents and directors, and 26 % of middle - and lower -level managers  in renewable energy companies 
in Sub -Saharan Africa . Comparing CEOs across sectors, McKinsey found that 6 % of African CEOs were 
women,86 while a UN study found that 3.5% of women from the leading publicly traded companies in G -20 
countries were women.87 The researchers also found that there were more women on the boards of renewable 
energy companies in Africa (27% ) than the G -20 country average (20 %) and the African average (18 %). By 
comparison, women comprised only 6% of the executive -level employees and 21% of board members in energy 
utilities in Sub -Saharan Africa in 2019.88 
 
4.1.2 Employment barriers for women in renewable energy industry  
Barriers to women’s participation in the renewable energy sector exist both at the recruitment stage and at career progression. At the same time, there is a notable difference in the perception of these barriers by men 
and women  too. According to a study conducted  among managers and employees of renewable energy 
companies in Sub -Saharan Africa, only 28% of men - compared to 40% of women  - believed that women faced 
more barriers than men when seeking to enter the sector. The gap increased further to 30% when  considering  
the perceived barriers to advancing women’s careers in the sector, with 45% of interviewed women and only

careers in renewable energy compared to men. In contrast, more men than women identified lack o f skills, 
training opportunities and women’s self -perception as the main reasons for women being less likely to receive

The barriers vary from country to country and each renewable energy subsector. For example , women’s 
participation in the wind energy sector is just 21% globally and even less in Africa at 8%.91 Despite this and the 
fact that the majority of jobs in the sector are of a technical nature (construction and manufacturing), a n IRENA 
survey of the sector involving  72 countries found that skills were not a barrier for women’s entry and retention 
in the wind industry; instead it was  social norms and perceptions, followed by a lack of suitable corporate 
policies, including work -life balance, care solutions, etc.  that were the barriers.92  
A USAID -funded study provides another example,  having in 2020 identif ied significant gaps in female 
representation in solar home system (SHS) market research, production and most field positions (i.e. ,

25 Gender Assessment  technicians who provide installation and after -sales support).93 And a 2021 study  of training institutions in 
Nigeria found evidence that the gender gap started before women and men join ed the workforce, referencing 
the lack of gender diversity among students.94 
Based on the literature review, the following have been  identified as the  key barriers for women’s employment 
and progression  in general , including in Sub-Saharan Africa’s  renewable energy sector : 
• Workplace policies and practices , including : 
– The absence of effective recruitment and promotion policies with specific gender diversity targets, 
which would exclude gender biases ;  
– The lack of access to field work, which impedes women’s career advancement by limiting their 
opportunities to  increase their understanding of renewable energy value chain and technical 
matters ; 
– Gender pay gap; 
– Sexual harassment and violence in the workplace;  and  
– The lack of access to mentoring and training opportunities . 
• Social norms and gender stereotypes , which affect girls and boys from an early age, often 
impeding their opportunities for future employment in any sector, not just  renewable energy. Such norms 
and stereotypes relate to traditional gender roles in society and at the household level and are linked to 
low school and university enrolment rates, the widespread misconception of women not being suited for 
technical roles, the unfavourable treatment of female employment , and the increased burden of unpaid 
household duties and responsibilities.  
• Legal barriers , which relate to restrictions of women ’s involvement in the work force. Such restrictions 
exist through general limitations such as the right of a husband to make decisions on his wife ’s 
employment, which is afforded to men in Cameroon, or indirectly through lack of prohibition of gender -
based discrimination in employment, as is the case of Nigeria. Labour legislation in a selection of 
countries in Africa, including REPP 2 target countries, such as Cameroon, DRC, Madagascar, Nigeria, 
Sierra Leone and Lesotho, also contains more specific restrictions on women ’s employment in dangerous 
and industrial jobs, which constitute core business functions in the renewable energy sector. It is argued that denial of access for women to opportunities in these jobs impedes their ability to reach leadership positions.  
 
4.2 Women as energy users  
Interconnections between the empowerment of women and the energy sector exist not only in employment 
opportunities afforded to women in the sector, but also in electricity access and its impact on the everyday life of women.  
It is widely recogni sed that where physical energy access is translated into actual use of energy services, it can 
help reduce women’s poverty by: (i) saving time (by substituting for manual labour) and enhancing convenience and comfort; (ii) reducing the use of polluting ener gy forms such as wood fuel  and kerosene; (iii) creating 
opportunities for new income generation options  and for enhancing existing ones; (iv) building social capital in 
education and health, a precondition for women’s empowerment; and (v) providing access to information and 
entertainment.  
 
93 Deloitte, 2020. Nigeria Power Sector Program. Gender Mainstreaming in the Solar Home System Value Chain . 
94 Deloitte, 2021. Power Africa. Nigeria Power Sector Program. Social Inclusion and Diversity in Nigeria’s Power Sector.

26 Gender Assessment  However, in Africa access to electricity is limited due to the absence of sufficient energy infrastructure, and 
even where such infrastructure is physically available the stability of the grid is often vulnerable. Furthermore,  
marginalised groups, including women and poor  people , are often hindered in their use of modern clean energy 
by lack of finance, appliances, information, and training or education.95  
According to a World Bank report, Sub- Saharan Africa remained the world’s least electrified region in 2020 and 
accounted for 77% of the global population lacking access to electricity. More than one  third of REPP 2 target 
countries are among the top 20 countries with the largest deficit in energy access: Nigeria (92 million people), 
DRC (72 million), Niger (20 million) and Madagascar (18 million), with Nigeria and DRC being world leaders in 
the total number of unserved people.  
While availability of country -level gender -disaggregated data on electricity access in Sub -Saharan Africa is 
limited and it is difficult to assess the extent to which lack of electricity access can be attributed to women in male-headed households ,
 studies have nonetheless s hown that women -owned households and enterprises find 
it harder to access modern energy. 96  The reasons for existing disparities in access can be attributed to the 
traditional distribution of gender roles , as well as differences in access to finance. Funding schemes for  
electricity  connections that take gender into account have been known to significantly increase overall 
connection rates.97 
Country -level studies have been performed for two REPP 2 target countries  – Nige and Zambia  - yielding  
different results for each. In Niger, female -headed households were less likely to have electricity access, either 
through grid -connection or off -grid solutions, as presented in Table 21. In Zambia, female -headed households 
were more likely to have electricity access through grid connection in both rural and urban areas, while male -
headed households were more likely to utilize off -grid solutions. At the sam e time, more female -headed 
households (93.1%) did not have electricity access at all in Zambian rural areas  compared to male -headed 
households (86.7% ) but were slightly more likely to have electricity access in urban areas than male -headed 
households, as shown in Table 23 below.

27 Gender Assessment  The primary  reasons for the lack of electricity connectivity also differed between the countries. In Niger, for 
both female - and male -headed households, the main reason for not being connected to the grid was that the 
grid was too far away or not available (82.4% and 87.4%, respectively), as presented in Table 2 4. This is 
equally an issue for female - and male -headed households in rural areas. However, it is more likely that female -
headed households will be unable to pay for the connection cost compared with their male  counterparts (8.8% 
versus 6.2%). This is especially true in urban areas, where 31.7% of female -headed households reported the 
connection cost as the main issue, compared with 23.6% of male -headed households.100 
In Zambia, both male -headed households and female -headed households identified “grid too far or unavailable” 
as the main reason they were not connected to the grid.  At the same time, as presented in Table 26 below, 
financial burden of interconnection cost was a bigger barrier for female -headed households than for male -
headed  households.

Regarding  cooking technologies, higher disparities were present in Niger, where female -headed households 
were slightly more likely to use three -stone stoves and less likely to use traditional stoves103 compared to male -
headed households, while there was no gender gap in the use of LPG stoves. In Zambia , male-headed 
households and female -headed households use d similar cooking technologies.  
At the same time, in both countries women aged 15 and older spent considerably longer  cooking or in the 
cooking area (more than two hours per day in Niger  and on average 10.6 hours per week in Zambia) than 
either men, girls, or boys. Women are thus much more likely to be affected by indoor air pollution.  
The above statistics supports aggregated global and regional findings that limited electricity access and the 
reliance on solid fuels for cooking and other household energy -demanding chores – as well as informal 
economic sector activities – has a higher toll on women, contributing to their “time poverty” and health 
issues.104

28 Gender Assessment  Increasing both genders ’ access to modern energy services has the capacity to (i) help women save time by 
substituting manual labour and reducing workloads, (ii) reduce indoor air pollution and enable other health 
benefits for women and children, (iii) improve education by enabling children to study and women to have 
greater flexibility in the organi sation of their work patterns, (iv) boost income generation, and (v) offer social 
benefits such as increased funding of households’ well -being and the education of children, including girls , as a 
result of the increased income of female members of the household, who reinvest most of their income in their 
families .105 
4.3 Limited funding to women -owned/led 
companies  
Africa has a higher share of female -owned businesses than other regions, yet women entrepreneurs still face 
multiple challenges access ing the  finance and support they need to grow their businesses.106 There is an estimated 
USD 42 billion financing gap for African women entrepreneurs across business value chains.107  In 2021, less than 
1% of funding raised by starts -ups in Africa went to female -only teams, and only 18% to teams that received 
funding were considered gender -diverse.108  
Based on Global Banking Alliance’s review of various government and private sector financing models, there are 
only a few examples of programmes geared specifically to increase access for women owned micro, small and 
medium enterprises . In 2017, only  16-20% of women  in West, Central, East and Southern Africa had access to 
long-term financing from financial institutions.109  
This is further confirmed by research undertaken by the Investment Manager, which suggests that currently there 
are no dedicated funding facilities that would directly target : 1) African women entrepreneurs in the clean energy 
space or 2) businesses that specifically address women’s needs through their business model design.

29 Gender Assessment  5 Gender Action P lan 
 
REPP 2’s investment mandate extends towards some of the most economically and socially vulnerable countries 
in Africa, with the majority (>50%) of the population living below the International Poverty Line in more than half 
of the target countries . The REPP 2 G ender Action Plan (GAP) summarises the actions that need to be implemented  
at the  facility level to ensure gender equality considerations are integrated at the investment level.  
As discussed above, gender gap s exist across key aspects of society  – from economic s and politics to health, 
education and involvement in the energy sector . Women in Sub -Saharan Africa find it hard to access modern 
energy, yet they are often burdened with domestic chores that prevent them from pursuing education or income -
generating activities . Access to energy can help individuals, households and business to complete certain time -
consuming tasks in more efficient manner . Access to electricity may free up time from fuel collection and 
household tasks for longer periods of study time or other productive use of time, decreas ing gender gaps in key 
educational and economic areas.  Also access to energy, through access to lights, information, mobile phones, are 
linked to increase perceived feeling  of safety and security.110  
Furthermore, t here is limited funding available to female -owned or female -led businesses in the region’s  
renewable energy sector . REPP 2 is taking direct action to increase the number of renewable energy companies 
that are aligned with 2X Challenge’s Gender Lens Investing (2X GLI) criteria  by allocating a portion  (minim 30%)  
of the Programme’s funding to these  companies . And since it is imperative to  designing and deliver products and 
services that better serve women’s  needs , REPP 2 also works  with its investees to integrate gender equality 
perspectives into the design, implementation, monitoring and assessment of all of their  renewable energy 
initiatives . 
We believe that funding women, empower s them to be invested and build companies, teams, products and 
markets they see transformative for the generations to come . This is a meaningful step to overcome the gender 
equality gap.  
 
5.1 Actions  
Two main actions have been identified to bridge the gender equality gap:  
 Action 1: Integrate a gender equality perspective into the design, implementation, monitoring and 
assessment of all renewable energy activities financed by REPP 2. This means requesting all investees 
to put in place sub -project level GAPs (Activity 9: Non -financial support to promote gender equity) with actionable 
targets to improve gender equality within their operations and project implementation will positively impact the 
industry and its beneficiaries.  This  involves:  
1. Analysing gender equality in the investee company, country of operation, and targeted sector ;  
2. Establishing an investee -specific GAP by identifying gender performance indicators and gender -
disaggregated targets against an established baseline; and  
3. Integrating the outcomes of (1) and (2) into a monitoring plan.  
 
REPP 2 will provide investee companies with supporting in assessing gender baseline and establishing 
action plan. Such support includes:

The REPP 2 investees will form gender action plans at two levels:  
• Corporate  in which the review and action plan focuses on  leadership commitment , support for gender 
equality and women’s  and vulnerable individuals ’ empowerment  and ensuring non-discrimination and equal 
opportunity policies and procedures  are in place to ensure equal recruitment, employment, and procurement. 
Guidance for establishment of the GAP at corporate level will be provided in a REPP 2  gender toolkit.  
• Project  in which the review will focus on gender -specific risks at the project level, ensuring stakeholder 
consultations on the project , its objectives  and design  with women, girls, men, and boys in the project 
area, gender advocates, and women’s civil society organisations (appendix 1 provide list of organisations 
to consider for collaboration in REPP 2 target countries), and relevant local and national authorities, as 
well as assessing the root causes, structural barriers and inequalities that put underrepresente d groups at 
disadvantage. The project level gap will focus , for example,  on inclusive stakeholder engagement, 
ensuring the safety and security of female employees and community members during construction 
activities (where relevant) as well as operation, ensuring effective grievance mechanism , marketing/ 
messaging strategy for female customers. Guidance for establishment of the GAP at corporate level will be provided in a REPP 2 gender toolkit. The chapter 10 of the annex 6 ESMF give guidance to inclusive 
stakeholder engagement. The results of this engagement should be used to formulate project level actions 
in the GAP.  
Growth and development funding (activit ies 1 and 2) do not require  the development of  a project level GAP (or 
ESMS) as this funding is only intended to help the project to prove its feasibility. However, growth and 
development funding (activity 1 and 2) can be used to finance the establishment of GAP (and/or ESMS).  See 
below figure .

Action 2: Dedicate a target of 30% of all funding towards companies that meet 2X GLI criteria. These 
are companies that:  
• Are founded by a woman or women or are majority -owned by women; and / or  
• Have at least 30% women in senior management; and / or  
• Provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women; and / or  
• Have a workforce that is at least 30% women, together with a progressive human resource policy.  
The funding can be  in from of early -stage growth funding for off- grid companies (activity 1), development -stage 
funding for grid -connected projects  (activity 2), finance for expansion of SHS businesses (activity 3), finance for 
construction of mini -grid portfolios (activity 4) or gap funding for construction of grid -connected projects  (activity 
5)  and can be in from of debt, equity or hybrid instruments, subject to the compa ny’s funding needs.  
The above  actions are further defined in Table 25 along with indicators that will be monitored and the expected 
targets. For ex -ante target setting the simple assumption has been made that 30 % 2X GLI aligned funding will 
lead to 30 % of female owned and led companies benefiting, 30 % of jobs being dedicated to women and  30 % 
end beneficiaries at household and business leve l to be women  in line with the 2X GLI Criteria. This is anticipated 
to be a conservative assumption as at household level female to male ratio is typically 50 -50.  
 
In line with REPP2 monitoring plan, gender indictors, actions and results are monitored  and reviewed and where 
relevant this action plan or its underlying actions and targets are updated accordingly.

Impact statement:  
REPP 2 will aim to create a paradigm shift by leveraging private sector funding to promote investments in small -scale renewable energy projects in LDCs and other sub -Saharan 
African countries. As result of this, local populations of some of the world's most vulnerable communities , including women and girls,  will have improved access to clean energy, 
improved access to climate resilient solutions and information, and the Host Countries will transition towards low -emission resilient development pathways, meeting their NDC targets. 
Accordingly, with increased investment, the GHG emissions from electricity generation will be reduced, climate resilience of the energy infrastructure is enhanced, and clima te 
resilience of vulnerable people and communities will be improved.  
Outcome statements:  
Outcome statement 3: Improved climate resilience for vulnerable people and communities, through access to clean energy solution which improves resilience and builds gender 
equity.  
Outcome statement 4: Increased private sector investment in renewable energy projects that enables local developers, builds gender equity and drives a market for smaller -scale 
renewables.  
Outcome statement 5: Contributing to stronger technology deployment, innovation and market transformation  
 
Outputs  statements:  
Output 1: Increased commercial viability  
All REPP 2 activities, including early -stage growth and development funding, gap funding for construction and support for market development combine to demonstrate the potential 
for technical and commercial viability of smaller -scale renewable energy businesses and projects including female owned and led businesses in Host Countries  and across the 
region.    
Output 2: Increased renewable energy capacity and generation  
Early- stage and construction funding from REPP 2 ensures that new renewable energy capacity and generation is realised. Renewable energy capacity and generation financed by REPP 
2 contributes directly to improved access to clean electricity for women and men and host -country priorities as outlined in their policy frameworks as well as their Nationally -Determined 
Contributions and National Adaptation Plans.   
Output 3: Improved skills and capacity  
REPP 2 support for project developers establish a more active and commercially -viable market for smaller -scale renewables, which is better -able to attract both public and private 
finance in future. These impacts accrue to Host Countries and more broadly throughout sub -Saharan Africa, with a demonstration impact for other emerging markets and with developers 
being better placed to expand to those markets. REPP 2 will directly contribute to improved gender equity through a focus on gender mainstreaming within  investee projects and 
businesses.

35 Gender Assessment  Appendix 1 
Table 26. List of organisations working locally with issues regarding GBV and SAH.  
Following is a list of organis ations which are in in different ways working with issues regarding GBV and gender equality. The list includes international organi sations as well as businesses that 
only work on a regional level. The primary objective of each organi sation is to teach and inform about inequality, as well as give information on how to approach the subject. The organi sations  
are suggested to be contacted by investee companies to assist with project level gender baseline setting, stakeholder engagement as well as establishing gender sensitive grievance 
mechanism. This list is not exclusive, and investee may consider partner ing with a different organisation working on gender equality issues in the project community.

Women’s Employment 
Women have anunder‐recognized role asenergy
consumers, producers andasenergy entrepreneurs .
Thisprovides agood foundation fortheir engagement
inmini-grid operations .The business case for
women’s employment canbemade both attheHQ
andthecommunity level:
•Performance :Firms with greater diversity and
presence offemale leaders have exhibited stronger
performance andreturn oninvestments .
•Innovation :Companies withalarger footprint of
female employees arelikely tobemore innovative
(and bring those ideas tomarket) .Women score
aswellasmeninabilities liked toinnovation (e.g.
motivating others andinspiring their team) .
•Improved Service Delivery :Workforce diversity
roots acompany initscustomer base and can
improve service quality .
•Safer Operating Environment :Diverse teams
can enhance the safety culture and ensure
protocols arefollowed .
•Community buy-in:Enhanced community
engagement and employment cancreate greater
social license tooperate .Opportunities to enhance talent 
pool and enhance equality Mlinda’s community mini-grid
deployment in India created
approximately 986 jobs.These jobs
include 180direct full-time jobs, 131full-
time equivalent jobs from contractual
work, and 675 productive -use jobs
through additional entrepreneurial
activities .

Understanding Customers and Productive Uses of Energy
Mini-gridcompanies have aninherent interest inlocal consumers andtheutilisation of
locally -generated power .
Withaccess toenergy services, localentrepreneurs, micro -enterprises, andfarmers can
generate local jobs andincome, andenhance livelihoods .However, given inherent
gender -gaps atthecommunity level, specific attention needs tobepaid toensure
productive useofenergy gains areequitably distributed .
Barriers toequitable distribution ofbenefits include :
•Women have inequitable access toresources, such asland, collateral, andfinance,
indicating aneed fortailored approaches (e.g.mobile money, subsidies, ready
boards) .
•Women areinvolved indifferent enterprises andaccess tolivelihoods given cultural
expectations andnorms .
•Cultural norms, limited participation incommunity andhousehold decision -making .
•Women have fewer opportunities toattend trainings andhave less access to
information given different mobility patterns .
•Unequal labor burden interms ofhousehold andcarework (unpaid) andfuelwood
collection which leads totimepoverty
Adeeper understanding ofthewomen’s segment canhelp acompany increase its
customer base andmarket size, enhance loyalty andmanage risks (e.g.payment,
community ownership) .


---
*Page 178*
---

Prepare
Engagement 
and data 
collection
Develop 
actions and 
monitor•Staffing and budgeting for understanding customers 
•Who to engage (e.g. female famers, business owners, male partners, 
service providers, local officials, and partners)•How to engage (e.g. FGD, interviews, community mapping, and 
roundtable (including tools))
•What to engage on (e.g. energy needs and value chains, willingness to 
pay, access to finance etc.)•When to engage (e.g. times, location, partner consent, season etc.)
•Finalize specific research approach, methods, and tools to undertake 
data collection•Test and finalize data collection tools (questionnaires, surveys) to 
collect data
•Areas you may consider collecting data on include:
-Women’s role in key sectors and value -chains (e.g. agribusiness) 
and in the households -Women’s energy needs to do current tasks and reduce 
labor/costs and meet energy use aspirations-Investigate affordability constraints and ability to pay (e.g. 
seasonal income with crop harvests etc.)-Access to finance (for connection, services and appliances 
adoption)
-Access to information and business development opportunities
-Identify key stakeholders that women trust or that could be 
engaged to form partnerships
•Identification of key gender gaps with regards to demand and supply 
gives company detailed description of possible issues to address•Verify findings at the company level through workshops/discussions•Commit to company action, set targets, allocate budget, and monitor 
progress

Risk Management: Gender -based Violence 
Gender -based violence (GBV) isanumbrella
term foranyharmful actperpetrated against a
person based onsocially ascribed (i.e.gender)
differences between males and females .It
includes acts that inflict physical, sexual, or
mental harm orsuffering, threats ofsuch acts,
coercion, andother deprivations ofliberty .These
acts canoccur inpublic orinprivate spaces .
Common forms ofGBVinclude sexual violence
(rape, attempted rape, unwanted touching,
sexual exploitation, and sexual harassment),
intimate partner violence (also called
domestic violence, including physical, emotional,
sexual, andeconomic abuse), forced andearly
marriage, and female genital
mutilation .(IASC 2015).
Violence against women isamanifestation of
historically unequal power relations between
men andwomen, which have ledtodomination
over, anddiscrimination against, women bymen
andtotheprevention ofthefulladvancement of
women (UNDeclaration ontheElimination of
Violence Against Women 1993).Workplace Sexual 
Harassment Sexual 
Exploitation and 
Abuse (SEA)
•Unwanted sexual 
advances
•Request for 
sexual favors
•Sexual physical 
contact•Exploitation of a 
vulnerable 
position, 
differential 
power or trust 
for sexual 
purpose 
•Actual or 
threatened 
sexual physical 
intrusion 
Company and 
Project -Site RiskSource: WBG Good Practice Note -Environmental & Social Framework 
for IPF Operations: Addressing Sexual Exploitation and Abuse and 
Sexual Harassment (SEA/SH) in Investment  Project Financing 
Involving Major Civil Works

Risk Management: Gender -based Violence 
Project Design AssessProject 
ImplementationAddressProject
ImplementationRespond
•Social risk assessment of 
community -level risks 
(child marriage, gender 
norms, girls drop -out rate)
•Assess labor influx and 
duration of project 
timelines
•Assessment of services for 
survivors (e.g. clinics, 
NGO’s, national hotlines)
•Review internal company 
capacity to respond to risks
•Use risk assessment tool 
and leverage safeguard 
instruments 
•Reflect on and update risk 
assessments during project 
implementation•Establish mitigation, 
reporting, and monitoring 
measures
•Based on risks identified, 
identify the corresponding 
mitigation, including 
adopting GBV policies and 
CoC, and have staff sign 
these (including 
contractors), reporting 
procedures, trainings,  
engage community, and 
raising awareness
•Monitor the effectiveness of 
the mitigation measures 
and adapt/make changes as 
appropriate•Provide essential services 
for survivors
•Report case through the 
reporting mechanism 
(survivor -centered)
•Cases brought forward 
documented and closed 
(focus on intake process)
•Check -in with community 
(Hire an expert)
